Scalable Machine Learning Algorithms using Path Signatures
Csaba Tóth

Abstract

In this thesis, we consider the integration of path signatures–a mathematical object rooted in rough path theory–with scalable machine learning algorithms to address challenges in sequential and structured data modelling. The key topics considered include:

•

Path Signatures
: Introduced as a hierarchical and theoretically robust feature representation for sequential data, path signatures faithfully capture dynamics while offering properties like invariance to reparameterization and tree-like equivalence. Challenges like computational overheads are addressed through novel algorithms throughout the thesis.

•

Gaussian Processes
: We demonstrate embedding signature kernels into Gaussian process models, offering an expressive probabilistic modelling approach for sequential data while tackling computational barriers via sparse variational inference techniques. This approach enhances performance on probabilistic time series classification tasks.

•

Seq2Tens
Framework
: Combines signature features with deep learning, using iterations of low-rank layers to mitigate computational costs while retaining expressiveness. Applications include time series classification, mortality prediction, generative modelling.

•

Graph Representation
: Extends path signatures to graph data and connects them to hypo-elliptic diffusions, combined with low-rank techniques offering scalable architectures for capturing global and local graph structures, and outperforming conventional graph neural networks on tasks requiring long-range reasoning.

•

Random Fourier Signature Features
: Introduces scalable random feature-based approximations for signature kernels with supporting theoretical results, overcoming computational limitations for large datasets while retaining state-of-the-art performance.

•

Recurrent Sparse Spectrum Signature Gaussian Processes
: Combines Random Fourier Signature Features with Gaussian Processes and a forgetting mechanism for adaptive context focus in time series forecasting, bridging short- and long-term dependencies.

The topics are divided into separate, self-contained chapters that can be read independently.

{romanpages}

Introduction

Unifying theme

The unifying theme of this thesis centers on the integration of path signatures, a mathematical framework rooted in rough path theory, with scalable machine learning algorithms to address challenges in sequential and structured data modelling. Sequential data, such as time series, DNA sequences, or video streams, represent a cornerstone of modern data analysis due to its prevalence across various fields, including finance, healthcare, and natural language processing. The core challenge lies in extracting meaningful representations from such data while preserving its intrinsic sequential structure and ensuring computational efficiency.

A prominent approach for exploiting sequential structure is through handcrafted feature engineering techniques, which find representations of the data so that well-established models become applicable. The dataset is then typically represented in a finite-dimensional Euclidean space. This approach is problematic in the sense that the feature extraction method often has to be tailored to the problem at hand, and some loss of information is inevitably incurred. In recent years, the signature method originating from rough path theory has become a popular method for extracting features of streamed data, that describe sequences and paths using a hierarchy of tensors with increasing degrees. Path signatures provide a robust theoretical foundation for representing sequential data as a hierarchy of tensor features, faithfully capturing the dynamics of the data. On the one hand, this has been remarkably successful due to the powerful mathematical machinery that underlies signatures allowing to provide strong theoretical guarantees. Their mathematical elegance offers unique benefits, such as invariance to reparameterization and universality for approximating continuous functions. On the other hand, this representation invokes a considerable computational burden due to the combinatorially increasing dimensionality of tensors of increasing degrees, as such their practical application in machine learning has been limited by significant computational challenges, e.g. the polynomial growth in feature space dimension leading to bottlenecks in handling high-dimensional data.

This thesis takes a multifaceted approach to address these challenges by developing novel algorithms that retain the theoretical strengths of path signatures while significantly improving their computational scalability. Central to this work is the integration of path signatures with popular machine learning methodologies, including Gaussian processes, deep learning approaches, and kernel methods. Additionally, the thesis extends the application of path signatures beyond sequential data to the domain of graphs, showcasing their versatility and adaptability. Overall, this approach proved to be a fruitful one often leading to outperforming state-of-the-art methods in their genre on tasks such as time series classification, time series forecasting, mortality prediction in healthcare, generative modelling, and graph-based learning. This unifying theme underscores the thesis’s contribution to bridging the gap between the theoretical elegance of path signatures and their practical deployment in real-world machine learning applications.

Outline

The thesis is organized into six chapters that serve as standalone blocks readable independently, each complete with their own set of introductions, conclusions, and appendices.

Chapter
1

This introductory chapter provides an accessible introduction to path signatures, a mathematical object rooted in rough path theory, that serves as a powerful feature representation for sequential data. This chapter lays the foundation for understanding the algebraic, analytic, and computational properties of path signatures that make them suitable for machine learning applications.
The chapter begins by defining tensor products and constructing tensor algebras, which form the hierarchical feature space where path signatures reside. Tensor algebras, with their associative and noncommutative structure, allow for the representation of data as sequences of tensors, while the dual space and operations like the shuffle product further enhance their algebraic utility. Path signatures are then presented as sequences of iterated integrals capturing the geometry of paths. For bounded variation paths, these signatures encode the interactions of path coordinates, with practical computation simplified using tools like Chen’s identity. Although the signature admits extensions to the unbounded variation setting, our applications lie in the discrete domain, and we defer to
[
182
]
for a review of rough path theory, one of the main themes of which is exactly that how highly oscillatory driving signals affect systems of differential equations, and how the signature can be extended to this setting.

The chapter emphasizes the key properties of path signatures: their universality as an algebra of functions that approximate continuous mappings, reparameterization invariance for robustness to changes in time sampling, and the ability to algebraically manipulate path concatenation and reversal.
Motivating examples illustrate how path signatures generalize polynomial feature maps for sequential data, providing a versatile and robust framework for sequence modelling. Challenges like the combinatorial growth of tensor dimensions are acknowledged, which is addressed via various scalable solutions later in the thesis, such as low-rank approximations and random projections. By establishing the theoretical and practical foundations of path signatures, Chapter 1 sets the stage for their integration into machine learning models.

Chapter
2

Gaussian Processes (
GP
s) are a cornerstone of Bayesian machine learning, providing a flexible, non-parametric framework for modelling uncertainty in data. However, their application to sequential data, such as time series or trajectories, is often limited by the difficulty of constructing covariance functions that adequately capture temporal dependencies and patterns. Path signatures offer a powerful solution to this challenge. They provide a hierarchical and structured encoding of sequence information through iterated integrals, making them an ideal tool for defining expressive covariance functions in
GP
s. This work introduces a novel approach to embedding signature kernels within the
GP
framework. Thus, we are concerned with learning from sequential data in a Bayesian manner by constructing Gaussian processes with the inner product of signatures as covariance functions, continuing on the premise of
[
146
]
, where a kernel trick was introduced for the inner product of signature features.

The chapter demonstrates the potential of integrating path signature features into
GP
s, offering a mathematically grounded and practical approach to Bayesian modelling of sequential data. By constructing covariance functions based on the inner product of signatures, the method captures the temporal and structural dependencies inherent in sequential data. The introduction of sparse variational inference further addresses the computational challenges, ensuring scalability even for large datasets of time series data. Empirical evaluations highlight the effectiveness of the approach, showcasing superior performance on time series classification tasks compared to conventional sequence kernels and comparable performance to state-of-the-art frequentist methods. The results underscore the versatility of signature-based
GP
s, providing a robust framework for combining theoretical rigor with practical applicability in Bayesian sequential data modelling. This integration not only enriches the representational capacity of
GP
s, but also opens up new venues for applying Bayesian methods to sequential data problems.

Chapter
3

Deep learning and Gaussian processes have emerged as powerful tools for modelling sequential data, yet both face limitations when processing complex, high-volume or high-dimensional streams. Signature features provide an effective representation of sequences but can be computationally expensive when incorporated into parametric models like neural networks as in
[
140
]
. We introduce
Seq2Tens
, a novel framework that bridges this gap by leveraging the expressive power of signature-based features while introducing low-rank linear functionals to mitigate computational challenges. This approach allows the representation of sequential data through layers of low-rank transformations, offering a computationally efficient mechanism to extract global sequence information. By stacking multiple such layers,
Seq2Tens
achieves deep learning-style expressiveness while preserving interpretability and scalability. This chapter presents both the theoretical foundation of
Seq2Tens
and its practical utility, extending the versatility of signature-based features into new domains of deep learning.

The chapter establishes
Seq2Tens
as an advancement in the scalable deployment of signature-based features for sequential data modelling. The framework introduces low-rank constraints on linear functionals of signatures, enabling efficient computations directly in the dual space. This idea is further enhanced by the concept of deep layers, which stack multiple low-rank transformations to recover lost expressiveness due to the low-rank constraint in the style of deep learning. Experimental results validate the effectiveness of
Seq2Tens
across a range of tasks, including time series classification, mortality prediction, and generative modelling, where it consistently outperforms state-of-the-art neural network baselines. The experiments demonstrate how
Seq2Tens
not only improves computational efficiency but also preserves the expressiveness of signature features, making it a practical and robust solution for modern sequential data challenges. This work paves the way for broader adoption of signature-based methods in deep learning while addressing scalability concerns.

Chapter
4

Graphs provide a natural representation for structured data, appearing in diverse fields such as social networks, molecular chemistry, and computer vision. A major challenge in graph learning is to design representations that capture not only local neighborhood structures but also long-range dependencies and global characteristics. Building upon the foundational ideas of path signatures and their algebraic properties, The chapter extends their application to graph domains by leveraging hypo-elliptic diffusion processes. This novel approach introduces the hypo-elliptic graph Laplacian, a tensor-valued operator that encodes graph substructures through the evolution of random walks and diffusion dynamics. By integrating this operator into neural network architectures, the chapter proposes a scalable framework for capturing complex interactions in graph-structured data. This innovation bridges the gap between local aggregation and long-range structural encoding in graph neural networks, offering a theoretically grounded and computationally efficient solution for graph learning tasks.

This work can be treated as a follow-up to Chapter
3
, where we generalized the signature method using algebraic techniques, and devised scalable neural network architectures for streamed data. We present a significant extension of signature-based methods from sequential data to graph domains, addressing a critical gap in the ability to model long-range interactions and hierarchical structures in graphs by classical graph neural networks. The introduction of the hypo-elliptic graph Laplacian provides a mathematically robust mechanism to encode diffusion dynamics and random walk distributions, enabling the representation of rich graph features. The chapter further adapts low-rank algorithms from the previous chapter to design scalable neural architectures for graph learning, ensuring computational efficiency without sacrificing expressiveness. Empirical evaluations demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on challenging graph tasks compared to classic graph neural networks, such as node classification and graph-level predictions, where traditional methods struggle to capture long-range dependencies. By combining algebraic insights with practical implementation, this work lays the groundwork for future exploration of graph-specific adaptations of signature-based methodologies.

Chapter
5

This chapter follows the premise of increased interest in the signature kernel. Signature kernels have emerged as a powerful tool for modelling sequential data, offering theoretical guarantees such as universality and invariance to reparameterization. Further, the benefits of kernelization allow us to compose the signature map with a static kernel enhancing expressiveness and allowing us to circumvent the curse of dimensionality incurred by high-degree tensors. However, their practical application faces significant computational barriers, particularly the quadratic complexity in sequence length and sample size, which limits scalability. We address this limitation by introducing Random Fourier Signature Features, a novel approximation method that combines the algebraic richness of path signatures with the efficiency of random feature maps. By constructing low-dimensional embeddings that approximate the signature kernel, this approach dramatically reduces the computational overhead while preserving key properties of the kernel with high probability. By refining this method with dimensionality reduction techniques, we end up with even more scalable variants capable of handling millions of sequences. This innovation extends the applicability of signature kernels to large-scale machine learning tasks, offering a practical solution for computationally intensive domains.

Thus, we make a contribution to signature kernels by overcoming their scalability challenges, introducing a random feature-based approximation that is both efficient and theoretically robust. The Random Fourier Signature Features approach leverages probabilistic concentration arguments to ensure accurate kernel approximations with high probability. Experimental evaluations demonstrate that this approach achieves performance on par with full-rank signature kernels on moderate datasets while significantly outperforming other random feature methods on large-scale datasets. This chapter not only addresses the computational bottlenecks of signature kernels but also establishes Random Fourier Signature Features as a scalable and versatile tool for modern machine learning, enabling the practical application of signature methods in domains requiring high-throughput data processing.

While working on this project, a general
Scikit-Learn
compatible Python package has also been produced for all kinds of signature kernel computations on
GPU
called
KSig
available at
https://github.com/tgcsaba/ksig
, and a preprint that serves as a user guide and documentation to it
[
264
]
. This paper is not included as part of this thesis due to space limitations.

Chapter
6

Time series forecasting is a crucial task in fields ranging from finance and energy to healthcare and logistics, requiring models that can capture both short-term dynamics and long-term dependencies. Traditional Gaussian processes (
GP
s) provide a powerful probabilistic framework but struggle with scalability and context adaptability for long time series data. We introduce Recurrent Sparse Spectrum Signature Gaussian Processes (
RS
3
GP
), a novel approach that combines the efficiency of low-rank signature features with variational inference in Gaussian processes.
RS
3
GP
incorporates a principled forgetting mechanism using a novel decay parameterization, adjusting the context length to focus on more recent time steps. This innovation enables
RS
3
GP
to overcome the challenges of traditional
GP
s and signature-based models, offering a scalable and interpretable solution for time series forecasting across diverse problems.

We present
RS
3
GP
as a novel approach that bridges the gap between theoretical rigour and practical applicability in time series forecasting. By leveraging the expressive power of Random Fourier Signature Features and introducing a decay-based forgetting mechanism,
RS
3
GP
dynamically adjusts its focus on relevant data segments, making it particularly effective in scenarios with both short-term and long-term dependencies. The model is computationally efficient, processing long sequences in real time through efficient parallelized computations while maintaining a scalable memory footprint. Experimental evaluations highlight its superior performance over traditional
GP
s and competitive results against deep learning models on synthetic and real-world datasets. This contribution establishes
RS
3
GP
as a competitive approach with the state-of-the-art for scalable, probabilistic time series forecasting. Overall, the proposed methodology opens new directions in advancing scalable and interpretable models in time series forecasting.

Chapter 1
Path Signatures

1.1
Tensors, tensor algebras, and their dual

First, we construct the space that path signatures live in called the tensor algebra.

1.1.1
Tensor products

Here we provide a brief overview of tensor products of vector spaces, which we will use to construct our feature space called the tensor algebra .

Preliminary example

We start with some preliminary examples about tensors.

Example 1.1

(Tensors on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
)
.

If
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{e})\in\mathbb{R}^{e}
are two vectors, then their tensor product
\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}
is defined as the
(d\times e)
-matrix, or degree-
2
tensor, with entries
(\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y})_{i,j}=x_{i}y_{j}
. This is also commonly called the outer product of the two vectors. The space
\mathbb{R}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{e}
is defined as the linear span of all degree-
2
tensors
\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{e}
.
If
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{f}
is another vector, then one may form a degree-
3
tensor
\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}\otimes\mathbf{z}
with shape
(d\times e\times f)
defined to have entries
(\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}\otimes\mathbf{z})_{i,j,k}=x_{i}y_{j}z_{k}
. The space
\mathbb{R}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{e}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{f}
is analogously defined as the linear span of all degree-
3
tensors
\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}\otimes\mathbf{z}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{e},\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{f}
, and so forth for higher orders.

Abstract tensor product

The abstract definition of the tensor product, which holds generally for infinite-dimensional vector spaces is given below.

Definition 1.2

(Abstract tensor product)
.

If
U
and
V
are (not necessarily finite-dimensional) vector spaces, their tensor product
U\otimes V
is a vector space together with a bilinear map
\otimes:U\times V\to U\otimes W
, such that any bilinear map
B:U\times V\to W
factors through
U\otimes V
, i.e. there exists a unique linear map
\tilde{B}:U\otimes V\to W
, which satisfies
B=\tilde{B}\circ\otimes
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{U}
and
\mathcal{B}_{V}
respectively be bases of
U
and
V
. Then,
U\times V
is unique up to isomorphism, and its basis is formed by the tensor product of the bases, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{U\otimes V}=\left\{\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v}\in U\otimes V\,:\,\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{B}_{U},\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{B}_{V}\right\}
.

Example 1.3
.

Similarly to the example about
\mathbb{R}^{d}
above, a coordinate-wise construction of the tensor product is given by identifying
U\otimes V
with the set of functions from
\mathcal{B}_{U}\times\mathcal{B}_{V}
to
\mathbb{R}
that are nonzero only on finitely many elements, which is a vector space by pointwise operations on functions. Specifically, for
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{B}_{U}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{B}_{V}
, we may identify
\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v}
with the function that takes
1
on
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
and
0
on every other element of
\mathcal{B}_{U}\times\mathcal{B}_{V}
. Then, we can extend this to
U\otimes V
by asserting the bilinearity of
\otimes
, so that if we denote
\mathbf{x}=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{B}_{U}}\alpha_{\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{y}=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{B}_{V}}\beta_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{v}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{B}_{U}}\alpha_{\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{u}\right)\otimes\left(\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{B}_{V}}\beta_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{v}\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{B}_{U}}\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{B}_{V}}\alpha_{\mathbf{u}}\beta_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v},

(1.1)

where finitely many coefficients are nonzero in
\left\{\alpha_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{B}_{U}\right\}
and
\left\{\beta_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{B}_{V}\right\}
.

We refer the reader to
[
223
,
299
,
155
]
for further details about abstract tensors.

Tensor products of Hilbert spaces

It is often that one comes across the definition of the tensor product formulated in the category of vector spaces as an abstract vector space such that any bilinear map factors through it as discussed in the previous paragraph. This can then be endowed with an inner product and completed to get a tensor product in the category of Hilbert spaces. However, there also exist explicit models of tensor products of Hilbert spaces. Hence, we introduce a concrete model of the Hilbert space tensor product originally proposed by
[
194
]
.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{H}_{m}
be Hilbert spaces. To each element
(h_{1},\dots,h_{m})\in\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\cdots\mathcal{H}_{m}
, associate the multi-linear operator
h_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{m}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{H}_{m}\to\mathbb{R}
defined for each
(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})\in\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{H}_{m}
by

\displaystyle(h_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{m})(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})=\left\langle h_{1},f_{1}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\cdots\left\langle h_{m},f_{m}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{m}}.

(1.2)

Take the linear span of all such multi-linear operators to build the space

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes^{\prime}\cdots\otimes^{\prime}\mathcal{H}_{m}=\Span\left\{h_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{m}\,:\,h_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}\right\},

(1.3)

and endow
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes^{\prime}\cdots\otimes^{\prime}\mathcal{H}_{m}
with an inner product via

\displaystyle\left\langle h_{1}\otimes\cdots h_{m},f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{m}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes^{\prime}\cdots\otimes^{\prime}\mathcal{H}_{m}}=\left\langle h_{1},f_{1}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\cdots\left\langle h_{m},f_{m}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{m}}

(1.4)

for all
h_{1},f_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1},\dots,h_{m},f_{m}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
, and extend by linearity to
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes^{\prime}\cdots\otimes^{\prime}\mathcal{H}_{m}
. Taking the topological completion of this space under this inner product gives a Hilbert space denoted by
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{m}
called the tensor product of the Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{H}_{m}
.

1.1.2
Tensor algebras

Now that we have discussed how to take tensor products of vector (Hilbert) spaces, we show how to embed a linear space
V
into a much bigger linear space
\mathsf{T}((V))
, which is also an associative algebra
1
1

1

An algebra
A
is a vector space
A
, where one can multiply elements together, i.e.
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\in A
for
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in A
.
using a so-called free construction.
Since the tensor product is associative, we can unambiguously take tensor powers of the vector space
V
, and we denote
V^{\otimes m}=V\otimes\cdots\otimes V
. Note that if
\mathcal{B}_{V}
is a basis of
V
, then the basis of
V^{\otimes m}
is given by all elements of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}\quad\text{for}\quad\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{m}\in\mathcal{B}_{V}.

(1.5)

Definition 1.5

(Extended tensor algebra)
.

We define the extended tensor algebra as formal series of tensors over a vector space
V
indexed by their degree
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}((V))=\prod_{m\geq 0}V^{\otimes m}=\left\{(t_{0},\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2},\dots)\,:\,\mathbf{t}_{m}\in V^{\otimes m}\text{ for }m\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

(1.6)

where by convention
V^{\otimes 0}=\mathbb{R}
is a scalar.

\mathsf{T}((V))
is again a vector space with vector addition and scalar multiplication defined coordinate-wise as

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{t}=\left(\mathbf{s}_{m}+\mathbf{t}_{m}\right)_{m\geq 0},\quad\lambda\mathbf{s}=\left(\lambda\mathbf{s}_{m}\right)_{m\geq 0}.

(1.7)

V
is a linear subspace of
\mathsf{T}((V))
by identifying
\mathbf{v}\in V
as
(0,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\dots)\in\mathsf{T}((V))
.
Further,
\mathsf{T}((V))
is also an associative unital algebra with unit
\mathbf{1}=(1,\mathbf{0},\dots)\in\mathsf{T}((V))
. It is endowed with a (noncommutative
2
2

2

Noncommutative means that
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\neq\mathbf{b}\mathbf{a}
in general for elements
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in V
of the algebra.
when
\dim V>1
) product defined for
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathsf{T}((V))

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{t}=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\mathbf{s}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{t}_{m-i}\right)_{m\geq 0}\in\mathsf{T}((V)).

(1.8)

An element
\mathbf{t}=(t_{0},\mathbf{t}_{1},\dots)\in\mathsf{T}((V))
is invertible if and only if
t_{0}\neq 0
, with inverse

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{-1}=\frac{1}{t_{0}}\sum_{n\geq 0}\left(\mathbf{1}-\frac{\mathbf{t}}{t_{0}}\right)^{n}

(1.9)

Definition 1.6

(Tensor algebra)
.

Another commonly encountered formulation of the tensor algebra consists of sequences of tensors with only finitely many non-zero coefficients, which we refer to simply as the tensor algebra. It is defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(V)=\bigoplus_{m\geq 0}V^{\otimes m}=\left\{(t_{0},\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{t}_{M},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\dots)\,:\,\mathbf{t}_{m}\in V^{\otimes m}\text{ for }m\in\mathbb{N},M\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

(1.10)

All algebraic operations carry over from
\mathsf{T}((V))
to
\mathsf{T}(V)
. Hence, we may equivalently define
\mathsf{T}(V)
as the subalgebra of
\mathsf{T}((V))
where all but finitely many projections are zero.

For example, take
V=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then the degree-
0
term is a scalar, the degree-
1
term is a vector, the degree-
2
term is matrix, the degree-
3
term is a
d\times d\times d
array, and so forth.
This process of turning
V
into an algebra
\mathsf{T}(V)
or
\mathsf{T}((V))
is a free construction; informally this means that this is the minimal structure that turns
V
into an algebra; for more details about tensor algebras, see
[
299
,
155
,
217
]
.

Definition 1.7

(Tensor algebra over a Hilbert space)
.

Now, let us consider the case when
V=\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space.
We now define for
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\bigoplus_{m\geq 0}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m}
their inner product as

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\right\rangle_{\bigoplus_{m\geq 0}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m}}=\sum_{m\geq 0}\left\langle\mathbf{s}_{m},\mathbf{t}_{m}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m}},

(1.11)

where the inner product
\left\langle\mathbf{s}_{m},\mathbf{t}_{m}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m}}
on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m}
is as in (
1.4
).
Finally, the topological completion of
\bigoplus_{m\geq 0}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m}
in this inner product gives a Hilbert space
\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{H})
, which we call the tensor algebra over the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, which can be equivalently defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{H})=\{\mathbf{t}=(t_{0},\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2},\ldots):\mathbf{t}_{m}\in\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m},\,\langle\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{H})}<\infty\}.

(1.12)

1.1.3
Duals of tensors

In this section, we study linear functionals acting on tensor algebras over vector spaces following the exposition in
[
182
]
. Recall that the algebraic dual
V^{\star}
for a vector space
V
denotes the set of all linear functions from
V
to
\mathbb{R}
, i.e.
L(V,\mathbb{R})
. When
V
is finite-dimensional,
V
and
V^{\star}
are isomorphic in a basis dependent way, and in particular
\dim V=\dim V^{\star}
. Let
\left\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{d}\right\}
be a basis of
V
. This induces a dual basis
\{\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\star},\dots\mathbf{e}_{d}^{\star}\}
of
V^{\star}
uniquely defined by

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\star},\mathbf{e}_{j}\right\rangle=\delta_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }i=j,\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1.13)

Similarly, we know that the basis of
V^{\otimes m}
is given by

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{i_{m}}\,:\,i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in[d]\right\},

(1.14)

and the basis of
(V^{\star})^{\otimes m}
is given by the elements

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}^{\star}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{i_{m}}^{\star}\,:\,i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in[d]\right\}.

(1.15)

Now, note that
\left(V^{\otimes m}\right)^{\star}\cong(V^{\star})^{\otimes m}
, where the isomorphism is given by identifying the basis of
\left(V^{\otimes m}\right)^{\star}
with the basis of
(V^{\star})^{\otimes m}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}^{\star}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{i_{m}}^{\star}\mapsto\left(\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{i_{m}}\right)^{\star},

(1.16)

and this isomorphism is natural, that is, independent of the choice of basis for V, i.e.

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}^{\star}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{i_{m}}^{\star},\mathbf{e}_{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{j_{m}}\right\rangle=\delta_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\delta_{i_{m},j_{m}}.

(1.17)

The linear action of
(E^{\star})^{\otimes m}
on
E^{\otimes m}
extends naturally to a linear mapping
(E^{\star})^{\otimes m}\to\mathsf{T}((V))^{\star}
defined for a
\mathbf{t}=(t_{0},\mathbf{t}_{1},\dots)\in\mathsf{T}((V))
by

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}^{\star}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{i_{m}}^{\star},\mathbf{t}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}^{\star}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{i_{m}}^{\star},\mathbf{t}_{m}\right\rangle.

(1.18)

In essence, if we think of
\mathbf{t}
as a non-commuting power series in the letters
\left\{i_{1},\dots i_{d}\right\}
, then this action picks up the coefficient of the word
I=(i_{1},\dots i_{m})\in[d]^{m}
. Analogously, by taking linear combinations of such linear functionals, the action of
\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
can be extended to a linear mapping
\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})\to\mathsf{T}((V))^{\star}
, and with this identification,
\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
can be identified as a strict subspace of
\mathsf{T}((V))^{\star}
. It is important that
\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
contains sequences of tensors with finitely many nonzero coefficients, since this allows to consider its action on
\mathsf{T}((V))
without any issues of convergence.

Next, we introduce a product on
\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
which turns it into a commutative algebra.

Definition 1.8

(Shuffle product
[
179
]
)
.

Let
\left\{\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\star},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{d}^{\star}\right\}
be a basis of
V^{\star}
, and let
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in[d]^{n}
,
J=(i_{n+1},\dots,i_{n+m})\in[d]^{m}
be arbitrary multi-indices. The shuffle product of two elementary tensors
\mathbf{e}_{I}^{\star}\in(V^{\star})^{\otimes n}
and
\mathbf{e}_{J}^{\star}\in(V^{\star})^{\otimes m}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{I}^{\star}\mathrel{\text{$\mspace{2mu}$\draw_cap_round:\draw_join_round:$\mspace{2mu}$}}\mathbf{e}_{J}^{\star}\sum_{\sigma\in\sh(n,m)}\mathbf{e}^{\star}_{i_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{e}^{\star}_{i_{\sigma^{-1}(n+m)}},

(1.19)

where the summation is taken over all
(n,m)
-shuffles, that is over the subset of all permutations of
\left\{1,2,\dots,n+m\right\}
, that satisfy
\sigma(1)<\dots<\sigma(n)
and
\sigma(n+1)<\dots<\sigma(n+m)
.
It can be checked that this construction does not depend on the choice of basis, and turns
\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
into a commutative algebra by considering its linear extension.

1.1.4
Motivating example: polynomial features

Finally, before introducing path signatures, we consider a motivating example in the spirit of statistical learning, and recall how polynomials in the coordinates of a vector
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be used to approximate continuous functions on compact sets, a property known as universality in the machine learning literature.

Definition 1.9

(Tensor exponential)
.

The tensor exponential is defined as

\displaystyle\exp_{\otimes}:V\to\mathsf{T}((V)),\quad\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{x})=\left(1,\mathbf{x},\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\otimes 2}}{2!},\dots,\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}}{m!},\dots\right).

(1.20)

That is, the map
\exp_{\otimes}
takes a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and maps it to the infinite sequence of tensors, which has as its degree-
m
term
\exp_{\otimes_{m}}(\mathbf{x})={\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}}/{m!}
.

Let
V=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We can build a functions
f:V\to\mathbb{R}
by taking linear functionals of
\exp_{\otimes}
, that is for
\ell\in\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
, we may consider the composition

\displaystyle f:\mathbf{x}\mapsto\ell\odot\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{x})=\left\langle\ell,\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle.

(1.21)

Hence, any choice of
\ell\in\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
induces a function
f:V\to\mathbb{R}
, which is a linear function in terms of
\exp_{\otimes}
. It might be helpful to spell out the definition of
f
in coordinates. By the definition of
\ell=(\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{M},\mathbf{0},\dots)\in\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
, we have for some
M\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\left\langle\ell,\exp_{\otimes}(x)\right\rangle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\frac{1}{m!}\left\langle\ell_{m},\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}\right\rangle.

(1.22)

Spelled out in coordinates,
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
and
\ell_{m}=\sum_{I\in[d]^{m}}a_{I}\mathbf{e}_{I}^{\star}
, this reads as

\displaystyle\left\langle\ell_{m},\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}\right\rangle=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in[d]}a_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}\mathbf{e}^{\star}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in[d]}a_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{m}}.

(1.23)

Thus putting this together, formulated in coordinates
f
is written as

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=a_{0}+a_{1}x_{1}+\cdots a_{d}x_{d}

(1.24)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2!}\left(a_{1,1}x_{1}^{2}+a_{1,2}x_{1}x_{2}+\cdots+a_{d,d}x_{d}^{2}\right)

(1.25)

\displaystyle+\vdots

(1.26)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{M!}\left(a_{1,\ldots,1}x_{1}^{M}+\cdots+a_{d,\ldots,d}x_{d}^{M}\right)

(1.27)

which is a polynomial of degree-
m
. The Weierstrass approximation theorem guarantees that continuous functions can be uniformly approximated over compact sets in terms of polynomials. This property makes polynomials a classical choice for machine learning, making it possible to learn from vector-valued data. Due to this approximation property, fit datasets well, but they can exhibit arbitrarily large oscillations between the interpolation points, and they are often replaced by other nonlinearities
[
216
]
.

1.2
Path signatures

In this part, let
(V,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{V})
be a finite-dimensional Banach space. In the following, we define the signature of a path and shed light on its properties, which make it a viable feature map for learning from streamed data. Path signatures can be seen as a natural generalization of the tensor exponential map, but instead of mapping a point in
V
to a sequence of tensors, its domain is the space of paths.
It generalizes many of the nice properties of polynomial features such as universality and simultaneously gives the option to ignore time-parameterization without an explicit search over all possible time changes (like in dynamic time warping approaches).

1.2.1
Definition

Definition 1.10
.

A continuous path
\mathbf{x}:[0,T]\to V
is of bounded variation if

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}=\sup_{D\subset[0,T]}\sum_{t_{i}\in D}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}_{t_{i}}-\mathbf{x}_{t_{i-1}}\right\rVert_{V}<\infty,

(1.28)

where
D=\{0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n}=T\,:\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
runs over all finite partitions of
[0,T]
.

We denote the set of bounded variation paths on some arbitrary time horizon by

\displaystyle\mathsf{Paths}(V)=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in C([0,T],V):T\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{x}_{0}=0,\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1\texttt{-var}}<\infty\right\},

To ease the notation, let us define the continuous
m
-simplex:

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}([0,T])=\left\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\,:\,0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}<T\right\},

(1.29)

and for a path
(\mathbf{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
denote its time horizon by
{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}=T
.

Definition 1.11
.

A classic way to obtain a structured and hierarchical description of a path
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
is by computing a sequence of iterated integrals called the path signature given as tensors of increasing degrees such that the degree-
m
object is

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{x}}])}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{t_{m}}=\int_{\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{x}}])}\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{t_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{t_{m}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\,\mathrm{d}t_{m},

(1.30)

where the integrals are defined in the Riemann–Stieltjes sense. Formally, we refer to the map that takes a path to its iterated integrals as the path signature map, and it is defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}:\mathsf{Paths}(V)\to\mathsf{T}((V)),\quad\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})=\left(1,\mathsf{S}_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\mathsf{S}_{2}(\mathbf{x}),\ldots\right).

(1.31)

For a path
(\mathbf{x}_{t})_{t}=(x^{1}_{t},\dots,x^{d}_{t})_{t}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
that evolves in
V
, one can spell this out in coordinates: the
m
-th signature level
\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x})\in V^{\otimes m}
is the tensor that has as its
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})\in[d]^{m}
–th coordinate entry the real number computed by the Riemann–Stieltjes integral

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}=\int_{\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])}\,\mathrm{d}x^{i_{1}}_{t_{1}}\cdots\,\mathrm{d}x^{i_{m}}_{t_{m}}=\int_{\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])}\dot{x}^{i_{1}}_{t_{1}}\cdots\dot{x}^{i_{m}}_{t_{m}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\,\mathrm{d}t_{m}

where
\dot{x}^{i}_{t}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x^{i}_{t}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}
denotes the time derivative.

Proposition 1.12

(Factorial decay
[
182
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
be a bounded variation path. We have for the level-
m
path signature feature for
m\in\mathbb{N}
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{V^{\otimes m}}\leq\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{{1\texttt{-var}}}^{m}}{m!}.

(1.32)

Next, we show that there is a precise connection between the algebra structure of
\mathsf{T}((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and operations on paths. Hence, we first define the concatenation operation on paths, and then state how this affects the signature.

Definition 1.13

(Path concatenation)
.

Let
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
such that
\mathbf{x}:[0,S]\to V
and
\mathbf{y}:[0,T]\to V
. Their concatenation denoted by
\mathbf{x}\star\mathbf{y}
is defined for
t\in[0,S+T]
as

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x}\star\mathbf{y})_{t}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{x}_{t}\quad&\text{if }t\in[0,S]\\
\mathbf{x}_{S}-\mathbf{y}_{0}+\mathbf{y}_{t-S}&\text{if }t\in[S,S+T].\end{cases}

(1.33)

Proposition 1.14

(Chen identity
[
182
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x}\star\mathbf{y})=\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{y}).

(1.34)

Similarly, path reversal can also be represented in terms of the signature.

Proposition 1.15

(Path reversal
[
182
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
defined on
[0,T]
taking values in
V
. Denote by
(\overleftarrow{\mathbf{x}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}=(\mathbf{x}_{T-t})_{t\in[0,T]}
. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(\overleftarrow{\mathbf{x}})=\mathsf{S}^{-1}(\mathbf{x}).

(1.35)

In particular, the range of
\mathsf{S}:\mathsf{Paths}(V)\to\mathsf{T}((V))
is a group.

1.2.2
Properties

Invariance under tree-like equivalence

A classic result going back to Chen
[
40
]
shows that the map
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})
is injective up to tree-like equivalence.
Loosely speaking, tree-like equivalence is from a purely analytic point of view more natural to work with than reparameterization since tree-like equivalence between paths is analogous to Lebesgue almost sure equivalence between sets.
Howevever, from a practical point of view, the difference between paths that are tree-like equivalent and paths that differ by a reparameterization is negligible and we invite the reader to use them as synonyms throughout.
Nevertheless, we give the precise definition below and refer the interested reader to
[
110
]
for a detailed discussion.

Definition 1.16
.

A bounded variation path
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
is
tree-like
if there exists a continuous function
h:[0,T]\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that
h(0)=h(T)=0
and for all
s<t

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{x}_{t}-\mathbf{x}(s)\right\rVert_{V}\leq h(s)+h(t)-2\inf_{u\in[s,t]}h(u).

(1.36)

Proposition 1.17
.

Let
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
be two paths of bounded variation. Then

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})=\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{y})

(1.37)

if and only if
\mathbf{x}\star\overleftarrow{\mathbf{y}}
is tree-like where
\star
denotes path concatenation and
\overleftarrow{\mathbf{y}}
denotes path reversal.

In particular for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
and
\ell\in\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
, for any functions of the form
f(\mathbf{x})=\langle\ell,\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})\rangle
,

f(\mathbf{x})=f(\mathbf{y})
if and only if
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
differ by parameterization (strictly speaking, by a tree-like equivalence).
This ability to factor out time-invariance can be very powerful since the space of all possible parameterizations is huge and we never make an explicit search over possible time changes like in the DTW distance.

However, we are often interested in time parameterization, and the path signature can be made sensitive to it by adding an extra coordinate to the path
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
before computing the signature features of this enhanced path
\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}=(t,\mathbf{x}_{t})\in\mathbb{R}\oplus V
. In other words, we make the parameterization part of the trajectory. The augmented path
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
is never tree-like since the first coordinate is strictly increasing. Hence, the map
\mathsf{Paths}(V)\ni\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathsf{S}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})\in\mathsf{T}((\mathbb{R}\oplus V))
is injective.

Universality

One of the most attractive properties of the monomial feature map
\mathbf{x}\rightarrow\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{x})
for vectors
\mathbf{x}\in V
, is that any continuous function
f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
can be uniformly approximated on compact sets as a linear functional of
\exp_{\otimes}
, that is
f(\mathbf{x})\approx\langle\ell,\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{x})\rangle
for some
\ell\in\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
.
The reason is that linear combinations of monomials (polynomials) form an algebra and the Stone–Weierstrass theorem applies.
Such approximation properties of feature maps are usually referred to as “universality” in the ML literature.
One of the most attractive properties of the signature feature map
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})
for paths
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
is that a universality result holds. A central result in this direction is the shuffle identity for path signatures, which we state below.

Theorem 1.18

(Shuffle identity
[
182
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
, and
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\langle\ell_{1},\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle\left\langle\ell_{2},S(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle=\left\langle\ell_{1}\mathrel{\text{$\mspace{2mu}$\draw_cap_round:\draw_join_round:$\mspace{2mu}$}}\ell_{2},\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle

(1.38)

In particular, the range of the path signature forms an algebra of real-valued functons. This means that the Stone-Weierstrass theorem applies, and linear functionals acting on the signature allow to approximate continuous functions on paths.

Proposition 1.19
.

For every continuous
f:\mathsf{Paths}(V)\rightarrow V
,
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathsf{Paths}(V)
compact,
\epsilon>0
there exists a
\ell\in\mathsf{T}(V^{\star})
such that

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K}}|f(\mathbf{x})-\langle\ell,\mathsf{S}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})\rangle|<\epsilon.}

(1.39)

The analogous result holds for paths
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(V)
without a time coordinate by replacing the domain of paths by equivalence classes of paths under tree-like equivalence.
For a proof and many extensions, see
[
44
]
.

Informally, iterated integrals of paths can be seen as a generalization of classical monomials and from this perspective, the approximation (
1.39
) can be regarded as the extension of classic polynomial regression to path-valued data.
Thus at least informally it is not surprising, that vanilla signature features suffer from similar drawbacks as classic monomial features; for example, if classic monomials are replaced by other nonlinearities this often drastically improves the approximations; see Section
1.2.4
for kernelized methods.

1.2.3
Examples

Linear paths

Consider the path
\mathbf{x}:[0,1]\rightarrow V
that just runs along a straight line
\mathbf{x}:t\mapsto t\mathbf{v}
, where
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a given vector.
Plugging this into the definition of the path signature (
1.30
), we get by a direct calculation that

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\otimes m}}{m!}\in V^{\otimes m},\quad\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})=\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{v})\in\mathsf{T}((V))

(1.40)

We see that for this special case the that
\mathbf{x}
is determined by its increment
\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0}=\mathbf{v}
, the path signature
\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})
equals the monomial feature map
\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{v})
of the total increment
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0}
. This shows how in this special case the signature reduces to monomial features, and this is one of the many reasons why signatures are regarded as “polynomials of paths”.

Piecewise linear paths

In general, these integrals need to be computed by standard integration techniques, but for a piecewise linear path
\mathbf{x}:[0,T]\to V
, that is partitioned into
L
disjoint intervals,
[0,T]=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{L}[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
, and
\mathbf{x}
is piecewise linear on each of these pieces with increments
\mathbf{x}_{t_{i}}-\mathbf{x}_{t_{i-1}}=\mathbf{v}_{i}
for some
\mathbf{v}_{i}\in V
for
i\in[L]
, these iterated integrals can be computed by Chen’s identity (Proposition
1.14
) via

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})=\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x}|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]})\cdot\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x}|_{[t_{1},t_{2}]})\cdots\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x}|_{[t_{L-1},t_{L}]})=\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{v}_{1})\cdot\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{v}_{2})\cdots\exp_{\otimes}(\mathbf{v}_{L}),

(1.41)

where
\mathbf{x}|_{[t_{i},t_{i+1}]}
denotes the restriction of
\mathbf{x}
to
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
.
Performing the algebra multiplication from (
1.8
), we get that the degree-
m
term reduces to an iterated sum of the form

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{m}\leq L}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!}\mathbf{v}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{i_{m}}\in V^{\otimes m},

(1.42)

where the coefficient
\mathbf{i}!
is such that if there are
k\in[m]
distinct entries among
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}
with number of repetitions
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
, then
\mathbf{i}!={p_{1}!\cdots p_{k}!}
.

1.2.4
Signature kernels

Kernel trick for path signatures

Signature kernels provide a means to compare paths through their signatures by defining inner products in the tensor algebra. Given their structure, they offer a powerful similarity measure for sequences of potentially different lengths, making them particularly suitable for sequential data analysis. The signature kernel inherently captures the algebraic and geometric structure of the paths by inheriting the powerful properties of path signatures, such as invariance to reparameterization and universality, see
[
33
, Thm. 2.2.4]
for a proof that the signature kernel is universal in the kernel learning sense.

Next, we will consider the setting when
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space, and our bounded variation paths evolve in this space, that is,
\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{H})
. Then, the signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}:\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{H})\times\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{H})\to\mathbb{R}
is defined for paths
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{H})
by taking the inner product of signatures

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x}),\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{H})}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\langle\mathsf{S}_{m}(x),\mathsf{S}_{m}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m}}

(1.43)

where
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{H})}
denotes the inner product in the tensor algebra
\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{H})
over the Hilbert space
\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{H})
, which itself is indeed a Hilbert space as discussed in Section
1.1.2
. In practice, one often introduces a truncation factor
M\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, and considers

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}(x,y)=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\langle\mathsf{S}_{m}(x),\mathsf{S}_{m}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m}},

(1.44)

and with some abuse of notation we may omit the truncation superscript, that is, when talking about the signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
, we may mean the truncated version.

First,
[
146
]
developed an algorithm for finite
M
, and later
[
228
]
formulated a
PDE
to solve for infinite
M
. The kernel trick of
[
146
]
is written on the level-
m
for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{H})
as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{s}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])\\
\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{y}}}])\end{subarray}}\left\langle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{s_{1}},\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\cdots\left\langle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{s_{m}},\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{t_{m}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

(1.45)

This allows to compute
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
solely by inner product evaluation on
\mathcal{H}
.

Lifting paths by static kernels

Since the kernel trick introduced above allows to compute the inner product of path signatures only by inner product evaluation, it is often beneficial to preprocess paths by lifting them to a higher-dimensional space, which can enhance the richness of the information captured by the signature. One such tool for this is the method of reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (
RKHS
), which has become wide-spread in machine learning, since it allows for nonlinear learning in infinite-dimensional spaces using the kernel trick
[
21
]
.

Now let
\mathcal{X}
be a set, which will be our data space where our paths take values. Further, let
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be a so-called static kernel on
\mathcal{X}
with
RKHS

{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
. Let us denote for an
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}}=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\cdot)\in{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
the reproducing kernel lift of
\mathbf{x}
. Analogously, for a path
(\mathbf{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathbf{x})
denote by
\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}}=(\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{t}})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathsf{Paths}({\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}})
the path
\mathbf{x}
lifted into the
RKHS
of
\mathsf{k}
,
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
. Then, (
1.45
) can formally be written for the lifts of
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})
as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])\\
\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{y}}}])\end{subarray}}\left\langle\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{s_{1}}},\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}\cdots\left\langle\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{s_{m}}},\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{t_{m}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}

(1.46)

\displaystyle=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])\\
\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{y}}}])\end{subarray}}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa(s_{1},t_{1}),\cdots\,\mathrm{d}\kappa(s_{m},t_{m}),

(1.47)

where
\kappa([s,t]\times[u,v])=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{v})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{u})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{s},\mathbf{y}_{v})+\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{s},\mathbf{y}_{u})
is a signed Borel measure on
[0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}]\times[0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{y}}}]
for fixed
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})
, see
[
146
, Thm. 2]
for a proof. This allows to compute the signature kernel for paths lifted into reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces, that significantly enhances the expressiveness of the signature kernel. Note that
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is a genuine infinite-dimensional space for non-degenerate kernels
\mathsf{k}
, for which there are various choices, see
[
216
, Ch. 4]
, e.g. the
RBF
or Matérn family of kernels. Abstractly, we can think of the signature kernel as taking a static kernel
\mathsf{k}
on the state space
\mathcal{X}
and turning it into a kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
for paths evolving in that state space. In the following, we will treat the static kernel
\mathsf{k}
as a hyperparameter of
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
, and we do not make the composition explicit, i.e. for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})
, we will write
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
and implicitly mean that
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
are first lifted into an
RKHS
before taking signatures. The standard formulation of signatures when
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{E}
is a Euclidean space can be recovered by using the linear kernel
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}
as the static kernel lift for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{E}
.

1.2.5
Rough paths

So far we assumed that our input domain consists of paths of bounded variation paths, but in the real-world the evolution of quantities is often subject to noise, e.g. a classical model in physics and engineering is
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\mathbf{a}_{t}+B_{t}
, where
\mathbf{a}
is a bounded variation path, but
B
is a Brownian sample path. Since Brownian sample paths are not of bounded variation,
\mathbf{x}
is not of bounded variation.
However, the iterated integrals of
\mathbf{x}
can still be defined as above, but one has to replace the iterated Riemann–Stieltjes integrals by Ito–Stratonovich integrals in the definition of
\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})
.
Even rougher trajectories such as fractional Brownian motion and non-Markovian processes can be handled that way with so-called rough path integrals.
Rough path theory provides a systematic study that comes with a rich toolbox combining analytic and algebraic estimates, rich enough to cover the trajectories of large classes of stochastic processes; see
[
182
,
178
,
87
]
.

1.2.6
Discrete-time setting

In practice, we often do not have access to paths, but are only given discrete-time observations that lie in some state space
\mathcal{X}
, that is, we work with the space of finite length sequences

\displaystyle\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})=\left\{\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\,:\,\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L}\in\mathcal{X},\mathbf{x}_{0}=\mathbf{0},L\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

(1.48)

where we denote the “effective” length of a sequence
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
by
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}=L
. Notice that for sequences we use
0
-based indexing, that does not count to the length. We make this choice because signatures use sequence increments, which reduces length by
1
-step.

Below, we will assume that
\mathcal{X}
is either a topological vector space (i.e.
\mathbb{R}^{d}
), or that as a preprocessing step our input sequence
\mathbf{x}
has been lifted to one using a static feature map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to V
, i.e. consider
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
substituted in place of
\mathbf{x}
which we do not make explicit here.

Discretized signature features

It is not obvious how to extend path signatures to the discrete-time setting, since they are defined in continuous-time via iterated integrals, and we discuss multiple choices below. First, we consider the so-called “canonical” choice, which proceeds by identifying
\mathbf{x}
with a continuous time path by its linear interpolation for any choice of time parameterization, and then computing the signature of this path.

To ease the notational burden, we define the following discrete-time
m
-simplices:

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}(L)=\left\{(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{m}\,:\,1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{m}\leq L\right\},

(1.49)

and its closed companion

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}_{m}(L)=\left\{(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{m}\,:\,1\leq i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{m}\leq L\right\}.

(1.50)

Then, under this choice of path embedding (linear interpolation), we can compute the level –
m
signature of
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
using the relation (
1.42
) by

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!}\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},

(1.51)

where
\delta\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i-1}
refers to first-order (backwards) sequence differencing.

Before discussing (
1.51
) in detail, let us first generalize this formula. Thus, next we consider other choices for approximating iterated path integrals using discrete-time data. We introduce the order-
p
approximation to the path signature considered in
[
146
, App. B]
. The order-
p
level-
m
signature feature
\mathsf{S}^{(p)}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{X})
of a sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}^{(p)}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!^{(p)}}\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},

(1.52)

where
\mathbf{i}!^{(p)}
is such that if there are
k
distinct indices in
\mathbf{i}
with number of repetitions
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
respectively, then it is defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}!^{(p)}=\begin{cases}p_{1}!\cdots p_{k}!\quad&\text{for }p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\leq p\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1.53)

Now, we notice that (
1.52
) is a generalization of (
1.51
), since the latter can be recovered by choosing
p=m
. As mentioned before, we refer to
p
as the order hyperparameter of the discretized signature features. Notice that (
1.52
) summarizes the sequence
\mathbf{x}
by a summation over its possibly contiguous length-
m
subsequences with at most
p
-repetitions for each sequence index, such that each term is normalized by a factor depending on the number of repetitions for each unique index. In particular, we will often focus on the
p=1
case, which is often referred to as a non-geometric approximation since there does not exist a continuous-time path such that
\mathsf{S}^{(1)}(\mathbf{x})
is its path signature. Nevertheless, a key observation of this thesis is that the order-
1
case often works well or even better than higher orders, while preserving a considerable amount of computation time and memory. This is explained in
[
263
]
, which is an extension of Chapter
3
, where my collaborator Patric proved that the
p=1
case is universal on the space of sequences given the existence a time coordinate which encodes the position within the sequence.

In particular, the
p=1
case can be written for a sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}^{(1)}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},

(1.54)

i.e. notice that the summation is taken exclusively over non-contiguous subsequences, and as such, can be treated as a generalization of
k
-mer features appearing in the context of string kernels, see
[
146
, Sec. 5]
for a connection to such classic kernels.

We remark that different choices of
p
correspond to different algebraic embeddings, i.e. replacing the tensor exponential in (
1.41
) with a different algebra embedding. This is one of the main themes of Chapter
3
, and different choices lead to beside mildly different computational complexities and performances, to interesting algebraic questions, see
[
68
,
69
]
for a discussion.

In the following, we may omit the order superscript when talking about signature features and kernels in the discrete-time setting, and simply treat it as a hyperparameter setting.

Discretized signature kernels

The signature is a powerful feature set for nonlinear regression on streamed data.
A computational bottleneck associated with it is the dimensionality of
\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{X})
. As we are dealing with tensors, for
\mathcal{X}
finite-dimensional
\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x})
is a tensor of degree-
m
which has
\left(\dim\mathcal{X}\right)^{m}
coordinates that need to be computed. This can quickly become computationally expensive. For infinite-dimensional
\mathcal{X}
, it is infeasible to directly compute
\mathsf{S}
.

The work of
[
146
]
takes the setting when
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space, and shows a kernel trick that allows to compute signature kernels for discrete data up to finite truncation
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
using dynamic programming, even when
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
is infinite-dimensional.
Subsequently,
[
228
]
proposed a
PDE
-based algorithm to approximate the untruncated signature kernel, which was further extended in
[
32
]
, and we refer to
[
163
]
for a recent overview of signature kernels.

Here, we focus on discrete-time, and continue the discussion of signature kernels. Our starting point is the generalized order-
p
approach of
[
146
]
, that results in the features (
1.52
).

The signature kernel is a powerful formalism that allows to transform any static kernel on
\mathcal{X}
into a kernel for sequences that evolve in
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be a static kernel with
RKHS

{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
. Now, let
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, and consider the discretized signature kernel of order-
p
and level-
m

{\mathsf{k}^{(p)}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
given as the inner product of order-
p
(lifted) signatures from (
1.52
)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}^{(p)}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathsf{S}_{m}^{(p)}(\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}}),\mathsf{S}_{m}^{(p)}(\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}})\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{\otimes m}}

(1.55)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!^{(p)}}\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}},\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})}\frac{1}{\mathbf{j}!^{(p)}}\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}}\right\rangle

(1.56)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!^{(p)}\mathbf{j}!^{(p)}}\left\langle\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}},\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{j_{1}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}\cdots\left\langle\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}},\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}

(1.57)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!^{(p)}\mathbf{j}!^{(p)}}\delta_{i_{1},j_{1}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}})\cdots\delta_{i_{m},j_{m}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}),

(1.58)

where
\delta_{i,j}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j-1})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i-1},\mathbf{y}_{j})+\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i-1},\mathbf{y}_{j-1})
denotes a second-order (backwards) cross-differencing operator.
The key insight by
[
146
]
is equation (
1.58
), i.e. that
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}
can be computed without computing
\mathsf{S}_{m}
itself by a kernel trick using only kernel evaluations.

The hyperparameters are:

(1)

the choice of the static kernel
\mathsf{k}
, for which there is a wide range of options, e.g. for
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
the
RBF
, exponential, polynomial or Matérn family of kernels;

(2)

any hyperparameters that
\mathsf{k}
comes with, such as the bandwidth or polynomial degree;

(3)

truncation level-
M
;

(4)

the choice of algebra embedding, i.e. the order-
p
;

(5)

and the choice of kernel normalization that scales each level
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}
appropriately. It also comes with theoretical guarantees such as analytic estimates when sequences converge to paths
[
146
]
, its maximum mean discrepancy (
MMD
)
[
107
]
metrizes classic topologies for stochastic processes, and can lead to robust statistics in the classic statistical sense (B-robustness); see
[
44
]
for details.

Although (
1.58
) looks expensive to compute,
[
146
]
applies dynamic programming to efficiently compute
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
for finite
M
using a recursive algorithm; an alternative algorithm is the above mentioned approach of approximating the (untruncated) signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
using
PDE
-discretization. Importantly, (
1.58
) avoids computing tensors, and only depends on the entry-wise evaluations of the static kernel
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})
. Indeed, this leads to a computational cost of
O({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})
with respect to length, that is feasible to compute for sequences evolving in high-dimensional state-spaces, but only with moderate sequence length. Note that the same bottleneck applies to
PDE
-based approaches. In part, the aim of Chapter
5
is to alleviate this quadratic cost in sequence length, while approximately enjoying the modelling capability of working within an infinite-dimensional
RKHS
.

1.3
Algorithms

1.3.1
Notation for algorithms

We define notation based on
[
146
]
for describing vectorized computations. We use
1
-based indexing for arrays. Note that elements outside the bounds of an array are treated as zeros. Let
A
and
B
be k-fold arrays of size
(n_{1}\times\dots\times n_{k})
, indexed by
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,n_{j}\}
for
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

We define the following operations:

(i)

The cumulative sum along axis
j
as:

\displaystyle A[:,\dots,:,\boxplus,:,\dots,:][i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1},i_{j},i_{j+1},\dots i_{k}]=\sum_{\kappa=1}^{i_{j}}A[i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1},\kappa,i_{j+1},\dots,i_{k}].

(1.59)

(ii)

The slice-wise sum along axis
j
as:

\displaystyle A[:,\dots,:,\Sigma,:,\dots,:][i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1},i_{j+1},\dots,i_{k}]=\sum_{\kappa=1}^{n_{j}}A[i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1},\kappa,i_{j+1},\dots i_{k}].

(1.60)

(iii)

The shift along axis
j
by
+m
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
as:

\displaystyle A[:,\dots,:,+m,:,\dots,:][i_{1},\dots,i_{j},\dots,i_{k}]=A[i_{1},\dots,i_{j}-m,\dots i_{k}]

(1.61)

(iv)

The Hadamard product of arrays
A
and
B
:

(A\odot B)[i_{1},\dots,i_{k}]=A[i_{1},\dots,i_{k}]\cdot B[i_{1},\dots,i_{k}].

(v)

Now, if
A
has shape
(n_{1}\times\cdots\times n_{j}\times\cdots\times n_{k})
and
B
has shape
(n_{1}\times\cdots\times n_{j}^{\prime}\times\cdots\times n_{k})
, then their Kronecker product along axis
j
is defined for
i_{j}\in[n_{j}]
and
i_{j}^{\prime}\in[n_{j}^{\prime}]
as

\displaystyle(A\boxtimes_{j}B)[i_{1},\dots,(i_{j}-1)n_{j}^{\prime}+i_{j}^{\prime},\dots,i_{k}]=A[i_{1},\cdots,i_{j},\cdots i_{k}]B[i_{1},\dots,i_{j}^{\prime},\dots,i_{k}].

(1.62)

1.3.2
Algorithms for path signatures

In this section, we provide adapted algorithms for computing signature features and kernels.

First, we provide an algorithm for computing the order-
p
signature features given in (
1.52
) in Algorithm
1
. This algorithm has not appeared explicitly in previous work, and is a combination of Algorithms 5 and 6 in
[
146
]
. The output of the algorithm are flattened signature tensors of dimension
d,d^{2},\dots,d^{M}
. Upon investigation, the time complexity of the algorithm is
O(MNLpd^{M})
and requires a space of
O(p^{2}NLD+Nd^{M})
. This is clearly inefficient due to the polynomail complexity in
d
and exponential in
M
, and in the later Chapters
3
and
5
we discuss how this complexity can be reduced via various low-rank and random projection techniques.

Next, we adapt the dual algorithm for computing the order-
p
signature kernel (
1.58
) from
[
146
, Alg. 6]
under Algorithm
2
. The output of the algorithm consists of signature kernel matrices per level
K_{1},\dots,K_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
. The time complexity of the algorithm is
O(N^{2}L^{2}d+MN^{2}L^{2}p^{2})
, while the space is
O(p^{2}N^{2}L^{2})
, where
d
is the complexity of static kernel evaluation. Due to joint quadratic complexity in
N^{2}
and
L^{2}
, this is infeasible for high-volume (
N
large) and long time series (
L
large). In Chapter
2
, we combine the signature kernel with Gaussian processes and variational inference techniques reducing the complexity to linear in
N
, allowing to scale to large datasets, although the length still remains a bottleneck. Later on, in Chapter
5
, we introduce scalable random approximations to the signature kernel, which alleviate the quadratic complexity in both
N
and
L
, while also scaling favourably in the “feature size” i.e. in the dimensionality of the random feature map that represents the random approximate kernel.

Algorithm 1

Computing order-
p
signature features (
1.52
).

1:

Input:
Time series
X=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{N})\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, truncation-
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, order-
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

2:

Optional: Add time-parameterization
\mathbf{x}_{i}\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{i,t},t/{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}})_{t=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}}}
for all
i\in[N]

3:

Tabulate to uniform length
L=\max_{j\in[N]}{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}}
by
\mathbf{x}_{i}\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}}})
for all
i\in[N]

4:

Initialize an array
U
with shape
[N,L,d]

5:

Fill
U
with values
U[i,l,:]=\mathbf{x}_{i,l}-\mathbf{x}_{i,l-1}
for all
i\in[N]
and
l\in[L]

6:

Accumulate into level-
1
features
P_{1}\leftarrow U[:,\Sigma,:]

7:

Initialize an array
R
of shape
[1,N,L,d]

8:

Assign values
R[1,:,:,:]\leftarrow U

9:

for

m=2

to

M

do

9:

Let
q\leftarrow\min(p,m)

9:

Collapse into expanding window features
P^{\prime}\leftarrow R[\Sigma,:,\boxplus,:]

9:

Update with next increment
P^{\prime}\leftarrow P^{\prime}[:,+1,:]\boxtimes_{-1}U

10:

Initialize new array
R^{\prime}
of shape
[q,N,L,d]

11:

Assign values
R^{\prime}[1,:,:,:]\leftarrow P^{\prime}

12:

for

r=2
to
q

do

13:

Update with current increment
R^{\prime}[r,:,:,:]\leftarrow\frac{1}{r}R[r-1,:,:,:]\boxtimes_{-1}U

14:

end

for

15:

Aggregate into level-
m
features
P_{m}\leftarrow R^{\prime}[\Sigma,:,\Sigma,:]

16:

Roll array
R\leftarrow R^{\prime}

17:

end

for

18:

Output:
Arrays of signature features per level
P_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\dots,P_{M}
.

Algorithm 2

Computing order-
p
signature kernels (
1.58
).

1:

Input:
Sequences
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1,\dots,N}\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, truncation-
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, order-
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, static kernel
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}

2:

Optional: Add time-parameterization
\mathbf{x}_{i}\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{i,t},t/{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}})_{t=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}}}
for all
i\in[N]

3:

Tabulate to uniform length
L=\max_{j\in[N]}{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}}
by
\mathbf{x}_{i}\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}}})
for all
i\in[N]

4:

Initialize an array
K
with shape
[N,N,L,L]

5:

Compute kernel increments
K[i,j,k,l]=\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i,k},\mathbf{x}_{j,k})
for
i,j\in[N]
and
k,l\in[L]

6:

Collapse into level-
1
kernel entries
K_{1}\leftarrow K[:,:,\Sigma,\Sigma]

7:

Initialize an array
R
of shape
[1,1,N,N,L,L]

8:

Assign values
R[1,1,:,:,:,:]\leftarrow K

9:

for

m=2

to

M

do

10:

Let
q\leftarrow\min(p,m)

11:

K^{\prime}\leftarrow R[\Sigma,\Sigma,:,:,\boxplus+1,\boxplus+1]\odot K

12:

Initialize new array
R^{\prime}
of shape
[q,q,N,N,L,L]

13:

Assign values
R^{\prime}[1,1,:,:,:,:]\leftarrow K^{\prime}

14:

for

r=2

to

q

do

15:

R^{\prime}[r,1,:,:,:,:]\leftarrow\frac{1}{r}R[r-1,\Sigma,:,:,:,\boxplus+1]\odot K

16:

R^{\prime}[1,r,:,:,:,:]\leftarrow\frac{1}{r}R[\Sigma,r-1,:,:,\boxplus+1,:]\odot K

17:

for

s=2

to

q

do

18:

R^{\prime}[r,s,:,:,:,:]\leftarrow\frac{1}{rs}R[r-1,s-1,:,:,:,:]\odot K

19:

end

for

20:

end

for

21:

Collapse into level-
m
kernel entries
K_{m}\leftarrow R^{\prime}[\Sigma,\Sigma,:,:,\Sigma,\Sigma]

22:

Roll array
R\leftarrow R^{\prime}

23:

end

for

24:

Output:
Signature kernel matrices per level
K_{1},\dots,K_{M}
.

Chapter 2
Gaussian Processes with Signature Covariances

2.1
Introduction

The evolution of some state variable, parameter or object gives naturally gives rise to sequential data, which is defined by having a notion of order on the incoming information.
The ordering relation, or index set does not have to represent physical time, but for simplicity we will call it as such. When the index-set is discrete, we refer to the data as a sequence, and when continuous as a path.
For example, besides time series (
TS
), sources of sequential data are text
[
205
]
,
DNA

[
114
]
, or even topological data analysis
[
43
]
.
This ubiquity of sequential data has received special attention by the machine learning community in recent years.

In this chapter, we are focused on combining signature kernels with these approaches:

Bayesian approaches

Often not only point predictions, but estimates of the associated uncertainties are required
[
94
]
.
Gaussian processes (
GP
)
[
216
]
provide flexible priors over functions of the data in nonparametric Bayesian models.
In the context of sequential data, two prominent ways to use
GP
s are:

(1)

using as covariance functions kernels specifically designed for sequences
[
176
,
58
,
59
,
5
]
,

(2)

modelling the evolution in a latent space, that emits the observations, as a discrete dynamical system with a
GP
prior on the transition function, a model called the Gaussian Process State Space Model (
GPSSM
)
[
85
,
84
,
187
,
78
,
70
,
127
]
.

These two approaches are not mutually exclusive; if one models the latent system as a higher order Markov process, then sequence kernels can incorporate the effect of past states.

Deep learning approaches

Deep learning approaches, such as the celebrated
LSTM
network
[
120
]
, other forms of
RNN
s
[
45
]
, convolutional networks, and transformers
[
275
]
have successfully been applied to a variety of tasks involving sequential data
[
254
,
201
,
285
]
. Deep learning models can approximate any continuous function, but the cost is a large number of parameters, high variance and poor interpretability.
This leaves the door open for alternative approaches not only as competitors, but as complementary building blocks in larger models.

Contribution

In principle, one can just use the signature kernel and algorithms as covariance function to define a
GP
for sequential data.
However, the computational complexity becomes quickly prohibitive, which ultimately does not lead to scalable performance on many time series benchmarks.
We therefore develop a different approach to signature covariances that builds on two recent advances in
GP
inference: variational inference
[
259
,
115
,
186
]
and inter-domain inducing points
[
157
]
to alleviate the computational burden.
In particular, we show that one can use low-rank tensors as inter-domain inducing points by optimizing a variational bound.
Moreover, we use this
GP
as a building block in combination with
RNN
s to build models that combine the strenghts of signatures, Bayesian models, and deep learning architectures.
This results in scalable inference algorithms and we use this to benchmark on standard
TS
datasets

(i)

against popular non-Bayesian time series classifiers purely in terms of accuracy,

(ii)

against alternative Bayesian models by comparing the calibration of uncertainties for predictions

.
Code and benchmarks are publically available at
http://github.com/tgcsaba/GPSig
.

2.2
Background and Notation

Given data
(\mathbf{X},Y)
consisting of
n_{\mathbf{X}}
inputs
