\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n_{\mathbf{X}}})\subset\mathcal{X}
with labels
Y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n_{\mathbf{X}}})\subset\mathbb{R}
, the Bayesian approach is to put a prior on a set of functions
\{f\,:\,f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}
, update this prior by conditioning on
(\mathbf{X},Y)
, and then use the resulting posterior to make inference about the label
y_{\star}
of an unseen point
\mathbf{x}_{\star}
.
When this is done with Gaussians, the central object is a
GP

f=(f_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}
which is specified by the mean and covariance functions.
Below we recall how covariances can be constructed from polynomial features, which we then extend to signatures.

2.2.1
The feature space view

Given a map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow V
that injects
\mathcal{X}
into a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, a natural way to put a prior on a function class
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is to consider linear functions of
\varphi
as model, that is
f_{\mathbf{x}}=\langle\ell,\varphi(\mathbf{x})\rangle
to model
f(\mathbf{x}_{i})\approx y_{i}
for some “weights“
\ell\in\mathcal{H}
. Uncertainty about
f
is then specified by uncertainty about
\ell
(and the hyperparameters of
\varphi
).
We refer to
\varphi
as a feature map and to
\mathcal{H}
as feature space.
The advantage of the nonparametric approach is that it allows one to avoid explicitly computing features and weights, and to perform inference directly on the function
f
.
Throughout we assume that
f=(f_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}
is a centered
GP
.
Then, predictions about unseen points can then be made by Bayesian inference using Gaussian conditioning.
If the task is classification where the labels
Y
are discrete, such an approach can be still applied by using a
GP

f=(f_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}
as nuiscance function to put a prior on the class membership probability by specifying
p(y=1|\mathbf{x})=\sigma(f_{\mathbf{x}})
, where
\sigma
is for example a sigmoid.

2.2.2
Polynomial features

First, we review the classical case of how a Gaussian process can be constructed from polynomial features, which we will later on generalize to signatures.

Now, let
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and define the monomial feature map

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathbf{x})=(1,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\otimes 2},\mathbf{x}^{\otimes 3},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\otimes M})

(2.1)

where
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}
is a tensor.
We refer to Section
1.1
about tensors.
If we set
f_{\mathbf{x}}=\langle\ell,\varphi(\mathbf{x})\rangle
and put a centered Gaussian prior on
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{M})
, then it follows from Section
1.1
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\mathbf{y}}\right]

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\ell\otimes\ell,\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}\otimes\mathbf{y}^{\otimes m}\right\rangle\right]

(2.2)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\ell\otimes\ell\right],\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}\otimes\mathbf{y}^{\otimes m}\right\rangle

(2.3)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\langle\Sigma_{m}^{2},\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}\otimes\mathbf{y}^{\otimes m}\rangle

(2.4)

where
\Sigma_{m}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\ell_{m}\otimes\ell_{m}]\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes(2m)}
.
Taking
\Sigma_{m}^{2}
to be an isotropic “diagonal“’ tensor
\Sigma_{m}^{2}=\sigma_{m}^{2}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}=1}^{m}(e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{m}})^{\otimes 2}
recovers the polynomial kernel,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}[f_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\mathbf{y}}]}=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sigma_{m}^{2}\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle^{m}.

(2.5)

Many other variations exist, for example other classes of polynomials, such as Hermite polynomials (the eigenfunctions of the classic
RBF
kernel), can increase the effectiveness, since they allow to make the associated feature expansion infinite dimensional.
However, what makes any such class of polynomials a sensible choice for
\varphi
is that by the Stone–Weierstrass theorem, any continuous compactly supported function
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
can be arbitrary well approximated as linear functions of
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
.
This approximation property is often called universality
[
189
,
245
]
.

2.3
From signature features to covariances

The signature feature map
\mathsf{S}
can be seen as a generalization of the polynomial feature map
\varphi
as defined in (
2.1
) from the domain
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
of vectors to the domain of bounded variation paths.

Recall from Section
1.2
that it is defined for a
(\mathbf{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\int_{\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,T])}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{t_{m}}\right)_{m}.

(2.6)

2.3.1
Automatic parameterization invariance determination

A classic empirical finding that led to dynamic time warping (
DTW
) is that functions of sequences are often invariant to a certain degree of time parameterization: for example, different speakers pronounce words at different speeds.
However, sometimes the parameterization matters, e.g. for financial data.
Thus we do not only care about

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{Paths}}={\{f:\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,:\,\text{continuous and compactly supported}\}}

(2.7)

but also about the subset of it that consist of parameterization invariant functions.
To make this precise, we call
(\mathbf{x}_{s})_{s\in[0,S]}
a reparameterization of
(\mathbf{y}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
if there exists a a smooth increasing function
\rho:[0,S]\rightarrow[0,T]
(the “time change”) such that
\mathbf{x}_{s}=y_{\rho(s)}
for all
s\in[0,S]
.

Often the function we want to learn is invariant to some but not extreme reparameterization, so we need a more nuanced way to quantify parameterization (in)variance.
Hence, what we really want is a hyperparameter
\tau\geq 0
that signifies the degree of parameterization invariance: for
\tau=0
all the prior mass should concentrate on the “extreme case” that is the subset of (
2.7
) consisting parameterization invariant functions; and as
\tau
gets increased the probability mass should spread out to parameterization sensitive functions.
This would allow to infer the degree of parameterization invariance by automatic relevance determination (
ARD
).

To accomplish this, we augment our paths with a hyperparameter
\tau\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x}^{(\tau)}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}=\left(\tau\cdot t,\mathbf{x}_{t})\right)_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\hat{\mathcal{X}}),

(2.8)

where
(\mathbf{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})
and
\hat{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{1+d}
.
This simply makes the parameterization part of the trajectory by adding an extra coordinate, and allows the magnitude of
\tau
to scale the effect of parameterization sensitivity.
Since signatures distinguish different trajectories (but not the speed at which we run through them), it follows that for
\tau>0
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(\tau)})=\Phi(\mathbf{y}^{(\tau)})\text{ if and only if }\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y},

(2.9)

while for
\tau=0
we have,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(0)})=\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{y}^{(0)})\text{ if and only if }\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{y}}

(2.10)

since the extra coordinate is “switched off”.
Here,
\sim
denotes tree-like equivalence, but we invite the reader to read
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{y}
as saying that
\mathbf{x}
is a reparameterization of
\mathbf{y}
.
This is strictly speaking not true and the precise mathematical statement is given in Section
1.2.2
, but note that for real-world data, tree-like equivalence is synonymous with reparameterization.

In the rest of the chapter, we omit the dependence on
\tau
from the notation, but it should be noted that we are still working with the parameterization augmented path
\mathbf{x}^{(\tau)}
in place of
\mathbf{x}
.

Vectors

Paths

Sequences

Domain

\mathcal{X}

\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})

\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})

Features

\varphi(\mathbf{x})=\left(\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}\right)_{m}

\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\int_{\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{t_{m}}\right)_{m}

\mathsf{S}^{(1)}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}\right)_{m}

Feature space

\prod_{m}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}

\prod_{m}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}

\prod_{m}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}

Functions

f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

f:\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

f:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}

Covariance

\sum_{m}\sigma_{m}^{2}\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle^{m}

\sum_{m}\sigma^{2}_{m}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{s}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])\\
\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{y}}}])\end{subarray}}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\left\langle d\mathbf{x}_{s_{m}},d\mathbf{y}_{t_{m}}\right\rangle

\sum_{m}\sigma_{m}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\left\langle\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{k}},\delta\mathbf{y}_{j_{k}}\right\rangle

Table 2.1
:
Comparison of polynomial and signature features

2.3.2
Signature covariances

In this section, we focus on deriving the signature covariances with paths lifted by the linear kernel for simplicity, but the construction extends to other variations, e.g. the
RBF
kernel, by replacing the coordinate-wise expansion of paths with the eigenfunctions of the kernel, which forms an orthonormal basis of the
RKHS
. The formulation gets more technical since in this case the linear functional
\ell
becomes infinite dimensional, and equation (
2.11
) becomes the Karhunen-Loeve expansion for
GP
s
[
3
]
.
Following the feature space view, we now argue in complete analogy to the case of the classical polynomial feature map
\varphi
for
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and define a centered
GP

f=(f_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})}
by putting a centered Gaussian prior on
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{M})
and setting

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{x}}=\left\langle\ell,\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle\quad\text{for }\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X}).

(2.11)

The
GP
is hence fully specified by its covariance function

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\left\langle\Sigma_{m}^{2},\int_{\mathbf{s}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{s_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{s_{m}}\otimes\int_{\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,T])}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{t_{m}}\right\rangle_{m}

(2.12)

that has
(\tau,M,\Sigma_{1}^{2},\ldots,\Sigma_{M}^{2})
as hyperparameters.
In particular, choosing an isotropic covariance structure for
\Sigma_{m}^{2}
completely analogously to how it is done above equation (
2.5
) gives that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sigma_{m}^{2}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{s}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])\\
\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}{([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{y}}}])}\end{subarray}}\left\langle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{s_{1}},\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}\right\rangle\cdots\left\langle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{s_{m}},\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{t_{m}}\right\rangle,

(2.13)

Finally, as we are given data not as paths, but as discrete-time observations
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, in practice we use the order-
1
discretized signature kernel (see Section
1.2.6
) given as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}^{(1)}_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sigma_{m}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\left\langle\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\delta\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}}\right\rangle\cdots\left\langle\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},\delta\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}\right\rangle,

(2.14)

and in forth going we suppress the dependence on the order-
p=1
hyperparameter.

2.3.3
Regularity of the resulting Gaussian process

Next, we want to find out the regularity properties of the
GP
with covariance (
2.13
). However, the index set
\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})
is a very large space so some care is needed.
In Appendix
2.A
, we compute covering numbers that yield explicit bounds on the modulus of continuity in terms of
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
L>0
and
\mathsf{Paths}_{L}(\mathcal{X})=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X}):\|\mathbf{x}\|_{{1\texttt{-var}}}\leq L\}
.
There exists a centered
GP

f=(f_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}_{L}(\mathcal{X})}
with
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
as defined in (
2.13
) as covariance function and that has continuous sample paths
\mathbf{x}\mapsto f_{\mathbf{x}}
.
Further, an explicit bound on its modulus of continuity in terms of
L
is given in equation (
2.33
).

We now have a well-defined
GP
for Bayesian inference for sequences at hand that inherits many of the attractive properties of signature features.
To turn this into useful models for large
TS
benchmarks we develop efficient inference algorithms in the next section.

2.4
Sparse variational inducing tensors

To reiterate, we are given data
(\mathbf{X},Y)
consisting of
n_{\mathbf{X}}
sequences
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n_{\mathbf{X}}})\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
of maximal length
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}}=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}}{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}
that evolve in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with labels
Y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n_{\mathbf{X}}})
, and the task is to predict labels
y_{\star}
of unseen points
\mathbf{x}_{\star}
.
For sequences, the sample size
n_{\mathbf{X}}
and associated covariance matrix inversion is not the only compational bottleneck but also the maximal length
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}}
, and the dimension
d
of the state space matter:
n_{\mathbf{X}}
,
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}}
and
d
can be simultaneously large.

In this section, we introduce a sparse variational inference scheme to approximate the posterior, that locates the inducing points in a space other than the data-domain; this approach is usually called
inter-domain
sparse variational inference
[
157
,
186
]
. This allows for more efficient data-representation and faster inference.
Key to our approach is that signature features take values in a well-understood subset of the feature space
\mathsf{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
This allows as us to augment the index set with structured tensors, and locate inducing points in this larger set.

2.4.1
Variational inference

As is well-known, inference for
GP
s scales as
O(n_{\mathbf{X}}^{3})
, see Section 3.3. in
[
216
]
.
This first led to sparse models,
[
208
]
, that select a subset
\mathbf{Z}=\{\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{n_{\mathbf{Z}}}\}
of
\mathbf{X}
consisting of
n_{\mathbf{Z}}\ll n_{\mathbf{X}}
points, and subsequently to pseudo-inputs,
[
241
]
, that select points
\mathbf{Z}
that are not necessarily in
\mathbf{X}
.
This was a big step towards complexity reduction, but pseudo-inputs are prone to overfitting,
[
185
]
.
A different idea is to treat
\mathbf{Z}
as parameters of a variational approximation
[
259
]
and not as model parameters; that is the points
\mathbf{Z}
are choosen simultaneously with the hyperparameters of the
GP
by maximising a lower bound on the log-marginal likelhood
\log p(Y)
, the so-called evidence lower bound (ELBO), given as

\displaystyle\log p(Y)\geq\mathbb{E}_{q(f_{\mathbf{x}})}[\log p(Y|f_{\mathbf{x}})]-\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(f_{\mathbf{Z}})\,\|\,p(f_{\mathbf{Z}})\right],

(2.15)

where
f_{\mathbf{x}}
and
f_{\mathbf{Z}}
denotes the
GP
evaluated at the data-points and the inducing locations. Typically,
q(f_{\mathbf{Z}})
is given a free-form multivariate Gaussian to be learnt from the data, and then extended to other indices of the
GP
by
prior conditional matching
, i.e.
q(f_{\mathbf{x}}|f_{\mathbf{Z}})=p(f_{\mathbf{x}}|f_{\mathbf{Z}})
. Initially applied to regression, this was extended to classification
[
34
,
115
]
.
Among its advantages are that it gives a nonparametric approximation to the true posterior, adding inducing points only improves the approximation, and any optimization method can be used to maximize the
ELBO
, most importantly, stochastic optimization; see
[
117
,
16
,
29
]
.

Inter-domain approaches

Another idea is to go beyond the original index set and place inducing points
\mathbf{Z}
in a different space
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
, that is, given a centered
GP

g=(g_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}
one augments the original index set
\mathcal{X}
by a set
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
to define a new
GP

(g_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
and then locates the inducing points in this bigger model.
This was suggested in
[
157
]
in the context of integral transforms, which was extended in
[
116
]
, and studied in more generality in
[
186
]
.
In general, it is not obvious how to find a useful augmentation set
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and define the covariance enlarged to
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
.

2.4.2
Inter-domain low-rank tensors

A feature space augmentation

Given any
GP
with a covariance function
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\langle\Phi(\mathbf{x}),\Phi(\mathbf{y})\rangle
where
\Phi
is explicitly known
1
1

1

Mercer’s Theorem guarantees the existence of
\Phi
, but not in a sufficiently explicit form.
, we propose that a natural augmentation candidate is the “feature space”
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\operatorname{span}\{\Phi(\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}\}
itself.
The covariance function
\mathsf{k}
of
g
can be simply extended to
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
by linearity,

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x})=\alpha\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\beta\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime\prime})

(2.16)

for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathbf{z}=\alpha\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\beta\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
; analogous for
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
with
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
.
For our
GP
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\operatorname{span}\{\Phi_{\tau}(\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})\}\subset\mathsf{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.17)

We can thus extend our signature covariance (
2.12
) to
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\cup\mathsf{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by (
2.16
).
This provides a flexible class of inducing point locations
\mathbf{Z}
by optimizing over elements of the tensor algebra
\mathbf{Z}\subset\mathsf{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We coin these inducing point locations as
inducing tensors
.

Consistency of augmentation

A subtle point about augmenting the index set is that maximizing the
ELBO
in (
2.15
) is not necessarily equivalent anymore to minimizing a rigorously defined KL divergence between the true posterior process and its approximation over the unaugmented index set. In
[
186
]
, a sufficient condition given for this to hold is that the prior
GP
evaluated at the newly added indices is deterministic conditioned on the original
GP
.
In the case of (
2.16
), this is easily seen to be true, since the augmented indices arise as linear combinations of (the lifts of) elements in the original index set.
Therefore, the corresponding
GP
evaluations arise as linear combinations of evaluations of the original process by the fact that the (pre-)feature space
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is a
representation
of the
Hilbert space generated by the process

[
21
, Sec. 3]
.

Representation of inducing tensors

We define our inducing tensors as in the low-rank format, specifically as rank-
1
tensors such each inducing point is represented as

\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}_{m})_{m=0,\ldots,M}\in\prod_{m=0}^{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes m},

where
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{z}_{m}=\mathbf{v}_{m,1}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m,m}
for
{m\geq 1}
.
We remark that this construction does not generally give tensors that can be signatures of paths.
However, they can be represented as linear combinations of signatures, since path signatures span the whole tensor algebra, hence the previous argument about the augmentation carries over. Also, informally, what gives the data-efficiency of inducing tensors is exactly that they are not represented in a basis of signatures.

By linearity of integration and the inner product, the inducing point covariance equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f_{\mathbf{z}}f_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}]=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sigma_{m}^{2}\langle\mathbf{z}_{m},\mathbf{z}_{m}^{\prime}\rangle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sigma_{m}^{2}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\langle\mathbf{v}_{m,k},\mathbf{v}_{m,k}^{\prime}\rangle,

(2.18)

and the cross-covariance between paths and inducing tensors equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\mathbf{z}}]=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sigma_{m}^{2}\langle\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x}),z_{m}\rangle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\int_{\mathbf{t}\in\Delta_{m}([0,{\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}}])}\langle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}},\mathbf{v}_{m,1}\rangle\cdots\langle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{t_{m}},\mathbf{v}_{m,m}\rangle,

(2.19)

while when working with discrete-time data, using the order-
1
signature (
2.14
) it equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\mathbf{z}}]=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\langle\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{v}_{m,1}\rangle\cdots\langle\delta\mathbf{x}_{{i_{m}}},\mathbf{v}_{m,m}\rangle.

(2.20)

Below we use the approximation (
2.20
) since it makes the recursive algorithms simpler but note that a simple modification exactly computes (
2.20
) for a marginal computational overhead.

2.4.3
Algorithms.

We need to compute the three covariance matrices:

(1)

K_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}
of inducing tensors
\mathbf{Z}
and inducing tensors
\mathbf{Z}
,

(2)

K_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}
of inducing tensors
\mathbf{Z}
and sequences
\mathbf{X}
,

(3)

K_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}
of sequences
\mathbf{X}
and sequences
\mathbf{X}
.

Using the above tensor representations allows to give vectorized algorithms for
(1)
and
(2)
in Algorithms
3
and
4
, respectively.
For
(3)
we use a variation of Algorithm
2
which can be found in
[
266
, App. D.2]
. We use notation defined in Section
1.3.1
. For
v,v^{\prime}\in V
,
d
denotes the time to compute
\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle
,
c
the memory requirement of
v
.

Algorithm 3

Computing the inducing covariances
K_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}

1:

Input:
Tensors
\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{z}_{i})_{i=1,\dots,n_{\mathbf{Z}}}\subset\prod_{n=0}^{m}V^{\otimes n}
, scalars
(\sigma^{2}_{0},\sigma^{2}_{1},\dots,\sigma^{2}_{m})
, depth
m\in\mathbb{N}

2:

Compute
K[i,j,n,k]\leftarrow\langle v^{i}_{n,k},v^{j}_{n,k}\rangle
for
i,j\in[n_{\mathbf{Z}}]
,
n\in[m]
and
k\in[n]

3:

Initialize
R[i,j]\leftarrow\sigma_{0}^{2}
for
i,j\in[n_{\mathbf{Z}}]

4:

for

n=1

to

m

do

5:

Assign
A\leftarrow K[:,:,n,1]

6:

for

k=2

to

n

do

7:

Iterate
A\leftarrow K[:,:,n,k]\odot A

8:

end

for

9:

Update
R\leftarrow R+\sigma_{n}^{2}\cdot A

10:

end

for

11:

Output:
Matrix of inducing covariances
K_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}\leftarrow R

Algorithm 4

Computing the cross-covariances
K_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}

1:

Input:
Tensors
\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{z}_{i})_{i=1,\dots,n_{\mathbf{Z}}}\subset\prod_{n=0}^{m}V^{\otimes n}
, sequences
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1,\dots,n_{\mathbf{X}}}\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, scalars
(\sigma^{2}_{0},\sigma^{2}_{1},\dots,\sigma^{2}_{m})
, depth
M\in\mathbb{N}

2:

Compute
K[i,j,l,m,k]\leftarrow\langle\mathbf{v}^{i}_{m,k},\delta\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{l}}\rangle
for
i\in[n_{\mathbf{Z}}]
,
j\in[n_{\mathbf{X}}]
,
l\in[L_{\mathbf{X}}]
,
m\in[M]
and
k\in[m]

3:

Initialize
R[i,j]\leftarrow\sigma_{0}^{2}
for
i\in[n_{\mathbf{Z}}]
,
j\in[n_{\mathbf{X}}]

4:

for

m=1

to

M

do

5:

Assign
A\leftarrow K[:,:,n,1]

6:

for

k=2

to

m

do

7:

Iterate
A\leftarrow K[:,:,:,m,k]\odot A[:,:,\boxplus+1]

8:

end

for

9:

Update
R\leftarrow R+\sigma_{m}^{2}\cdot A[:,:,\Sigma]

10:

end

for

11:

Output:
Matrix of cross-covariances
K_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\leftarrow R

Proposition 2.2
.

Algorithm
3
computes the covariance matrix
K_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}
of
n_{\mathbf{Z}}
inducing points in
O(M^{2}\cdot n_{\mathbf{Z}}^{2}\cdot d)
steps.
Algorithm
4
computes the cross-covariance matrix
K_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}
in
O(M^{2}\cdot n_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot n_{\mathbf{Z}}\cdot{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot d)
steps.
Additionally to storing the inducing tensors
\mathbf{Z}
, Algorithm
3
requires
O(M^{2}\cdot n_{\mathbf{Z}}^{2})
memory, Algorithm
4
requires
O(M^{2}\cdot n_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot n_{\mathbf{Z}}\cdot{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}})
memory.

Proposition
2.2
follows details the scalability and we emphasize:

(i)

Both algorithms are linear in the maximal sequence length
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}}
.

(ii)

M
is a hyperparameter, and in all our experiments we learnt from the data
M\leq 5
, thus the quadratic complexity in
M
is negligible.

(iii)

The memory cost of inducing tensors
\mathbf{Z}
is much less than for the data
\mathbf{X}
, which is stored in
O(n_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot d)
memory, which is important because the inducing tensors are variational parameters, and not amenable to subsampling, while the learning inputs can be subsampled as noted by
[
117
]
.
Especially for
GP
Us memory cost is decisive and such savings are very important.

The computation of
K_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}
detailed in Appendix
[
266
, App. D.2]
has time complexity
O((M+d)\cdot n_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}\cdot{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}}^{2})
and memory of
O(d\cdot n_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}}+n_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}\cdot{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{X}}}^{2})
. However, given a factorizing likelihood, one only requires
K_{\mathbf{X}}=[k(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x})]_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}}
, which eliminates the quadratic cost in
n_{\mathbf{X}}
.
It turns out that this is enough to train on
GP
Us with reasonable minibatch sizes (e.g.
n_{\mathbf{X}}=50
) on several real world datasets. Finally, note that the
ELBO
(
2.15
) requires an additional matrix inversion and multiplication in
O(n_{\mathbf{Z}}^{2}\cdot n_{\mathbf{X}}+n_{\mathbf{Z}}^{3})
time, which is not significant in our case.

2.5
Experiments

TS classification

Using
GPFlow

[
64
]
,
Keras

[
46
]
, we implemented three models:
GPSig
,
GPSigLSTM
, and
GPSigGRU
.
All three use the signature covariance with the low-rank inducing tensors of Section
2.4
.
Note that in place of the signature kernel with the Euclidean lift (
2.14
), we precompose the signature kernel with the
RBF
kernel as detailed in Section
1.2.4
and
1.2.6
, so that it has greater flexibility. All computations carry on mutatis mutandis by replacing inner product evaluations with the
RBF
kernel. Regarding the implemented models,
GPSig
is a plain vanilla variational
GP
classifier.
Previous applications of neural nets to covariance constructions, in particular
[
290
,
5
]
, inspired
GPSigLSTM
and
GPSigGRU
that include an
RNN
as a sequence-to-sequence transformation
with
h
hidden units; see Figures
2.2
and
2.3
where
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
denotes augmentation with lags and
\mathsf{k}_{\hat{\mathbf{x}}}
a static kernel lift. We benchmarked these
GP
models on
16
multivariate
TS
classification datasets, a collection introduced in
[
17
]
that was a semi-standard archive at the time of working on this project in
TS
classification, e.g. see the citations in
[
266
, App. E.4]
for papers that use these datasets. The same datasets are also used in
[
128
]
to compare several deep learning architectures for
TSC
.

As Bayesian baselines we used three
GP
models:

(i)

GPLSTM
and

(ii)

GPGRU
consist of an
LSTM
and a
GRU
network with an
RBF
kernel on top, in which case the
RNN
s are used as a sequence-to-vector transformation from
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
\mathbb{R}^{h}
;

(iii)

GPKConv1D
uses the convolutional kernel introduced in
[
273
]
in
1
-dimension (time)

. Throughout we used sparse variational inference: for
GPSigLSTM
,
GPSigGRU
,
GPSig
, the inducing tensors detailed in Section
2.4
are used; for
GPLSTM
and
GPGRU
the inducing points are located in the output space of the
RNN
layer,
\mathbb{R}^{h}
; for
GPKConv1D
, the inducing patches of
[
273
]
are used.

We used
n_{\mathbf{Z}}=500
for all models; further all use a static kernel in one form or another, which we fixed to be the
RBF
kernel.
The signature kernel was truncated
2
2

2

For these experiments, the
M=4
value seemed to give an optimal trade-off between computational complexity and expressiveness of the kernel, see
[
266
, App. E.1]
for more details.
at
M=4
, and for
GPSig

p=1
lags were used; the
GPSigRNN
s did not use lags, as the sequence of hidden states already incorporate lagged information about past observations. The window size in
GPKConv1D
was set to
w=10
.
The
RNN
-architectures were selected independently for all models by grid-search among
6
variants, that is, the number of hidden units from
\{8,32,128\}
and with or without dropout. For training, early stopping was used with
n=500
epochs patience; a learning rate of
\alpha=1\times 10^{-3}
; a minibatch size of
50
; as optimizer Adam
[
143
]
and Nadam
[
72
]
were employed. Implementations, datasets, and the training and grid-search methodology are respectively detailed in Appendices E.1, E.2, and E.3 in
[
266
]
.

Discussion of results

For
GP
s, we report accuracies and negative log-predictive probabilities (
NLPP
), the latter take not only accuracies, but the calibration of probabilities into account as well.
Table
2.2
shows the average ranks among the
GP
s.
The full table of
NLPP
s and accuracies with mean and standard deviation over 5 model trains are reported in Appendix
2.B
in Table
2.4
and Table
2.5
.
As non-Bayesian baselines, we report accuracies of eight frequentist
TS
classifiers in Table
2.6
. On Figure
2.1
, we visualize the box-plot distributions of

(i)

negative log-predictive probabilities of
GP
s,

(ii)

accuracies of both
GP
s and frequentist methods.

The signature models perform consistently the best in terms of average rankings of both
NLPP
and accuracy among the
GP
s. Particularly, they achieve stronger mean performance and a smaller variance across datasets. To explain this, inspecting the results in Tables
2.4
,
2.5
, we observe that all other
GP
baselines perform very poorly on some datasets, while the signature based models perform at least moderately well on all datasets. We believe this ties in to the universality property of signatures, see Section
1.2.2
. The convolutional
GP
,
GPKConv1D
, which also has a very small parameter set, performed rather competitively with the deep kernel baselines, even on larger datasets. Comparison among variants of
GPSig
can be summarized as follows: for smaller datasets (
n_{\mathbf{X}}<300
),
GPSig
outperforms other variants as it has a very small parameter set; for larger datasets
(n_{\mathbf{X}}\geq 300)
,
GPSigLSTM
performs best which conforms with the intuition that
RNN
s suffer from small sample sizes.
A related observation is that
GPLSTM
and
GPGRU
perform about on par, while
GPSigLSTM
does much better than
GPSigGRU
, which suggests that the signature makes explicit use of the additional complexity of the
LSTM
network.

Compared only in terms of accuracy,
GPSig
competes with frequentist classifiers: it outperforms the usual
DTW
baseline and competes with state-of-the art classifiers such as
MUSE
and
MLSTMFCN
. Purely based on accuracy, these win overall, but the difference is usually small, hence the extra Bayesian advantages come at a small cost. Obviously
TS
classification is a vast field and many other models could be considered; e.g. we did not use recurrent
GP
s or
GPSSM
s since

(1)

they have so-far not been used for
TS
classification, possibly because there is no sequential nature in the output space,

(2)

we did not find a
GPFlow
implementation that would allow to use sequence kernels in the
GP
transition function. (Implementation of
[
127
]
does currently not allow taking subsequences of past states in the transition function.
An implementation would require much further work, but an interesting project would be to combine our models.)

) across data domains with the 1

Domain analysis

Next, we provide a breakdown of the performance of the various
GP
models across data domains. The datasets can be categorized into 4 different domains: handwriting, motion, sensor, and speech. The average ranks of
NLPP
s of each
GP
model for each data domain is provided in Table
2.3
. We observe the following points:

(1)

on handwriting datasets, the vanilla
GPSig
model provides the best performance with
GPSigLSTM
in second and
GPKConv1D
in third while
GPSigGRU
underperforms;

(2)

on motion datasets, the additional flexibility of the preprocessing
LSTM
layer shines and
GPSigLSTM
takes first place while
GPSig
second and
GPSigGRU
third;

(3)

on sensor datasets again
GPSig
performs the best and
GPSigLSTM
the second best while
GPSigGRU
severely underperforms;

(4)

somewhat surprisingly, on speech datasets the
GRU
architecture provides the best performance, and
GPSigGRU
takes first place while
GPGRU
second and
GPKConv1D
third while the other models underperform.

Overall, we conclude that the vanilla
GPSig
model often provides sufficient performance, while augmenting it with a preprocessing
LSTM
layer can often provide further improvements. However, in some cases, i.e. as seen on speech data here, the inductive bias of the
GRU
layer is needed, and the signature kernel can only perform well when the data has been first fed through one such layer.

Inducing tensors vs inducing sequences

Our results rely on the inter-domain approach using tensors to locate inducing points from Section
2.4
. An alternative is to use sequences for the inducing points,
\mathbf{Z}\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, and controlling their maximal length
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{Z}}}=\max_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{Z}}{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{z}}}
to be of order
m
, i.e.
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{Z}}}\sim m
.
We coin this approach
inducing sequences
.
Intuitively, one expects the inducing tensors to be more efficient than inducing sequences, since they make full use of the structure of the signature feature space/covariance.
To test this intuition, we compared the performance of the inducing tensors and inducing sequences subject to both having the same computational complexitiy.
For this experiment, we took the AUSLAN dataset
[
73
]
, which consists of
n_{c}=95
classes for
n_{\mathbf{X}}=1140
training examples.
This is a challenging dataset as the inducing variables need to characterize the abundance of classification boundaries.

We used
GPSig
with the same settings as in the previous experiments.
The hyperparameters of the kernel were a-priori learnt with
n_{\mathbf{Z}}=500
inducing tensors, and we purely investigated how the quality of the approximation changes for both approaches by varying the number of inducing points
n_{\mathbf{Z}}
.
For each number of inducing variables, both approaches were trained independently
5
times for
300
epochs with random initialization of the inducing variables, for details
[
266
, App. E.3]
.
We plot on Figure
2.4
three metrics:

(1)

the achieved
ELBO
,

(2)

the achieved accuracy, and

(3)

NLPP
on the testing set

.
At
n_{\mathbf{Z}}=500
both approaches are close to saturation, but the inducing tensors consistently perform better.
We remark that in practice, an important aspect is also how well the kernel hyperparameters can be recovered, that we did not consider here, and is a tricky question for sparse variational inference in general
[
16
]
.
Although, intuition suggests that the closer the model to saturation is with respect to the inducing points, the more consistent should the optimization be with un-sparsified variational inference.

Figure 2.4
:
Achieved
ELBO
(top), accuracy (middle), mean
NLPP
(bottom) after
300
epochs of training the variational parameters with random initialization and frozen pre-learnt kernel hyperparameters. Solid is the mean over
5
independent runs, dashed is the
1
-std region.

Visualizing inducing tensors

To gain more intuition, we visualized the feature space for one of the trained models on AUSLAN with
n_{\mathbf{Z}}=500
inducing tensors.
We used
UMAP

[
188
]
with the semi-metric
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\sqrt{\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x})+\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y})-2\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}
for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
, see Figure
2.5
.
There are two imminent observations:

(i)

in the point cloud corresponding to the data, the classes hardly look linearly separable;

(ii)

the tensors, however, seem to live on a completely separate subspace than the data.

The algorithm achieves
92\%
accuracy on this set, therefore, point
(i)
is likely due to information being lost in the projection.
However, point
(ii)
challenges the intuition about classical sparse variational inference, that the inducing points are located mixed-in with the data-points, concentrating close to the classification boundaries
[
115
]
.
In general, the mechanism of how inter-domain inducing points represent the information in the data seems to be more complicated than classically.

To explain point
(ii)
, we remark that this phenomenon is not surprising at all: signature features live in a manifold that is embedded in the linear tensor space
\mathsf{T}({\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}})
.
In general, sparse tensors of the form (
2.4.2
) will not be signatures of paths.
We believe variational inference works because of an interplay of two factors:
Firstly, signatures of finite sequences can be written as finite linear combinations of such sparse tensors.
Secondly, the prior conditional term used to define
q(f_{\mathbf{x}}|f_{\mathbf{Z}}))=p(f_{\mathbf{x}}|f_{\mathbf{Z}})
.
The feature space is congruent to the prior
GP

[
21
]
, which means that for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, the value of
f_{\mathbf{x}}
given
f_{\mathbf{Z}}
is not only almost deterministic when
\mathbf{x}
is close to any of
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{Z}
, but when it is close to any linear combinations of elements in
\mathbf{Z}
.
By the first remark this can always be achieved given a large enough
n_{\mathbf{Z}}
.
To sum up, the inducing tensors do not represent signature features individually, but form atomic building blocks such that their linear combinations induce the actual variational posterior at the data-examples.

Figure 2.5
:
A
UMAP
visualization of the features corresponding to data-points (coloured), and inducing tensors (black) in the feature space on the AUSLAN dataset.

2.6
Conclusion

We used a classical object from stochastic analysis – signatures – to define a
GP
for sequential data.
The
GP
inherits many of the theoretical guarantees that are known for signature features such as universality and parameterization invariance.
To make it scalable, we develop “inducing tensors” that exploit the structure of the feature space, inter-domain inducing points, and variational inference.
Applied in a plain vanilla variational framework, this yields a classifier,
GPSig
, that is not only competitive in terms of
NLPP
with other
GP
models, but also with state-of-the-art frequentist
TS
classifiers in terms of accuracy alone. As one of our reviewers remarked, several datasets we consider have a strong signal-to-noise ratio, which makes it worthwhile to point out that even for such datasets, the alternative
GP
baselines suffer on at least some of them, while the proposed models are consistently able to learn on all datasets. This observation ties in to the universality property, and it suggests that
GP
s with signatures can be a good starting point when building Bayesian models on time series datasets.

We also demonstrate that signatures can be used as a building block in deep kernels to build larger
GP
models that leverage the benefits of both,
RNN
s and signatures. Interestingly, we find that the vanilla
GPSig
model outperforms the
GPSigRNN
s for smaller datasets, conforming to the intuition that smaller sample sizes are detrimental for recurrent neural nets. To really get the best of both worlds, one could insert an additional model selection step, that specifies whether a parametric transformation is layer used before feeding the input into the kernel or not. Alternatively, it could also be possible to increase the flexibility of the sequential
GP
model while staying within a purely nonparametric framework using deep
GP
s
[
61
]
by e.g. applying a
GP
layer as observation-wise state-space embedding before the kernel computation. The inference framework of
[
226
]
for deep
GP
s could also come in handy when moving to datasets with lower signal-to-noise ratios, which can require
GP
models capable of handling not only epistemic (reducible) uncertainty, but aleatoric (irreducible) uncertainty in the data. It would also be interesting to see if such sequence kernels can be used to improve recurrent
GP
models
[
187
,
127
]
by incorporating sequential information into the
GP
transition function, that could potentially allow for a more efficient latent state representation.

Appendix 2.A
Continuity of
GP
s with Signature Covariances

We specified a covariance function
\mathsf{k}
on the set
\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})
as inner product of the signature map.
This guarantees that
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\langle\Phi(\mathbf{x}),\Phi(\mathbf{y})\rangle
is a positive definite function, and from the general theory of stochastic processes by the Kolmogorov extension theorem
[
75
]
, the existence of a centered
GP

(f_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})}
such that
\mathbb{E}[f_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\mathbf{y}}]=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.
However, this does not guarantee that the sample paths
\mathbf{x}\mapsto f_{\mathbf{x}}
are continuous.
Seminal work of Dudley
[
74
]
showed that such regularity estimates can be derived by bounding the growth of the covering number of the index set of the
GP

f
under the semi-metric

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[|f_{\mathbf{x}}-f_{\mathbf{y}}|^{2}]}=\sqrt{\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x})-2\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y})}.

(2.21)

Already when when the index set is finite dimensional “nice” covariance functions can lead to discontinuous
GP
s, see e.g. Section 1.4. in
[
3
]
.
Our
GP
has as index set the space of bounded variation paths
\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})
, which is infinite-dimensional so some caution is needed.
However, as we show below we can cover this space by lattice paths and derive covering number estimates.

Theorem 2.3
.

For
V>0
and
\epsilon>0
denote with
N(\epsilon,V)
the covering number of the set

\displaystyle\mathsf{Paths}_{L}(\mathcal{X})=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})\,:\,\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\right\}

(2.22)

of bounded variation paths of length less or equal than
V
under the
d_{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}}
pseudo-metric.
Then

\displaystyle\log_{2}N(\epsilon,L)\leq 2(d+1)L\frac{\sqrt{M}}{\epsilon}

(2.23)

Proof.

By definition of the metric
d_{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}}

\displaystyle d_{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}}(x,y)

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{\langle\Phi(\mathbf{x})-\Phi(\mathbf{y}),\Phi(\mathbf{x})-\Phi(\mathbf{y})\rangle}

(2.24)

\displaystyle=\|\Phi(\mathbf{x})-\Phi(\mathbf{y})\|.

(2.25)

By definition
\Phi
and of the norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\prod_{m=0}^{M}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}
this reads

\displaystyle d_{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\|\int d\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}-\int d\mathbf{y}^{\otimes m}\|^{2}

(2.26)

\displaystyle\leq M\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\Delta_{m}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

(2.27)

where we denote
\Delta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\|\int d\mathbf{x}^{\otimes m}-\int d\mathbf{y}^{\otimes m}\|
.
Let
\mathsf{Lattice}_{{s},{L}}(\mathcal{X})\subset\mathsf{Paths}_{L}(\mathcal{X})
be the set of lattice paths starting at
0\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that take steps of size
s
and that are of total length at most
L
.
By the results in Section 4 of
[
181
]
, for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}_{L}(\mathcal{X})
and every
n\geq 1
there exists a
\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Lattice}_{{L2^{-n}},{L}}(\mathcal{X})
such that for every
m\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\frac{d}{2^{n-1}}\frac{4L^{m-1}}{(m-1)!}.

(2.28)

Since
\frac{L^{m-1}}{(m-1)!}\leq e^{L}-1
we can apply (
2.28
) with

n=n(\epsilon)=1-\log_{2}\frac{{\epsilon}}{d\sqrt{M}4(e^{L}-1)}
to get
\Delta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\epsilon
.
Hence, there exists a lattice path
\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Lattice}_{{L2^{-n(\epsilon)}},{L}}(\mathcal{X})
such that

\displaystyle d_{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\epsilon.

(2.29)

Further, the set
\mathsf{Paths}(\mathcal{X})
is finite and we can bound it by

\displaystyle|\mathsf{Lattice}_{{L2^{-n(\epsilon)}},{L}}(\mathcal{X})|

\displaystyle\leq(2^{d}+1)^{L2^{n(\epsilon)}}\leq 2^{(d+1)L2^{n(\epsilon)}}

(2.30)

\displaystyle=2^{2(d+1)L2^{n(\epsilon)-1}}=2^{2(d+1)L\frac{\sqrt{M}}{\epsilon}}

(2.31)

where the first inequality follows since a lattice path has at every step
2^{d}
directions to choose from and in addition can choose not to make a step.
The last equality follows from the definition of
n(\epsilon)
.
Since
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Paths}_{L}(\mathcal{X})
was chosen arbitrary it follows that
\mathsf{Paths}_{L}(\mathcal{X})
can be covered by
2^{2(d+1)L\frac{\sqrt{M}}{\epsilon}}
balls of radius
\epsilon
centered at lattice paths.
∎

Theorem
2.3
combined with Dudley’s celebrated entropy estimates gives regularity results for samples of our
GP
.
In fact, this even yields a modulus of continuity for our
GP
.

Theorem 2.4
.

There exists a centered
GP

(f_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}^{L}_{paths}}
that has a covariance
\mathbb{E}[f_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\mathbf{y}}]
the signature covariance function
k(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\langle\Phi(\mathbf{x}),\Phi(\mathbf{y})\rangle
.
Moreover, if we denote its modulus of continuity on
\mathsf{Paths}_{L}(\mathcal{X})
with

\displaystyle\omega(\delta)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Paths}_{L}(\mathcal{X})\\
d_{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})<\delta\end{subarray}}|f_{\mathbf{x}}-f_{\mathbf{y}}|

(2.32)

then it holds with probability one that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\omega(\delta)}{\sqrt{\delta}4\sqrt{(d+1)L\sqrt{M}}+c\delta\sqrt{\ln\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}}\leq 24

(2.33)

where
c>0
denotes a universal constant.

Proof.

The existence of a centered
GP

(\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}}
with covariance
k
follows from general results about Gaussian processes.
The existence of a continuous modification
(f_{\mathbf{x}})_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{paths,L}}
of follows from Dudley’s theorem if

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log_{2}N(\epsilon,L)}d\epsilon<\infty

(2.34)

but by Theorem
2.3
we have

\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log_{2}N(\epsilon,L)}d\epsilon\leq\sqrt{2(d+1)L\sqrt{M}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}d\epsilon<\infty.

Dudley’s results immediately yield a modulus of continuity in probability.
By standard arguments this can be strengthened to give an almost sure modulus of continuity.
Concretely, we use the formulation given in Theorem 2.7.1 in Chapter 5 of
[
138
]
which guarantees that

\limsup_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\omega(\delta)}{\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{N(\frac{\epsilon}{2},L)}d\epsilon+c\delta\sqrt{\ln\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}}\leq 24.

The bound (
2.33
) follows immediately since first term in the denominator equals

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{\log_{2}N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2},L\right)}d\epsilon

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2(d+1)L\sqrt{M}}2\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\delta}

(2.35)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\delta}4\sqrt{(d+1)L\sqrt{M}}.

(2.36)

∎

2.4
and Table
2.5

GP
models considered in Section
2.5
. For each method-dataset pair, 5 models were trained with initialization described in
[

, App. E.3]
. The variance of the results is therefore due to random initialization and minibatch randomness. The
RNN

[
266
, Tab. 4]
. As non-Bayesian baselines, we report the results of recent frequentist
TS
classification methods from the respective publications, that is,
[
59
,

,

,

,

,

,

]
. Particularly for
MLSTMFCN
, we report the same results as in
[

]
.

Chapter 3
Seq2Tens

3.1
Introduction

A central task of learning is to find representations of the underlying data that efficiently and faithfully capture their structure.
In the case of sequential data, one data point consists of a sequence of objects.
This is a rich and non-homogeneous class of data and includes classical uni- or multi-variate time series (sequences of scalars or vectors), video (sequences of images), and text (sequences of letters).
Particular challenges of sequential data are that each sequence entry can itself be a highly structured object and that data sets typically include sequences of different length which makes naive vectorization troublesome.

Contribution

Our main result is a generic method that takes a
static feature map
for a class of objects (e.g. a feature map for vectors, images, or letters) as input and turns this into a feature map for sequences of arbitrary length of such objects (e.g. a feature map for time series, video, or text).
We call this feature map for sequences
Seq2Tens
; among its attractive properties are that it

(i)

provides a structured description of sequences; generalizing classical methods for strings,

(ii)

comes with theoretical guarantees such as universality,

(iii)

can be turned into modular and flexible neural network (
NN
) layers.

The key ingredient to our approach is to embed the feature space of the static feature map into the tensor algebra, and then the product in this algebra is then used to “stitch together” the static features of the sequence entries in a structured way.

Outline

Section
3.2
formalizes the main ideas of
Seq2Tens
.
Section
3.3
shows how low-rank (
LR
) constructions combined with
seq2seq
transforms allows one to efficiently use this rich algebraic structure.
Section
3.4
applies the results of Sections
3.2
and
3.3
to build modular and scalable
NN
layers for sequential data.
Section
3.5
demonstrates the flexibility and modularity of this approach on both discriminative and generative benchmarks.
Section
3.6
makes connections with previous work and summarizes this article.
In the appendices we provide mathematical background, extensions, and detailed proofs for our theoretical results.

3.2
Capturing order by non-commutative multiplication

We denote the set of sequences of elements in a set
\mathcal{X}
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})=\{\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,L}:\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathcal{X},\,L\geq 1\}

(3.1)

where
L\geq 1
is some arbitrary length. Notice that in this chapter we use
1
-based indexing for sequences, and for a sequence
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
,
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}=L
.
Even if
\mathcal{X}
itself is a linear space, e.g.
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}
,
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
is never a linear space since there is no natural addition of two sequences of different length.

3.2.1
Seq2Tens
in a nutshell

Given any vector space
V
we may construct the tensor algebra
\mathsf{T}((V))
over
V
.
We describe the space
\mathsf{T}((V))
in detail in Section
1.1.2
, but as for now the only thing that is important is that
\mathsf{T}((V))
is also a vector space that includes
V
, and that it carries a non-commutative product, which is, in a precise sense, “the most general product” on
V
.

The main idea of
Seq2Tens
is that any “static feature map” for elements in
\mathcal{X}
,
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to V
, can be used to construct a new feature map
\Phi:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathsf{T}((V))
for sequences in
\mathcal{X}
by using the algebraic structure of
\mathsf{T}((V))
:
the non-commutative product on
\mathsf{T}((V))
makes it possible to “stitch together” the individual features
\phi(\mathbf{x}_{1}),\ldots,\phi(\mathbf{x}_{L})\in V\subset\mathsf{T}((V))
of the sequence
\mathbf{x}
in the larger space
\mathsf{T}((V))
by multiplication in
\mathsf{T}((V))
.
With this we may define the feature map
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
for a sequences
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as follows

(i)

lift the map
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to V
to a map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{T}((V))
,

(ii)

map
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathsf{Seq}(\mathsf{T}((V)))
by
(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\mapsto(\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{L}))
,

(iii)

map
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathsf{T}((V)))\to\mathsf{T}((V))
by multiplication
(\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{L}))\mapsto\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{1})\cdots\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{L})
.

In a more concise form, we define
\Phi
as

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathsf{T}((V)),\quad\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{L}\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{i})

(3.2)

where
\prod
denotes multiplication in
\mathsf{T}((V))
.
We refer to the resulting map
\Phi
as the
Seq2Tens
map, which stands short for
Seq
uences-
2
-
Tens
ors
.
Why is this construction a good idea?
First note, that step
(i)
is always possible since
V\subset\mathsf{T}((V))
and we discuss the simplest such lift before Theorem
3.1
as well as other choices in
[
263
, App. B]
.
Further, if
\phi
, respectively
\varphi
, provides a faithful representation of objects in
\mathcal{X}
, then there is no loss of information in step
(ii)
.
Finally, since step
(iii)
uses “the most general product” to multiply
\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{1})\cdots\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{L})
one expects that
\Phi(\mathbf{x})\in\mathsf{T}((V))
faithfully represents the sequence
\mathbf{x}
as an element of
\mathsf{T}((V))
.

Indeed in Theorem
3.1
below we show an even stronger statement, namely that if the static feature map
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to V
contains enough non-linearities so that non-linear functions from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
can be approximated as
linear functions
of the static feature map
\phi
, then the above construction extends this property to functions of sequences.
Put differently,
if
\phi
is a universal feature map for
\mathcal{X}
, then
\Phi
is a universal feature map for
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
;
that is, any non-linear function
f(\mathbf{x})
of a sequence
\mathbf{x}
can be approximated as a linear functional of
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
,
f(\mathbf{x})\approx\langle\ell,\Phi(\mathbf{x})\rangle
.
We also emphasize that the domain of
\Phi
is the space
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
of sequences of
arbitrary
(finite) length.
The remainder of this Section gives details about steps
(i)
,
(ii)
,
(iii)
for the construction of
\Phi
.

3.2.2
Construction

Lifting static feature maps

Step
(i)
in the construction of
\Phi
requires turning a given feature map
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to V
into a map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{T}((V))
.
Throughout the rest of this article we use the lift

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathbf{x})=(1,\phi(\mathbf{x}),0,0\ldots)\in\mathsf{T}((V)).

(3.3)

We discuss other choices in
[
266
, App. B]
, but attractive properties of the lift
3.3
are that

(1)

the evaluation of
\Phi
against low-rank tensors becomes a simple recursive formula (Proposition
3.3
,)

(2)

it is a generalization of sequence sub-pattern matching as used in string kernels (
[
263
, App. B.3]
,

(3)

despite its simplicity it performs exceedingly well in practice (Section
3.4
).

Extending to sequences of arbitrary length

Steps
(i)
and
(ii)
in the construction specify how the map
\Phi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathsf{T}((V))
behaves on sequences of length-
1
, that is, single observations. Step
(iii)
amounts to the requirement that for any two sequences
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(V)
, their concatenation defined as
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}},\mathbf{y}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{y}_{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\in\mathsf{Seq}(V)
can be understood in the feature space as (non-commutative) multiplication of their corresponding features

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{z})=\Phi(\mathbf{x})\cdot\Phi(\mathbf{y}).

(3.4)

In other words, we inductively extend the lift
\varphi
to sequences of arbitrary length by starting from sequences consisting of a single observation, which is given in (
3.2
).
Repeatedly applying the definition of the tensor convolution product in (
1.8
) leads to the following explicit formula

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}\in V^{\otimes m},\hskip 5.0pt\Phi(\mathbf{x})=(\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x}))_{m\geq 0},

(3.5)

where
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\in\mathsf{Seq}(V)
and the summation is over non-contiguous subsequences.

Some intuition: generalized pattern matching

Our derivation of the feature map
\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\in\mathsf{T}((V))
was guided by general algebraic principles, but (
3.5
) provides an intuitive interpretation.
It shows that for each
m\geq 1
, the entry
\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x})\in V^{\otimes m}
constructs a summary of a long sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(V)
based on subsequences
(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}})
of
\mathbf{x}
of length-
m
.
It does this by taking the usual tensor product (see Section
1.1
)
\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}\in V^{\otimes m}
and summing over all possible non-contiguous subsequences.
This is completely analogous to how string kernels provide a structured description of text by looking at non-contiguous substrings of length-
m
(indeed, Appendix
[
263
, App. B.3]
makes this rigorous).
However, the main difference is that the above construction works for arbitrary sequences and not just sequences of discrete letters.

The algebraic background allows to prove that
\Phi
is universal, see Theorem
3.1
below.

Universality

A function
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to V
is said to be
universal for
\mathcal{X}
if all continuous functions on
\mathcal{X}
can be approximated as linear functions on the image of
\phi
.
One of the most powerful features of
NN
s is their universality
[
124
]
.
A very attractive property of
\Phi
is that it preserves universality: if
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to V
is universal for
\mathcal{X}
, then
\Phi:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathsf{T}((V))
is universal for
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
.
To make this precise, note that
V^{\otimes m}
is a linear space and therefore any
\ell=(\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{M},0,0,\ldots)\in\mathsf{T}((V))
consisting of
M
tensors
\ell_{m}\in V^{\otimes m}
, yields a linear functional on
\mathsf{T}((V))
; e.g. if
V=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and we identify
\ell_{m}
in coordinates as
\ell_{m}=(\ell_{m}^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}}
then

\displaystyle\langle\ell,\mathbf{t}\rangle:=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\langle\ell_{m},\mathbf{t}_{m}\rangle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}}\ell^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}}_{m}\mathbf{t}_{m}^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}}.

(3.6)

Thus linear functionals of the feature map
\Phi
, are real-valued functions of sequences.
Theorem
3.1
below shows that any continuous function
f:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
can by arbitrary well approximated by a
\ell\in\mathsf{T}((V))
,
f(\mathbf{x})\approx\langle\ell,\Phi(\mathbf{x})\rangle
.

Theorem 3.1

(Universality)
.

Let
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to V
be a universal map with a lift that satisfies some mild constraints, then the following map is universal:

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathrm{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathsf{T}((V)),\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\Phi(\mathbf{x}).

(3.7)

A detailed proof and the precise statement of Theorem
3.1
is given in
[
263
, App. B]
.

3.3
Approximation by low-rank linear functionals

The combinatorial explosion of tensor coordinates and what to do about it

The universality of
\Phi
suggests the following approach to represent a function
f:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
of sequences: First compute
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
and then optimize over
\ell
(and possibly also the hyperparameters of
\phi
) such that
f(\mathbf{x})\approx\langle\ell,\Phi(\mathbf{x})\rangle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\langle\ell_{m},\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x})\rangle
.
Unfortunately, tensors suffer from a combinatorial explosion in complexity, i.e. even just storing
\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x})\in V^{\otimes m}\subset\mathsf{T}((V))
requires
O(\operatorname{dim}(V)^{m})
real numbers.
Below we resolve this computational bottleneck as follows: in Proposition
3.3
we show that for a special class of low-rank elements
\ell\in\mathsf{T}((V))
, the functional
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\langle\ell,\Phi(\mathbf{x})\rangle
can be efficiently computed in both time and memory.
This is somewhat analogous to a kernel trick since it shows that
\langle\ell,\Phi(\mathbf{x})\rangle
can be cheaply computed without explicitly computing the feature map
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
.
However, Theorem
3.1
guarantees universality under no restriction on
\ell
, thus restriction to rank-
1
functionals limits the class of functions
f(\mathbf{x})
that can be approximated.
Nevertheless, by iterating these “low-rank functional” constructions in the form of
seq2seq
transformations this can be ameliorated.
We give the details below but to gain intuition, we invite the reader to think of this iteration analogous to stacking layers in a neural network: each layer is a relatively simple non-linearity (e.g. a sigmoid composed with an affine function) but by composing such layers, complicated functions can be efficiently approximated.

Rank-1 functionals are computationally cheap

Degree
m=2
tensors are matrices and low-rank approximations are widely used in practice
[
270
]
to address quadratic complexity.
The definition below generalizes the rank of matrices to tensors of any degree
m
.

Definition 3.2
.

The
rank
(also called
CP
rank

[
31
]
) of a degree-
m
tensor
\mathbf{t}_{m}\in V^{\otimes m}
is the smallest number
r\geq 0
such that one may write

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}_{m}=\sum_{i=0}^{r}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{i}^{m},\quad\mathbf{v}_{i}^{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{i}^{m}\in V.

(3.8)

We say that
\mathbf{t}=(\mathbf{t}_{m})_{m\geq 0}\in\mathsf{T}((V))
has rank-
1
(and truncation-
M
) if each
\mathbf{t}_{m}\in V^{\otimes m}
is a rank-
1
tensor and
\mathbf{t}_{i}=0
for
i>M
.

The ranks of signature tensors related are investigated in
[
90
]
.
A direct calculation shows that if
\ell
is of rank-
1
, then
\langle\ell,\Phi(\mathbf{x})\rangle
can be computed efficiently by inner product evaluations.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\ell=(\ell_{m})_{m\geq 0}\in\mathsf{T}((V))
be of rank-
1
and truncation-
M
. Then,

\displaystyle\langle\ell,\Phi(\mathbf{x})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\langle\mathbf{v}_{k}^{m},\phi(\mathbf{x}_{i_{k}})\rangle

(3.9)

where
\ell_{m}=\mathbf{v}_{1}^{m}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}^{m}\in V^{\otimes m}
,
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{m}\in V
and
m=0,\ldots,M
.

Note that the inner sum is taken over all non-contiguous subsequences of
\mathbf{x}
of length-
m
, analogously to
m
-mers of strings; the proof of Proposition
3.3
is given in
[
263
, App. B]
. While (
3.9
) looks expensive, by casting it into a recursive formulation over time, it can be computed in
O(M^{2}\cdot{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}\cdot d)
time and
O(M^{2}\cdot({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}+c))
memory, where
d
is the inner product evaluation time on
V
, while
c
is the memory footprint of a
v\in V
. This can further be reduced to
O(M\cdot{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}\cdot d)
time and
O(M\cdot({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}+c))
memory by an efficient parameterization of the rank-
1
element
\ell\in\mathsf{T}((V))
. We give further details and numerical scalability analysis in Appendix
3.A
.

Low-rank
Seq2Tens
maps

The composition of a linear map
\mathcal{L}:\mathsf{T}((V))\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
\Phi
can be computed cheaply in parallel using (
3.9
) when
\mathcal{L}
is specified through a collection of
N\in\mathbb{N}
rank-
1
elements
\ell^{1},\ldots,\ell^{N}\in\mathsf{T}((V))
such that

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L}):=\mathcal{L}\circ\Phi(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{L})=(\langle\ell^{j},\Phi(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{L}))_{j=1}^{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

(3.10)

We call the resulting map
\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
a
L
ow-rank
S
eq
2T
ens
map of width-
N
and truncation-
M
, where
M\in\mathbb{N}
is the maximal degree of
\ell^{1},\dots,\ell^{N}
such that
\ell^{j}_{i}=0
for
i>M
. The
LS2T
map is parametrized by

(1)

the component vectors
\mathbf{v}_{j,m}^{k}\in V
of the rank-
1
elements
\ell^{j}_{m}=\mathbf{v}_{j,m}^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{j,m}^{m}
,

(2)

by any parameters
\theta
that the static feature map
\phi_{\theta}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow V
. We denote these parameters by
\tilde{\theta}=(\theta,\ell^{1},\dots,\ell^{N})

. In addition, by the subsequent composition of
\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}
with a linear functional
\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we get the following function subspace as hypothesis class

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{H}}=\big\{\langle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha_{j}\ell^{j},\Phi(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\rangle\,:\,\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\big\}\subsetneq\mathcal{H}=\big\{\langle\ell,\Phi(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\,:\,\ell\in\mathsf{T}((V))\big\}

(3.11)

Hence, we acquire an intuitive explanation of the (hyper)parameters: the width of the
LS2T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
specifies the maximal rank of the low-rank linear functionals of
\Phi
that the
LS2T
can represent, while the span of the rank-
1
elements,
\texttt{span}(\ell^{1},\dots,\ell^{N})
determine an
N
-dimensional subspace of the dual space of
\mathsf{T}((V))
consisting of at most rank-
N
functionals.

Recall now that without rank restrictions on the linear functionals of
Seq2Tens
features, Theorem
3.1
would guarantee that any real-valued function
f:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
could be approximated by
f(\mathbf{x})\approx\langle\ell,\Phi(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\rangle
. As pointed out before, the restriction of the hypothesis class to low-rank linear functionals of
\Phi(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L}
) would limit the class of functions of sequences that can be approximated. To ameliorate this, we use
LS2T
transforms in a
seq2seq
fashion that allows us to stack such low-rank functionals, significantly recovering expressiveness.

Sequence-to-sequence transforms

Motivated by the empirical successes of stacked
RNN
s
[
105
,
104
,
254
]
, we build sequence-to-sequence (
seq2seq
) transformations.
We can use
LS2T
to build
seq2seq
transformations in the following way: fix the static map
\phi_{\theta}:\mathcal{X}\to V
parametrized by
\theta
and rank-
1
elements such that
\tilde{\theta}=(\theta,\ell^{1},\dots,\ell^{N})
and apply the resulting
LS2T
map
\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\big(\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}(\mathbf{x}_{1}),\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}),\dots,\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\big).

(3.12)

Note that the cost of computing the expanding window
seq2seq
transform in (
3.12
) is no more expensive than computing
\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})
itself due to the recursive nature of our algorithms, for further details see Appendix
3.A
.

Deep
seq2seq
transforms

Inspired by the empirical successes of deep
RNN
s
[
105
,
104
,
254
]
, we iterate the transformation (
3.12
)
D
-times:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{N_{2}})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{N_{D}}).

(3.13)

Each of these mappings
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{N_{i}})\rightarrow\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{N_{i+1}})
is parametrized by the parameters
\tilde{\theta}_{i}
of a static feature map
\phi_{\theta_{i}}
and a linear map
\mathcal{L}_{i}
specified by
N_{i}
rank-
1
elements of
\mathsf{T}((V))
; these parameters are collectively denoted by
\tilde{\theta}_{i}=(\theta_{i},\ell_{i}^{1},\dots,\ell_{i}^{N_{i}})
.
Evaluating the final sequence in
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{N_{D}})
at the last observation-time
t=L
, we get the deep
LS2T
map with depth-
D

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\theta}_{D}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n_{D}}.

(3.14)

Making precise how the stacking of such low-rank
seq2seq
transformations approximates general functions requires more tools from algebra, and we provide a rigorous quantitative statement in
[
263
, App. C]
.
Here, we just appeal to the analogy made with adding depth in neural networks mentioned earlier and empirically validate this in our experiments in Section
3.4
.

3.4
Building neural networks with
LS2T
layers

The
Seq2Tens
map
\Phi
built from a static feature map
\phi
is universal if
\phi
is universal, Theorem
3.1
.
NNs form a flexible class of universal feature maps with strong empirical success for data in
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and thus make a natural choice for
\phi
.
Combined with standard deep learning constructions, the framework of Sections
3.2
and
3.3
can build modular and expressive layers for sequences.

Neural
LS2T
layers

The simplest choice among many is to use as static feature map
\phi:\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{h}
a feedforward network with depth-
P
,
\phi=\phi_{P}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{1}
where
\phi_{j}(\mathbf{x})=\sigma(\mathbf{W}_{j}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}_{j})
for
\mathbf{W}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{h\times d}
,
\mathbf{b}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{h}
.
We can then lift this to a map
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathsf{T}((\mathbb{R}^{h}))
as prescribed in (
3.3
).
Hence, the resulting
LS2T
layer
\mathbf{x}\mapsto(\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{i=1,\ldots,L}
is a
seq2seq
transform
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathsf{Seq}{\mathbb{R}^{h}}
that is parametrized by
\tilde{\theta}=(\mathbf{W}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{W}_{P},\mathbf{b}_{P},\ell_{1}^{1},\dots,\ell^{N_{1}}_{1})
.

Bidirectional
LS2T
layers

The transformation in (
3.12
) is completely causal in the sense that each step of the output sequence depends on the past.
For generative models, it can behove us to make the output depend on both past and future, see
[
104
,
14
,
168
]
.
Similarly to bidirectional
RNN
s
[
235
,
106
]
, we may achieve this by defining a bidirectional layer,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}^{\operatorname{b}}_{(\tilde{\theta}_{1},\tilde{\theta}_{2})}(\mathbf{x}):\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{N+N^{\prime}}),\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto(\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}_{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}),\tilde{\Phi}_{\tilde{\theta}_{2}}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{L}))_{i=1}^{L}.

(3.15)

The sequential nature is kept intact by making the distinction between what classifies as past (the first
N
coordinates) and future (the last
N^{\prime}
coordinates) information. This amounts to having a form of precognition in the model, and has been applied in e.g. dynamics generation
[
168
]
, machine translation
[
252
]
, and speech processing
[
104
]
.

Convolutions and
LS2T

We motivate to replace the time-distributed feedforward layers proposed in the paragraph above by temporal convolutions (CNN) instead. Although theory only requires the “preprocessing” layer to be a static feature map, we find that it is beneficial to capture some of the sequential information as well, e.g. using
CNN
s or
RNN
s. From a mathematical point of view,
CNN
s are a straightforward extension since they can be interpreted as time-distributed feedforward layers applied to the input sequence augmented with a
p\in\mathbb{N}
number of its lags for
CNN
kernel size
p
(see
[
263
, App. D.1]
for further discussion).

In the following, we precede our deep
LS2T
blocks by one or more
CNN
layers. Intuitively,
CNN
s and
LS2T
s are similar in that both transformations operate on subsequences of their input sequence. The main difference between the two lies in that
CNNs operate on contiguous subsequences
, and therefore, capture local, short-range nonlinear interactions between timesteps;
while
LS2T
s ((
3.9
)) use all non-contiguous subsequences
, and hence, learn global, long-range interactions in time. This observation motivates that the inductive biases of the two types of layers (local/global time-interactions) are highly complementary in nature, and we suggest that the improvement in the experiments on the models containing vanilla
CNN
blocks are due to this.

3.5
Experiments

We demonstrate the modularity and flexibility of the above
LS2T
and its variants by applying it to

(i)

multivariate time series classification,

(ii)

mortality prediction in healthcare,

(iii)

generative modelling of sequential data.

In all cases, we take a strong baseline model (
FCN
and
GP-VAE
, as detailed below) and upgrade it with
LS2T
layers. As Thm.
3.1
requires the
Seq2Tens
layers to be preceded by at least a static feature map, we expect these layers to perform best as an add-on on top of other models, which however can be quite simple, such as a
CNN
. The additional computation time is negligible (in fact, for
FCN
it allows to reduce the number of parameters significantly, while retaining performance), but it can yield improvements.
This is remarkable, since the original models are already state-of-the-art on well-established benchmarks.

3.5.1
Multivariate time series classification

As the first task, we consider multivariate time series classification (
TSC
) on an archive of benchmark datasets collected by
[
17
]
. Numerous previous publications report results on this archive, which makes it possible to compare against several well-performing competitor methods from the
TSC
community. These baselines are detailed in
[
263
, App. E.1]
. This archive was also considered in a recent popular survey paper on
DL
for
TSC

[
128
]
, from where we borrow the two best performing models as deep learning baselines:
FCN
and
ResNet
.
The
FCN
is a fully convolutional network which stacks 3 convolutional layers of kernel sizes
(8,5,3)
and filters
(128,256,128)
followed by a global average pooling (
GAP
) layer, hence employing global parameter sharing. We refer to this model as
128
. The
ResNet
is a residual network stacking 3
FCN
blocks of various widths with skip-connections in between
[
113
]
and a final
GAP
layer.

The
FCN
is an interesting model to upgrade with
LS2T
layers, since the
LS2T
also employs parameter sharing across the sequence length, and as noted previously, convolutions are only able to learn local interactions in time, that in particular makes them ill-suited to picking up on long-range interactions, which is exactly where the
LS2T
can provide improvements.
As our models, we consider three simple architectures:

(i)

\texttt{LS2T}_{64}^{3}
stacks
3

LS2T
layers of order-
2
and width-
64
;

(ii)

\texttt{FCN}_{64}\text{-}\texttt{LS2T}_{64}^{3}
precedes the
\texttt{LS2T}_{64}^{3}
block by an
64
block; a downsized version of
128
;

(iii)

\texttt{FCN}_{128}\text{-}\texttt{LS2T}_{64}^{3}
uses the full
128
and follows it by a
\texttt{LS2T}_{64}^{3}
block as before.

Also, both
FCN-LS2T
models employ skip-connections from the input to the
LS2T
block and from the
FCN
to the classification layer, allowing for the
LS2T
to directly see the input, and for the
FCN
to directly affect the final prediction. These hyperparameters were only subject to hand-tuning on a subset of the datasets, and the values we considered were
H,N\in\{32,64,128\}
,
M\in\{2,3,4\}
and
D\in\{1,2,3\}
, where
H,N\in\mathbb{N}
is the
FCN
and
LS2T
width, resp., while
M\in\mathbb{N}
is the
LS2T
order and
D\in\mathbb{N}
is the
LS2T
depth. We also employ techniques such as time-embeddings
[
174
]
, sequence differencing and batch normalization, see
[
263
, App. D.1]
;
[
263
, App. E.1]
for further details on the experiment and Figure 2 in there for a visualization of the architectures.

:

\{>\}
,
\{<\}
,
\{=\}
refer to the respective events that the row method is better, the column method is better, or that they are equivalent.

Results

The full table of results is available in Table
3.6
in Appendix
3.B
, where for the models that we trained ourselves, i.e.
128
,
ResNet
,
\texttt{LS2T}_{64}^{3}
,
\texttt{FCN}_{64}\text{-}\texttt{LS2T}_{64}^{3}
,
\texttt{FCN}_{128}\text{-}\texttt{LS2T}_{64}^{3}
, we report the mean and standard deviation of test set accuracies over 5 model trains. Figure
3.1
depicts the box-plot distributions of classification accuracies and the corresponding critical difference (
CD
) diagram. The
CD
diagram depicts the mean ranks of each method averaged over datasets with a calculated
CD
region using the Nemenyi test
[
198
]
for an alpha value of
\alpha=0.1
. For the Bayesian signed-rank test
[
20
]
, we used the implementation from
https://github.com/janezd/baycomp
, and the posterior probabilities are compared in Table
3.1
. The posterior distributions themselves are visualized on Figure 4
[
263
, App. E.1]
. The region of practical equivalence (
rope
) was set to
\texttt{rope}=1\times 10^{-3}
, that is, two accuracies were practically equivalent if they are at most the given distance from each other.

The results of the signed-rank test indicate that while
\texttt{LS2T}_{3}^{64}
is only better with high probability (
p\geq 0.8)
than 5 of the baselines,
\texttt{FCN}_{64}\text{-}\texttt{LS2T}_{3}^{64}
performs better than all except
\texttt{FCN}_{128}
and
ResNet
with high probability (
p\geq 0.8
), while
\texttt{FCN}_{128}\text{-}\texttt{LS2T}_{3}^{64}
performs better than
all baselines
with high probability (
p\geq 0.8
).
Note however that
\texttt{FCN}_{64}\text{-}\texttt{LS2T}_{3}^{64}
has fewer parameters than
\texttt{FCN}_{128}
by around
60\%
, see Table 6 in
[
263
, App. E.1]
, while providing comparable or slightly improved performance. In conclusion, we have verified that
\texttt{LS2T}_{3}^{64}
already performs well on some classification tasks, and by preceding it with an
FCN
, its performance is elevated to outperforming all baselines. This observation is not surprising, as Thm.
3.1
warrants, the strength of the
LS2T
layer shines when it is preceded by state-space nonlinearities. Hence, we expect
LS2T
s to find their best use as a modular building block being part of larger models. We also suggest the improvement on the vanilla
FCN
composed of
CNN
blocks is due to the
LS2T
enhancing the model’s ability to pick up long-range interactions, see the discussion at the end of Section
3.4
.
