Chapter 5
Random Fourier Signature Features

5.1
Introduction

Machine learning has successfully been applied to tasks that require learning from complex and structured data types on non-Euclidean domains. Feature engineering on such domains is often tackled by exploiting the geometric structure and symmetries existing within the data
[
25
]
.
Learning from sequential data (such as video, text, audio, time series, health data, etc.) is a classic, but an ongoing challenge due to the following properties:

•

Non-Euclidean data.
The data domain is nonlinear since there is no obvious and natural way of adding sequences of different length.

•

Time-space patterns.

Statistically significant patterns can be distributed over time and space, that is, capturing the order structure in which “events” arise is crucial.

•

Time-warping invariance.
The meaning of many sequences is often invariant to reparameterization also frequently called time-warping, at least to an extent; e.g. a sentence spoken quicker or slower contains (essentially) the same information.

•

Discretization and irregular sampling.

Sequences often arise by sampling along an irregularly spaced grid of an underlying continuous time process. A general methodology should be robust as the sampling gets finer, sequences approximate paths (continuous-time limit), or as the discretization grid varies between sequences.

•

Scalability.
Sequence datasets can quickly become massive, so the computational complexity should grow subquadratically, in terms of all of the state-space dimension, and the length and number of sequences.

The signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
is the state-of-the-art kernel for sequential data
[
266
,
228
,
165
]
that addresses the first 4 of the above questions and can rely on the modular and powerful framework of kernel learning
[
234
]
.
Its construction is motivated by classic ideas from stochastic analysis that give a structured description of a sequence by developing it into a series of tensors.
We refer to
[
163
]
for a recent overview of its various constructions and applications. In the real-world, various phenomena are well-modelled by systems of differential equations. The path signature arises naturally in the context of controlled differential equations. The role of the signature here is to provide a basis for the effects of a driving signal on systems of controlled differential equations. In essence, it captures the interactions of a controlling signal with a nonlinear system. This explains the widespread applicability of signatures to various problems across the sciences
[
182
]
. There is also geometric intuition behind signatures, see Section 1.2.4 in
[
42
]
.

Features vs Kernel/Primal vs Dual

Kernel learning circumvents the costly evaluation of a high- or infinite-dimensional feature map by replacing it with the computation of a Gram matrix which contains as entries the inner products of features between all pairs of data points.
This can be very powerful since the inner product evaluation can often be done cheaply by the celebrated "kernel trick", even for infinite-dimensional feature spaces, but the price is that now the computational cost is quadratic in the number of samples, and downstream algorithms further often incur a cubic cost usually in the form of a matrix inversion. On the other hand, when finite-dimensional features can used for learning, the primal formulation of a learning algorithm can perform training and inference in a cost that is linear with respect to the sample size assuming that the feature dimension is fixed. This motivates the investigation of finite-dimensional approximations to kernels that mimic their expressiveness at a lower computational cost. It is an interesting question how the feature dimension should scale with the dataset size to maintain a given (optimal) learning performance in downstream tasks,
which is investigated for instance by
[
219
,
30
,
251
,
170
,
246
,
156
]
.

Computational Cost of the Signature kernel

In the context of the signature kernel, one data point is itself a whole sequence.
Hence, given a data set
\mathbf{X}
consisting of
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
sequences where each sequence
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})
is of maximal length
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}\leq\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and has sequence entries
\mathbf{x}_{i}
in a state-space of dimension
d
, then the existing algorithms to evaluate the Gram matrix of the
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
scale quadratically, i.e. as
O(N^{2}\ell^{2}d)
, both in sequence length
\ell
and number of sequences
N
.
So far this has only been addressed by subsampling (either directly the sequence elements to reduce the length or by column subsampling via the Nyström approach
[
287
]
), which can lead to crude approximations and performance degradation on large-scale datasets.

Contribution

Random Fourier Features (
RFF
)
[
209
]
is a classic technique to enjoy both the benefits of the primal and dual approach.
Here, a low-dimensional and random feature map is constructed, which although does not approximate the feature map of a translation-invariant kernel, its inner product is with high probability close to the kernel itself.
The main contribution of this article is to carry out such a construction for the signature kernel.
Concretely, we construct a random feature map on the domain of sequences called Random Fourier Signature Features (
RFSF
), such that its inner product is a random kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}}
for sequences that is both

(i)

an unbiased estimator for
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
, and

(ii)

has analogous probabilistic approximation guarantees to the classic
RFF
kernel.

The challenge is that a direct application of the classic
RFF
technique is not feasible since this relies on Bochner’s theorem which does not apply since the sequence domain is not even a linear space and the feature domain is non-Abelian, which makes the use of (generalizations of
[
88
]
) Bochner’s theorem difficult due to the lack of sufficiently explicit representations.
We tackle this challenge by combining the algebraic structure of signatures with probabilistic concentration arguments; a careful analysis of the error propagation yields uniform concentration guarantees similar to the
RFF
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then, we introduce dimensionality reduction techniques for random tensors further approximating
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}}
to define the extremely scalable variants
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}}
called
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
saving considerable amounts of computation time and memory by low-dimensional projection of the feature set of the
RFSF
.

Hence, analogously to the classic
RFF
construction, the random kernels
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}},\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}},\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}}
simultaneously enjoy the expressivity of an infinite-dimensional feature space as well as linear complexity in sequence length.
This overcomes the arguably biggest drawback of the signature kernel, which is the quadratic complexity in sample size and sequence length; the price for reducing the complexities by an order is that this approximation only holds with high probability. As in the case of the classic
RFF
, our experiments show that this is in general a very attractive tradeoff. Concretely, we demonstrate in the experiments that the proposed random features

(1)

provide comparable performance on moderate sized datasets to full-rank (quadratic time) signature kernels,

(2)

outperform other random feature approaches for time series on both moderate- and large-scale datasets,

(3)

allow scaling to datasets of a million time series

.

Related Work

The signature kernel has found many applications; for example, it is used in ABC-Bayes
[
76
]
, economic scenario validation
[
8
]
, amortised likelihood estimation
[
77
]
, the analysis of RNNs
[
82
]
, analysis of trajectories in Lie groups
[
162
]
, metrics for generative modelling
[
27
,
141
]
, or dynamic analysis of topological structures
[
96
]
.
For a general overview see
[
163
]
.
All of these applications can benefit from a faster computation of the signature kernel with theoretical guarantees.
Previous approaches address the quadratic complexity of the signature kernel only by subsampling in one form or another:

[
146
]
combine a structured Nyström type-low-rank approximation to reduce complexity in dimension of samples and sequence length,
[
266
]
combine this with inducing point and variational methods,
[
228
]
uses sequence-subsampling,
[
165
]
use diagonal approximations to Gram matrices in a variational setting.
Related to this work is also the random nonlinear projections in
[
180
]
; further,
[
192
]
combine linear dimension projection in a general pipeline and
[
55
]
use signatures in reservoir computing.
Directly relevant for this work is recent progress on tensorized random projections
[
250
,
212
]
.
Random Fourier Features
[
209
,
211
]
are well-understood theoretically
[
253
,
244
,
246
,
172
,
10
,
255
,
35
,
271
,
35
]
.
In particular, its generalization properties are studied in e.g.
[
12
,
170
,
251
,
156
]
, where it is shown that the feature dimension need only scale sublinearly in the dataset size for supervised learning, and a similar result also holds for kernel principal component analysis
[
246
]
.
Several variations have been proposed over the years
[
159
,
81
,
49
,
300
,
48
,
47
,
50
]
, even finding applications in deep learning
[
256
]
. Alternative random feature approaches for polynomial and Gaussian kernels based on tensor sketching have been proposed in e.g.
[
281
,
280
,
279
]
. Gaussian sketching has also been applied in the RKHS for kernel approximation
[
152
]
. For a survey, the reader is referred to
[
35
,
173
]
.

Outline

Section
5.2
provides background on the prerequisites of our work: Random Fourier Features, and Signature Features/Kernels. Section
5.3
contains our proposed methods with theoretical results; it introduces Random Fourier Signature Features (
RFSF
)
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
,

RFSF
kernels
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
(where
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is the truncation level introduced later), and most importantly their theoretical guarantees. Theorem
5.3
quantifies the approximation
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\approx\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
uniformly. Then, we discuss additional variants: the
RFSF-DP
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
and the
RFSF-TRP
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
, which build on the previous construction using dimensionality reduction with corresponding concentration results in Theorems
5.6
and
5.9
. Section
5.4
compares the performance of the proposed scalable signature kernels against popular approaches on
SVM
multivariate time series classification, which demonstrates that the proposed kernel not only significantly improves the computational complexity of the signature kernel, it also provides comparable performance, and in some cases even improvements in accuracy as well. Hence, we take the best of both worlds: linear batch, sequence, and state-space dimension complexities, while approximately enjoying the expressivity of an infinite-dimensional
RKHS
with high probability.

5.2
Background

5.2.1
Notation

We denote the real numbers by
\mathbb{R}
, natural numbers by
\mathbb{N}=\left\{0,1,2,\dots\right\}
, positive integers by
\mathbb{Z}_{+}=\left\{1,2,3,\dots\right\}
, the range of positive integers from
1
to
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
by
\left[n\right]=\left\{1,2,\dots,n\right\}
. Given
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote their maximum by
a\vee b=\max(a,b)
and their minimum by
a\wedge b=\min(a,b)
. We define the collection of all ordered
m
-tuples with non-repeating entries by

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}(n)=\left\{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{m}\leq n\,:\,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in[n]\right\}.

(5.1)

In general,
\mathcal{X}
refers to a subset of the input domain, where the various objects are defined, generally taken to be a subset
\mathbb{R}^{d}
unless otherwise stated. For a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote its
\ell_{p}
norm by
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\lvert x_{i}\right\rvert^{p}\right)^{1/p}
. For a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times e}
, we denote the spectral and the Frobenius norm
by
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{2}=\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}=1}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}
and
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{F}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{e}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}
, where
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{e}\}
is the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{e}
. The transpose of a matrix
\mathbf{A}
is denoted by
\mathbf{A}^{\top}
. For a differentiable
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we denote its gradient at
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\nabla f(\mathbf{x})=\left(\nicefrac{{\partial f(\mathbf{x})}}{{\partial x_{i}}}\right)_{i=1}^{d}
, and its collection of partial derivatives with respect to
\mathbf{s}=(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}})
by
\partial_{\mathbf{s}}f(\mathbf{x})=\left(\nicefrac{{\partial f(\mathbf{x})}}{{\partial x_{i_{j}}}}\right)_{j=1}^{k}
.

\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
refers to sequences of finite, but unbounded length with values in the set
\mathcal{X}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})=\{\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\,:\,\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathcal{X},L\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}.

(5.2)

We denote the effective length of a sequence
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
by
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}=L
, and define the 1
st
-order forward differencing operator as
\delta\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{i+1}-\mathbf{x}_{i}
. We define the
1
-variation functional of a sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{{1\texttt{-var}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell_{\mathbf{x}}-1}\left\lVert\delta\mathbf{x}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}
as a measure of sequence complexity.

5.2.2
Signature Features for Sequential Data

A challenge in machine learning when constructing feature maps for datasets of sequences is that the sequence length can vary from instance to instance; the space of sequences
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})=\left\{(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{\ell}\,:\,\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{\ell}\in\mathcal{X}\hskip 3.0pt\text{and}\hskip 3.0pt\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right\}
includes sequences of various lengths, and they should all get mapped to the same feature space, while preserving the information about the ordering of elements. A concatenation property of path signatures called Chen’s identity
[
182
, Thm. 2.9]

turns concatentation into multiplication provides a principled approach to construct features for sequences. Below we recall the construction from Chapter
3
of the discrete-time order-
1
signature features originally introduced in Section
1.2.6
.

The key idea is to define the discrete-time signature of
1
-step increments, and then glue features together by algebra multiplication to guarantee the Chen identity holds by construction.
Now assume we are given a static feature map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
into some Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
. Our task is to construct from this feature map for elements of
\mathcal{X}
, a feature map for sequences of arbitrary length in
\mathcal{X}
.
A natural first step is to apply the feature map
\varphi
elementwise to a sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
to lift it into the feature space
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
of
\varphi
,
\varphi(\mathbf{x})=\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{i})\right)_{i=0}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\in\mathsf{Seq}({\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}})
. The challenge is now to construct a feature map for sequences in
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
.
Simple aggregation of the individual features fails; e.g. summation of the individual features
\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{i})
would lose order information, vectorization
\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{0}),\ldots,\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})\right)\in{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}
makes sequences of different length not comparable.
It turns out that multiplication is well-suited for this task in a suitable algebra.

Fortunately, there is a natural way to embed any Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
into a larger Hilbert space
\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})
that is also a non-commutative algebra, see Sec.
1.1.2
.
First, we take the 1
st
differences,

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\mapsto\delta\varphi(\mathbf{x})=\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{i})-\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{i-1})\right)_{i=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\in{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}},\quad\text{where}\hskip 3.0pt\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})

(5.3)

since it is more natural to keep track of changes rather than absolute values.
Then we identify
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
as a subset of
\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})
.
The simplest choice given the above construction of
\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})
is

\displaystyle\iota:\mathbf{h}\mapsto(1,\mathbf{h},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\ldots)\in\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})\quad\text{where}\hskip 3.0pt\mathbf{h}\in{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}.

(5.4)

A direct calculation shows that composing the maps (
5.3
), (
5.4
), and multiplying the individual entries in
\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})
results in a sequence summary using all non-contiguous subsequences, since in each multiplication step a sequence entry is either selected once or not at all.
This gives rise to the discretized signatures
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
with
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}\geq 2
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\iota(\delta\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{i}))=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\delta\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}})\right)_{m\geq 0},

(5.5)

where
\Delta_{m}:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\to\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{m}

is as defined in (
5.1
) and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
. Thus, the sequence feature is itself a sequence, however, now a sequence of tensors indexed by their degree
m\in\mathbb{N}
in contrast to being indexed by the time index
i\in[{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}]
.
These sequence features are invariant to a natural transformation of time series called time-warping, but can also be made sensitive to it by including time as an extra coordinate with the mapping
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\mapsto(t_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}
. It also possesses similar approximation properties to path signatures in Section (
1.2.2
), i.e. uniform approximation of functions of sequences on compact sets; see Appendices A and B in
[
263
]
.

Despite the abstract derivation, the resulting feature map
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}}
is—in principle—explicitly computable when
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
; see
[
142
]
for details.
However, when the static feature map
\varphi
is high- or infinite-dimensional, this is not feasible and we discuss a kernel trick further below.

Remark 5.1
.

We used the map
\iota
, as defined in (
5.4
), to embed
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
into
\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})
.
Other choices are possible, for example one could use the embedding
\hat{\iota}:{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}\to\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})
for
\mathbf{h}\in{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}

\displaystyle\hat{\iota}(\mathbf{h})=\left(1,\mathbf{h},\frac{\mathbf{h}^{\otimes 2}}{2!},\frac{\mathbf{h}^{\otimes 3}}{3!},\ldots\right){\in\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})}.

(5.6)

This embedding is actually the classical choice in mathematics, but different choices of the embedding lead to, besides potential improvements in benchmarks, mildly different computational complexities and interesting algebraic questions
[
69
,
263
,
265
]
.

Finally, it can be useful to only consider the first
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
tensors in the series
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathbf{x})
analogously to using the first
M
moments in classic polynomial regression to avoid overfitting. Hence, we define the
M
-truncated signature features for
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
as

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x})=\left(1,\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}(\mathbf{x}),\ldots,\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{M}}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\dots\right)\quad\text{for}\hskip 3.0pt\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X}),

(5.7)

where
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x})
is the projection of
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathbf{x})
onto
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{\otimes m}
.
In practice, we regard
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and the choice of the embedding as hyperparameters to optimize. From now on, we will focus on the order-
1
case, but we note that all algorithms can be generalized fully to the higher-order case.

Signature Kernels

The signature is a powerful feature set for nonlinear regression on paths and sequences.
A computational bottleneck associated with it is the dimensionality of the feature space
\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})
. As we are dealing with tensors, for
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
finite-dimensional
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x})
is a tensor of degree-
m
which has
\left(\dim{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}\right)^{m}
coordinates that need to be computed. This can quickly become computationally expensive. For infinite-dimensional
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
, e.g. when
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (
RKHS
), which is one of the most interesting settings due to the modelling flexibility, it is infeasible to directly compute
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}}
.
In Section
1.2.6
, we discuss a kernel trick allows to compute the inner product of discretized signature features (
5.5
) up to a given degree
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
using dynamic programming, even when
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
is infinite-dimensional.
As mentioned before, here we focus on discrete-time, and our starting point is the approach of order-
1
discretized signature kernel, which is often referred to as a non-geometric approximation to the signature kernel
[
69
]
. For the continuous-time treatment of signature kernels, see Section
1.2.4
.

Above we described a generic way to turn a static feature map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
into a feature map
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x})
for sequences, see (
5.5
). The signature kernel is a powerful formalism that allows to transform any static kernel on
\mathcal{X}
into a kernel for sequences that evolve in
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous and bounded kernel, and from now on, let
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
denote its
RKHS
, and
\varphi(\mathbf{x})=\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}}\equiv\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\cdot)
the associated reproducing kernel lift for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
. We define the
M
-truncated (discretized) signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
for
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x}),\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})}=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\left\langle\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x}),\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{\otimes m}}

(5.8)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\delta_{i_{1},j_{1}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}})\cdots\delta_{i_{m},j_{m}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}),

(5.9)

where we defined the level-
m
(discretized) signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
for
m\in[M]
as
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x}),\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{\otimes m}}
, and
\delta_{i,j}
denotes 2
nd
-order cross-differencing such that
\delta_{i,j}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\mathbf{y}_{j+1})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\mathbf{y}_{j})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j+1})+\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})
for
i\in[{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}]
and
j\in[{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}]
.

Although (
5.9
) looks expensive to compute,
[
146
]
applies dynamic programming to efficiently compute
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}
using a recursive algorithm. Importantly, (
5.9
) avoids computing tensors, and only depends on the entry-wise evaluations of the static kernel
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})
. Indeed, this leads to a computational cost of
O((M+d){\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})
, which is feasible for sequences evolving in high-dimensional state-spaces, but only with moderate sequence length. Note that the same bottleneck applies to PDE-based approaches. In part, the aim of this article is to alleviate this quadratic cost in sequence length, while approximately enjoying the modelling capability of working within an infinite-dimensional
RKHS
.

5.2.3
Random Fourier Features

Kernel methods allow to implicitly use an infinite-dimensional feature map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
by evaluation of the inner product
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\langle\varphi(\mathbf{x}),\varphi(\mathbf{y})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
, when
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space. This inner product can often be evaluated without direct computation of
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
and
\varphi(\mathbf{y})
via the kernel trick. Although this makes them a powerful tool due to the resulting flexibility, the price of this flexibility is a trade-off in complexity with respect to the number of samples
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Disregarding the price of evaluating the kernel
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
momentarily, kernel methods require the computation of a Gram matrix with
O(N^{2})
entries, that further incurs an
O(N^{3})
computational cost by most downstream algorithms, such as
KRR

[
239
]
,
GP

[
216
]
, and
SVM

[
234
]
. Several techniques reduce this complexity, and the focal point of this article is the Random Fourier Feature (
RFF
) technique of
[
209
,
210
,
211
]
, which can be applied to any continuous, bounded, translation-invariant kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
1
1

1

A kernel is called translation-invariant if
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{z})
for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Throughout, we write with some abuse of notation
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\equiv\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

A corollary of Bochner’s theorem
[
220
]
is that any continuous, bounded, and translation-invariant kernel
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
can be represented as the Fourier transform of a non-negative finite measure
\Lambda
called the spectral measure associated to
\mathsf{k}
, i.e. for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp(i\mathbf{w}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}))\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda(\mathbf{w}).

(5.10)

We may, without loss of generality, assume that
\Lambda
is a probability measure such that
\Lambda(\mathbb{R}^{d})=0
, which amounts to working with the kernel
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})/\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{0})
.
[
209
]
proposed to draw
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

i.i.d.

samples from
\Lambda
,
\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{\tilde{d}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda
, to define the random feature map for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}:\mathcal{X}\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}=\mathbb{R}^{2\tilde{d}},\quad\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}\left(\cos\left(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\right),\sin{\left(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\right)}\right),

(5.11)

where
\mathbf{W}=\left(\mathbf{w}_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\tilde{d}}
. Then, the corresponding random kernel is defined for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{k}}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}}=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}}\cos\left(\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\right)

(5.12)

to provide a probabilistic approximation to
\mathsf{k}
. Indeed, it is a straightforward exercise to check that
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right]\approx\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.
This approximation converges exponentially fast in
\tilde{d}
and uniformly over compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as proven in
[
209
, Claim 1]
. This bound was later tightened and extended to the derivatives of the kernel in the series of works
[
244
,
255
,
35
]
, and we provide an adapted version under Theorem
5.26
in the supplement.

5.3
Methodology

The goal of this section is to build random features for sequences, that enjoy the benefit of linear sequence length and low-dimensional feature complexity with theoretical guarantees that the corresponding inner product is close to the
M
-truncated (discretized) signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}
with high probability. We construct these random features in a two step process: firstly, we reduce the feature space from infinite to finite (but high) dimensionality through a careful construction using random Fourier features (
RFF
s), and in the second step we apply further dimensionality reduction to reduce the complexity to an even lower dimensional space in order to aid in scalability. Although we present this construction as distinct steps, the steps are coupled during the computation, and the features can be computed directly without the initial step.

5.3.1
Random Fourier Signature Features

In Section
5.2
, we recalled the
RFF
construction, which associates to a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant kernel
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
on
\mathcal{X}
a spectral measure
\Lambda
, and approximates
\mathsf{k}
by drawing samples from
\Lambda
to define the random features
\tilde{\varphi}:\mathcal{X}\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}
(
5.11
), and the random kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
(
5.12
). Afterwards, we presented a generic way to turn any such static features
\tilde{\varphi}:\mathcal{X}\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}
for elements of
\mathcal{X}
into sequence features for sequences that evolve in
\mathcal{X}
via
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{H})
. Applying this construction with the
RFF
as feature map on
\mathcal{X}
would already result in a random feature map for sequences, i.e. a map from
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
into
\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}
.
Taking the inner product in
\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}
of this new random feature map for sequences would, however, only yield a biased estimator for the truncated signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}
.
We correct for this bias by revisiting our previous construction, and build an unbiased approximation to
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}
using independent
RFF
copies in each tensor multiplication step. Then, we show in Theorem
5.3
that this random estimator comes with good probabilistic guarantees.

The probabilistic construction procedure is outlined in the following definition.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}
be
i.i.d.
random matrices sampled from
\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}
for
RFF
dimension
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and define the independent
RFF
maps
\tilde{\varphi}_{m}:\mathcal{X}\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}
as in (
5.11
), i.e.
\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}\left(\cos({{\mathbf{W}^{(m)}}^{\top}}\mathbf{x}),\sin({\mathbf{W}^{(m)}}^{\top}\mathbf{x})\right)
for
m\in\left[M\right]
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
. The
M
-truncated Random Fourier Signature Feature (
RFSF
) map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}
from sequences in
\mathcal{X}
into the free algebra over
\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}
is defined for truncation level
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}})\right)_{m=0}^{M}.

(5.13)

Further, the
RFSF
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
can be computed for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}}=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}^{\otimes m}}

(5.14)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\delta_{i_{1},j_{1}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}})\cdots\delta_{i_{m},j_{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}),

(5.15)

where we defined the level-
m

RFSF
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
as
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}^{\otimes m}}
with the convention that
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{0}}\equiv 1
, and
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{M}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
are independent
RFF
kernels defined as in (
5.12
) with the random weights
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(M)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\tilde{d}}
.

Since the feature map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
can be directly evaluated in the feature space recursively,
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
has linear complexity in the sequence length. However, it requires computing high-dimensional tensors, where the degree-
m
component
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x})\in\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}^{\otimes m}
has
(\dim\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}})^{m}=(2\tilde{d})^{m}
coordinates, making it infeasible for large
m,\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Remark
5.4
discusses the computational complexity in detail. Further, note that the kernel can be evaluated by means of a kernel trick exactly analogously to the evaluation of (
5.9
), but in this case there are no computational gains compared to the infinite-dimensional signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

Next, we provide a theoretical analysis to show that the random kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
converges to the ground truth signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
exponentially fast and uniformly over compact state-spaces
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, generalizing the result
[
209
, Claim 2]
to this non-Euclidean domain of sequences.
Throughout the analysis, we need certain regularity properties of
\Lambda
in order to invoke quantitative versions of the law of large numbers, i.e. properties such as boundedness, existence of the moment-generating function, moment-boundedness, or belonging to certain Orlicz spaces of random variables. Boundedness of the spectral measure is too restrictive an assumption, since a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant kernel
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is characteristic if and only if the support of its spectral measure is
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, see
[
243
, Prop. 8]
. Hence, we instead work with the assumption that its moments are well-controllable, i.e. the tails of the distribution are not “too heavy”. Specifically, we assume the Bernstein moment condition that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[w_{i}^{2m}\right]\leq\frac{m!S^{2}R^{m-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0pti\in[d]

(5.16)

for some
S,R>0
. We show in the Supplementary Material under Lemmas
5.21
and
5.22
, in a more general context, that this is equivalent to
\Lambda
being a sub-Gaussian probability measure; see e.g.
[
24
, Sec 2.3]
and
[
277
, Sec. 2.5]
about sub-Gaussianity. This of course includes the spectral measure of the Gaussian kernel defined for bandwidth
\sigma>0
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}

\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\exp\left(-\nicefrac{{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}}{{2\sigma^{2}}}\right)
, which has a Gaussian spectral distribution
\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sigma^{2}}}\mathbf{I}_{d}\right)
, and therefore calculation gives
\mathbb{E}_{w\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sigma^{2}}}\right)}\left[w^{2m}\right]=\frac{2^{m}\Gamma\left(m+\frac{1}{2}\right))}{\sigma^{2m}\sqrt{\pi}}<\frac{m!}{2}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sigma^{2}\sqrt[4]{\pi}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{m-2}
,
since
\Gamma\left(m+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\right)<\Gamma(m+1)=m!
. Hence
\Lambda
satisfies condition (
5.16
) with
S,R
as given here.
Now we state our approximation theorem regarding
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
, which quantifies that it is a (sub-)exponentially good estimator of
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}
with high probability and uniformly.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant
kernel with spectral measure
\Lambda
, which satisfies (
5.16
).
Let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact and convex with diameter
\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert
,
\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}=\{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}:\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}\}
. Then, the following quantities are finite:
\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right]
,
L=\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\top}\right]\right\rVert_{2}^{1/2}
,
E_{i,j}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}\right\rvert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}\right]
and
D_{i,j}=\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lVert\nabla\left[\frac{\partial^{2}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{j}}\right]\right\rVert_{2}
for
i,j\in[d]
. Further, for any max. sequence
1
-var
V>0
, and signature level
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, for
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq

(5.17)

\displaystyle\leq m\begin{cases}\left(C_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\beta_{d,m,V}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}+d\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)(S^{2}+R)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{2}\right)\,&\text{ for }\,\epsilon<\beta_{d,m,V}\\
\left(C_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\beta_{d,m,V}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{(d+1)m}}+d\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)(S^{2}+R)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,v}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m}}\right)\,&\text{ for }\,\epsilon\geq\beta_{d,m,V},\end{cases}

(5.18)

where
C_{d,\mathcal{X}}=2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}16\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}(D_{i,j}+E_{i,j})^{\frac{d}{d+1}}
and
\beta_{d,m,V}=m\left(2V^{2}\left(L^{2}\vee 1\right)\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)\right)^{m}
.

The proof is provided in the supplement under Theorem
5.36
. The result shows that the random kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
approximates the signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}
uniformly over subsets of
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
of sequences
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
with maximal
1
-variation
V
,
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V
, assuming that the state-space
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a convex and compact domain.
The error bound is analogous to the classic
RFF
bounds, in the sense that the tail probability decreases exponentially fast as a function of the
RFF
dimension
\tilde{d}
. The functional form of the bound is inherited from Theorem
5.26
, which provides an analogous result for the derivatives of
RFF
. This link follows from Lemma
5.35
, which connects the concentration of the
RFSF
kernel to the second derivatives of
RFF
.

The main difference from the classic case, i.e.
[
209
, Claim 1]
and Theorem
5.26
, is the appearance of
\beta_{d,m,V}
which controls a regime change in the tail behaviour. Concretely, for
\epsilon<\beta_{d,m,V}
(
5.18
) has a polynomial plus a sub-Gaussian tail, while for
\epsilon>\beta_{d,m,V}
has a
\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{m}}\right)
-subexponential tail.
This is not surprising as the inner summand in (
5.15
) is the
m
-fold tensor product of
m
independent
RFF
kernels, which makes the tail heavier exactly by an exponent of
\nicefrac{{1}}{{m}}
.
The constant itself,
\beta_{d,m,V}
, depends on

(i)

the maximal sequence 1-variation
V
, which measures a notion of time-warping invariant sequence complexity;

(ii)

the Lipschitz constant of the kernel
L
(see Examples
5.28
and
5.29
);

(iii)

the trace of the second moment of
\Lambda
,
\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right]
;

(iv)

the state-space dimension
d
;

(v)

and the signature level
m
itself.

Remark 5.4
.

Algorithm
7
demonstrates the computation of the
RFSF
map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
given a dataset of sequences
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
. Upon inspection, we can deduce that the algorithm has a computational complexity of
O\left(N\ell(Md\tilde{d}^{M})\right)
. Importantly, it is linear in
\ell
, the sequence length, although scales polynomially in the
RFF
sample size
\tilde{d}^{M}
, and exponentially in the truncation-
M
.

5.3.2
Dimensionality Reduction: Diagonal Projection

Previously, we introduced a featurized approximation
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
to the signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}}
, called the
RFSF
kernel, which reduces the computation from the infinite-dimensional
RKHS
to a finite-dimensional feature space using random tensors. Although this makes the computation in the feature space viable of the
RFSF
map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
, it is still tensor-valued, which incurs a computational cost of
O(\tilde{d}+\tilde{d}^{2}+\cdots+\tilde{d}^{m})
in the
RFF
dimension
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Now, we take another step towards scalability and apply further dimensionality reduction. By examining the structure of these tensors, we introduce a diagonally projected variant called
RFSF-DP
that considerably reduces their sizes. We emphasize that the above
RFSF
construction is crucial: it approximates the inner product in an infinite-dimensional space, and now we further approximate it in an even lower dimensional space. The benefit is that one does not have to go through the computation of the initial
RFSF
map, but only the selected degrees of freedom have to be computed.

As a first observation, we notice that the computation of (
5.15
) can be reformulated, due to (
5.12
) and linearity of the differencing operator, in the following way:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}^{m}}\sum_{q_{1},\dots,q_{m}=1}^{\tilde{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\delta_{i_{p},j_{p}}\cos\left({\mathbf{w}_{q_{p}}^{(p)}}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}}-\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right)

(5.19)

by spelling out the definition of the
RFF
kernel, where
\mathbf{w}_{1}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{\tilde{d}}^{(m)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda
, such that
\mathbf{W}^{(p)}=\left(\mathbf{w}_{1}^{(p)},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{\tilde{d}}^{(p)}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\tilde{d}}
as defined in Def.
5.2
. Now, we may observe that there is a dependency structure among the samples being averaged in (
5.19
), since the outer summation is over the Cartesian product
(q_{1},\dots,q_{m})\in[\tilde{d}]^{\times m}
, which suggests that we might be able to drastically reduce the degrees of freedom by restricting this summation to only go over an independent set of samples.
One way to do this is to restrict to multi-indices of the form
\mathcal{I}=\left\{(q,\dots,q)\in[\tilde{d}]^{\times m}\,|\,q\in[\tilde{d}]\right\}
, i.e. we diagonally project the index set, motivating the name of the approach stated in the following definition.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\mathbf{w}^{(1)}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}^{(M)}_{\tilde{d}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda
for
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and define
\hat{\varphi}_{m,q}:\mathcal{X}\to\hat{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
with sample size
\hat{d}=1
for
q\in[\tilde{d}]
and
m\in[M]
, such that
\hat{\varphi}_{m,q}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\cos({\mathbf{w}^{(m)}_{q}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}),\sin({\mathbf{w}^{(m)}_{q}}^{\top}\mathbf{x})\right)
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
. The
M
-truncated Diagonally Projected Random Fourier Signature Feature (
RFSF-DP
) map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{DP}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}^{\texttt{DP}}=\bigoplus_{m=0}^{M}\left({\hat{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}^{\otimes m}\right)^{\tilde{d}}
is defined for truncation
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{DP}}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}\left(\left(\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\delta\hat{\varphi}_{1,q}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\hat{\varphi}_{m,q}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}})\right)_{q=1}^{\tilde{d}}\right)_{m=0}^{M}.

(5.20)

Then, the
RFSF-DP
kernel can be directly computed for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
via

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{DP}}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{DP}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}^{\texttt{DP}}}=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{DP}}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{DP}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\left({\hat{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}^{\otimes m}\right)^{\tilde{d}}}

(5.21)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sum_{q=1}^{\tilde{d}}\mathrlap{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}(\ell_{\mathbf{x}}-1)\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}(\ell_{\mathbf{y}}-1)\end{subarray}}}\hskip 38.0pt\delta_{i_{1},j_{1}}\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{1,q}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}})\cdots\delta_{i_{m},j_{m}}\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{m,q}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}),

(5.22)

where we defined the level-
m

RFSF-DP
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{DP}}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{DP}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\left({\hat{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}^{\otimes m}\right)^{\tilde{d}}}
with the convention that
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{0}}\equiv 1
, and
\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{m,q}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
are independent
RFF
kernels with sample size
\hat{d}=1
defined for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}
as
\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{m,q}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\hat{\varphi}_{m,q}(\mathbf{x}),\hat{\varphi}_{m,q}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}
with the random weights
\mathbf{w}^{(m)}_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
q\in[\tilde{d}],m\in[M]
.

Note that by the definition of the
RFF
kernels in (
5.22
), we may substitute that
\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{p,q}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\cos({\mathbf{w}^{(p)}_{q}}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}))
for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}
, so (
5.22
) is equivalently written for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{q=1}^{\tilde{d}}\mathrlap{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}(\ell_{\mathbf{x}}-1)\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}(\ell_{\mathbf{y}}-1)\end{subarray}}}\hskip 40.0pt\delta_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cos({\mathbf{w}^{(1)}_{q}}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}-\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}}))\cdots\delta_{i_{m},j_{m}}\cos({\mathbf{w}^{(m)}_{q}}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}-\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}})),

(5.23)

which is what we set out to do in the above paragraph; that is, restrict the outer summation onto the diagonal projection of the index set.
Another way to look at Definition
5.5
is that the
RFSF-DP
kernel in (
5.22
) is constructed by defining
\tilde{d}
independent
RFSF
kernels, each with internal
RFF
sample size
\hat{d}=1
, and then taking their average; the concatenation of their corresponding features are then the features of the
RFSF-DP
map. Note that for
RFF
sample size
1
, each
RFF
map has dimension
2
, i.e.
\hat{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and hence, the corresponding
RFSF
kernels have dimension
1+2+\cdots+2^{M}=(2^{M+1}-1)
, which by concatenation results in the overall dimensionality of the
RFSF-DP
kernel being
\dim\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}^{\texttt{TRP}}=\tilde{d}\left(2^{M+1}-1\right)
. This relates to the computational complexity of the
RFSF-DP
map; for details see Remark
5.7
.

Next, we state our concentration result regarding the level-
m

RFSF-DP
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant kernel with spectral measure
\Lambda
, which satisfies (
5.16
). Then, for level
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, and
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\min\left\{\left(\frac{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}\epsilon}{2C_{d,m,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}}\right)^{2},\left(\frac{\tilde{d}\epsilon}{\sqrt{8}C_{d,m,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m}}\right\}\right),

(5.24)

where
L=\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\top}\right]\right\rVert
is the Lipschitz constant of
\mathsf{k}
, and
C_{d,m,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}>0
is bounded by

\displaystyle C_{d,m,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\leq\sqrt{8}e^{4}(2\pi)^{1/4}e^{1/24}(4e^{3}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}/m)^{m}\left(\left(2d\max(S,R)\right)^{m}+\left(L^{2}/\ln 2\right)^{m}\right).

(5.25)

The proof is provided in the supplement under Theorem
5.37
. The result shows that the
RFSF-DP
kernel converges for any two sequences
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
with a
\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{m}}\right)
-subexponential convergence rate with respect to the sample size
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Similarly to Theorem
5.3
, the bound has a phase transition, where for small values of
\epsilon
, it has a sub-Gaussian tail, while for larger values, it has a
\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{m}}\right)
-subexponential tail. A crucial difference from the previous bound is that now the phase transition happens at
\epsilon^{\star}=C_{d,m,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}2^{\frac{2m-3/2}{2m-1}}\tilde{d}^{\frac{1-m}{2m-1}}
, which depends on the sample size
\tilde{d}
. This means that for fixed value of
\epsilon>0
, the phase transition always happens eventually as
\tilde{d}
gets large enough, hence the convergence rate with respect to
\tilde{d}
is
\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{m}}\right)
-subexponential regardless of the value of
\epsilon
. The slightly reduced rate of convergence compared to the
RFSF
kernel in Theorem
5.3
is to be expected, since the sample size of the
RFSF-DP
kernel is analogously reduced by an exponent of
\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{m}}\right)
with respect to
\tilde{d}
in comparison.
The constant
C_{d,m,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}
, similarly to (
5.18
), depends on

(i)

the 1-variation of sequences
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}
that measure the complexity of the sequences;

(ii)

L>0
, the Lipschitz constant of the kernel
\mathsf{k}
(see Examples
5.28
,
5.29
);

(iii)

the moment bound parameters
S,R>0
from condition (
5.16
);

(iv)

the state-space dimension
d
; and

(v)

the signature level
m
.

Remark 5.7
.

Algorithm
8
demonstrates the computation of the
RFSF-DP
map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{DP}}
given a dataset of sequences
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
. Upon counting the operations, we deduce that the algorithm has a computational complexity
O\left(N\ell\tilde{d}(Md+2^{M})\right)
. Crucially, it is linear in both
\ell
, the maximal sequence length, and
\tilde{d}
, the sample size of the random kernel.

5.3.3
Dimensionality Reduction: Tensor Random Projection

Previously, we built the
RFSF-DP
map by subsampling an independent set from the samples that constitute
RFSF
kernel. Here, we propose an alternative dimensionality reduction technique that starts again from the
RFSF
map, and uses random projections to project this generally high-dimensional tensor onto a lower dimension. Random projections are a classic technique in data science for reducing the data dimension, while preserving its important structural properties. They are built upon the celebrated Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma
[
135
]
, which states that a set of points in a high-dimensional space can be embedded into a space of much lower dimension, while approximately preserving their
geometry. Exploiting this property, we construct a tensor random projected (
TRP
) variant of our random kernel called
RFSF-TRP
, such that the computation is coupled between the
RFSF
and
TRP
maps, similarly to a kernel trick.

Tensorized random projections
[
250
,
212
]
construct random projections for tensors with concise parameterization that respects their tensorial nature. Given tensors
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, the
TRP
map with
CP
(
CANDECOMP/PARAFAC

[
148
]
) rank-
1
is built via a random functional

\texttt{Pr}:\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\texttt{Pr}(\mathbf{s})=\left\langle\mathbf{p}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{p}_{m},\mathbf{s}\right\rangle_{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}}
, where
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d})
are
d
-dimensional component vectors sampled from a standard normal distribution. Then, the inner product can be estimated as
\texttt{Pr}(\mathbf{s})\texttt{Pr}(\mathbf{t})\approx\mathbb{E}\left[\texttt{Pr}(\mathbf{s})\texttt{Pr}(\mathbf{t})\right]=\left\langle\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\right\rangle_{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}}
. Variance reduction is achieved by stacking
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such random projections, each with
i.i.d.
component vectors
\mathbf{p}_{1}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}^{(n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d})
. Hence, the
TRP
operator is defined as

\displaystyle\texttt{TRP}:\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\texttt{TRP}(\mathbf{s})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\left\langle\mathbf{p}_{1}^{(i)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{p}_{m}^{(i)},\mathbf{s}\right\rangle\right)_{i=1}^{n}.

(5.26)

On the one hand, this allows to represent the random projection map onto
\mathbb{R}^{n}
using only
O(nmd)
parameters as opposed to the
O(nd^{m})
parameters in a densely parametrized random projection; and on the other, it allows for downstream computations to exploit the low-rank structure of the operator, as we shall do so in the definition stated below.

Definition 5.8
.

Let
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}
be
i.i.d.
random matrices sampled from
\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}
for
RFF
dimension
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, define the independent
RFF
maps
\tilde{\varphi}_{m}:\mathcal{X}\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}
as in (
5.11
), i.e.
\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x})=\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}}\left(\cos({{\mathbf{W}^{(m)}}^{\top}}\mathbf{x}),\sin({\mathbf{W}^{(m)}}^{\top}\mathbf{x})\right)
for
m\in\left[M\right]
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, and let
\mathbf{P}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{P}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}^{\tilde{d}}\left(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{2\tilde{d}}\right)

be random matrices with
i.i.d.
standard normal entries. The
M
-truncated Tensor Random Projected Random Fourier Signature Feature (
RFSF-TRP
) map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{TRP}}\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}^{\texttt{TRP}}=\mathbb{R}^{M\tilde{d}}
is defined for truncation level
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{TRP}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}\left(\left(\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(1)}_{q},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}})\right\rangle\cdots\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(m)}_{q},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}})\right\rangle\right)_{q=1}^{\tilde{d}}\right)_{m=0}^{M}

(5.27)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\left({\mathbf{P}^{(1)}}^{\top}\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}})\right)\odot\cdots\odot\left({\mathbf{P}^{(m)}}^{\top}\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}})\right)\right)_{m=0}^{M},

(5.28)

where
\mathbf{P}^{(m)}=\left(\mathbf{p}_{q}^{(m)}\right)_{q=1}^{\tilde{d}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\tilde{d}\times\tilde{d}}
, and
\odot
denotes the Hadamard product
2
2

2

The Hadamard product stands for component-wise multiplication of the vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathbf{x}\odot\mathbf{y}=\left(x_{i}y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{n}
.
.
The
RFSF-TRP
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
can then be directly computed for sequences
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{TRP}}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{TRP}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}}=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{TRP}}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{TRP}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}^{\otimes m}}

(5.29)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sum_{q=1}^{\tilde{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{q},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{q},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle,

(5.30)

where we defined the level-
m

RFSF-TRP
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
for
m\leq M
as
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{TRP}}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{TRP}}(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}^{\otimes m}}
with the convention that
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{0}}\equiv 1
.

We remark that (
5.28
) is equivalent to the
TRP
operator (
5.26
) applied to the
RFSF
map (
5.13
) by exploiting bilinearity of the inner product, and using that it factorizes over the tensor components, as described in (
1.4
). Then, the unbiasedness of (
5.30
) follows from the fact that the
TRP
operator is an isometry under expectation, which is applied to the
RFSF
tensor
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
, therefore
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
kernel is conditionally an unbiased estimator of
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
given the
RFSF
weights
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(m)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\tilde{d}}
.
By the tower rule for expectations,
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
is an unbiased estimator of
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}
. The approximation quality is then governed by two factors:

(i)

how well the
TRP
projected kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
approximates
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
;

(ii)

the quality of the approximation of
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
with respect to
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}
.

Note that
(ii)
has already been discussed in Theorem
5.3
. Here, we state the following theoretical result which quantifies
(i)
. Combining these two results by means of triangle inequality and union bounding quantifies that
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
is a good estimator of
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}
.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant kernel with spectral measure
\Lambda
, which satisfies (
5.16
). Then, the following bound holds for
RFSF-TRP
kernel for signature level
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
sequences
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
and
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq C_{d,\Lambda}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{m^{2}\tilde{d}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}}{2\sqrt{2}e^{3}R\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right),

(5.31)

where the absolute constant is defined as
C_{d,\Lambda}=2\left(1+\frac{S}{2R}+\frac{S^{2}}{4R^{2}}\right)^{d}
.

The proof is given in the supplement under Theorem
5.38
utilizing the hypercontractivity of Gaussian polynomials
[
134
]
that is used to quantify the concentration of the
TRP
estimator. The concentration of the
RFSF-TRP
kernel is then governed by Theorems
5.3
and
5.9
combined. Together, they show that for smaller values of
\epsilon
(i.e. the regime change as discussed below Theorem
5.3
), the probability has a polynomial plus a sub-Gaussian tail, while for large
\epsilon
, it has a
\left(\frac{1}{2m}\right)
-subexponential tail due to (
5.31
), and the dominant convergence rate with respect to
\tilde{d}
is
\left(\frac{1}{4m}\right)
-subexponential. This means that in terms of convergence,
RFSF-TRP
is the slowest among the 3 variations introduced so far. However, it is also the most efficient in terms of overall dimension, hence downstream computational complexity as well, since
\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}^{\texttt{TRP}}=\mathbb{R}^{M\tilde{d}}
.
Remark
5.10
discusses the computational complexity in detail.

Remark 5.10
.

Algorithm
9
demonstrates the computation of the
RFSF-TRP
map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{TRP}}
given a dataset of sequences
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
. Counting the operations, here we can deduce that the algorithm has an
O\left(MN\ell\tilde{d}(d+\tilde{d})\right)
computational complexity. This variation is also linear in
\ell
, the maximal sequence length, although it is quadratic in
\tilde{d}
.

5.3.4
Numerical evaluation

Figure 5.1
:
Approximation error of random kernels against
RFF
size on
\log
-
\log
plot.

Here, we numerically evaluate the approximation error of the proposed scalable kernels, that is,
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
. We do not include
RFSF
since its dimensionality shows polynomial explosion in the base sample size
\tilde{d}
due to its tensor-based representation, which makes its computation infeasible for reasonable values of
\tilde{d}
. We generate
d
-dimensional synthetic time series of length-
\ell
using a
VAR
(1) process
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, such that
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{t+1}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{d}}}A\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{t}+\mathbf{\epsilon}_{t}
, where
A\sim\mathcal{N}^{d\times d}(0,1)
and
\mathbf{\epsilon}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{d})
. Then, we compute the normalized version
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
of
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
, which is rescaled to have
1
-variation
V>0
, i.e.
\mathbf{x}_{t}=V\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{t}/\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\right\rVert_{{1\texttt{-var}}}
. We set
d=10
,
\ell=100
,
\sigma=0.1
and
V=100
. We compute the squared deviation between the groundtruth signature kernel and the randomized approximations for two randomly sampled time series in this way. This process is repeated for
100
randomly sampled time series and
100
times resampled random kernel evaluations, giving rise to overall
10000
evaluations for each value of
\tilde{d}
. Figure
5.1
shows the average approximation error plotted against values of
\tilde{d}
on a
\log
-
\log
plot. We can observe that both
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
have approximately the same error curves for a given value of truncation level
M
, and the steepness appears to be the same across different levels of
M
. This means that
RFSF-TRP
is slightly more efficient in terms of dimensionality, since its dimension is
M\tilde{d}
as opposed to
2^{M+1}\tilde{d}
in
RFSF-DP
. We also observe that both curves are close to being linear, which indicates that the approximation error scales approximately as
O(\tilde{d}^{-\alpha})
for some value of
\alpha>0
.

5.4
Experiments

5.4.1
Time series classification

We perform multivariate time series classification to investigate the performance of the scalable
RFSF
variants compared to the full-rank signature kernel and other quadratic time baseline kernels, and further, to demonstrate the scalability to large-scale datasets, where the quadratic sample complexity becomes prohibitive.
We use support vector machine (
SVM
)
[
248
]
classification for classifying multivariate time series on datasets of various sizes. For quadratic time kernels, the dual
SVM
formulation is used, while for kernels with feature representations, we use the primal formulation that has linear complexity in the size of the dataset
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
aiding in scalability to truly large-scale datasets. For each considered kernel/feature, we use a
GPU
-based implementation provided in the
KSig
library
3
3

3

https://github.com/tgcsaba/KSig

[
264
]
. For large-scale experiments with the featurized kernels, linear
SVM
implementation is used from the
cuML
library
[
215
]
, while the dual
SVM
on moderate-scale datasets uses the
sklearn
library
[
204
]
. For multi-class problems, we use the one-vs-one classification strategy. This study is also the largest scale comparison of signature kernels to date which extends the datasets considered in
[
228
]
.
The hardware used was 2 computer clusters equipped with overall 8 NVIDIA 3080 Ti
GPU
s.

Table 5.1
:
Computational complexities of kernels in our experiments;
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is the number of time series,
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is their length,
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is their state-space dimension,
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is the signature truncation level,
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is the
RF
dimension,
W\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is the warping length in
RWS
.

RFSF-DP

RFSF-TRP

KSig

KSigPDE

RWS

GAK

RBF

RFF

O\left(N\ell\tilde{d}\left(Md+2^{M}\right)\right)

O\left(N\ell M\tilde{d}\left(d+\tilde{d}\right)\right)

O\left(N^{2}\ell^{2}\left(M+d\right)\right)

O\left(N^{2}\ell^{2}d\right)

O\left(N\ell Wd\right)

O\left(N^{2}\ell^{2}d\right)

O\left(N^{2}\ell d\right)

O\left(N\ell d\tilde{d}\right)

5.4.2
Methods

We compare the proposed variants
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
to the baselines described here:

(1)

the
M
-truncated Signature Kernel
[
146
]

KSig
formulated via the kernel trick, and is a quadratic time baseline;

(2)

the Signature-PDE Kernel
[
228
]

KSigPDE
, which uses the 2
nd
-order PDE solver and also has quadratic complexity;

(3)

the Global Alignment Kernel
[
58
]

GAK
, one of the most popular sequence kernels to day and can can be related to the signature kernel, see
[
146
, Sec. 5]
;

(4)

Random Warping Series
[
292
]

RWS
, which produces features by
DTW
alignments between the input and randomly sampled time series;

(5)

the
RBF
kernel, which treats the whole time series as a vector of length
\mathbb{R}^{\ell d}
,

(6)

Random Fourier Reatures
[
209
]

RFF
, which also treats the time series as a long vector. We excluded
RFSF
from the comparison, as it is unfeasible to compute it with reasonable sample sizes
\tilde{d}
due to the polynomial explosion of dimensions in its tensor-based representation. The complexities are compared in Table
5.1
.

5.4.3
Hyperparameter selection

For each dataset-kernel, we perform cross-validation to select the optimal hyperparameters that are optimized over the Cartesian product of the following options. For each method that requires a static kernel, we use the
RBF
kernel with bandwidth hyperparameter
\sigma>0
. This is specified in terms of a rescaled median heuristic, i.e.

\displaystyle\sigma=\alpha\operatorname{med}\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}/2\,|\,i\in[{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}],j\in[\ell_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}],\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{X}\right\},\quad\text{for}\hskip 3.0pt\alpha>0,

(5.32)

where
\alpha
is chosen from
\alpha\in\{10^{-3},\dots,10^{3}\}
on a logarithmic grid with
19
steps. For each kernel that is not normalized by default (i.e. the
GAK
and
RBF
kernels are normalized, the former is because without normalization it blows up) , we select whether to normalize to unit norm in feature space via
\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})/\sqrt{\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x})\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y})}
. The
SVM
hyperparameter
C>0
is selected from
C\in\{10^{0},10^{1},10^{2},10^{3},10^{4}\}
. Further, motivated by previous work that investigates the effect of path augmentations in the context of signature methods
[
192
]
, we chose 3 augmentations to cross-validate over. First is parameterization encoding, which gives the classifier the ability to remove the warping invariance of a given sequence kernel, adding the time index as an additional coordinate, i.e. for each time series in the dataset
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}
, we augment it via
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\mapsto(\beta i/{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}},\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}
, where
\beta>0
is the parameterization intensity chosen from
\beta\in\{10^{0},10^{1},10^{2},10^{3},10^{4}\}
. The second augmentation is the basepoint encoding, the role of which is to remove the translation invariance of signature features. Note that when the static base kernel is chosen to be a nonlinear kernel other than the Euclidean inner product, the signature kernel is not completely translation-invariant due to the state-space nonlinearities, but it is close being that by the
L
-Lipschitz property in Lemma
5.28
valid for of the static kernels considered in this work. The basepoint encoding adds an initial
\mathbf{0}
step at the beginning of each time series, i.e. for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})\mapsto(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})
. The third augmentation is the lead-lag map, which is defined as
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})\mapsto\left((\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{0}),(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{0}),(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1}),\dots,(\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}},\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}),(\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}},\mathbf{x}_{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})\right)
. For the truncation-based signature kernels, we select the truncation level
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
from
M\in\{2,3,4,5\}
. For
RWS
, we select the warping length from
W\in\{10,20,\dots,100\}
as suggested by the authors. This makes
RWS
the most expensive feature-based kernel, and so as to fit within the same resource limitations, we omit cross-validating over the path augmentations. We select the standard deviation
\sigma>0
of the warping series from the same grid as
\alpha
in (
5.32
). For all
RF
approaches, we set the
RF
dimension
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, so the overall dimension is
1000
. Note that for
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
this is respectively
2^{M+1}\tilde{d}
and
M\tilde{d}
, where
\tilde{d}
is the base
RFF
sample size; for
RWS
it is the number of warping series
\tilde{d}
; while for
RFF
it is twice the number of samples
2\tilde{d}
.

5.4.4
Datasets

UEA
Archive

The
UEA
archive
[
63
]
is a collection of overall 30 datasets for benchmarking classifiers on multivariate time series classification problems containing both binary and multi-class tasks.
The data modality ranges from various sources e.g. human activity recognition, motion classification, ECG classification, EEG/MEG classification, audio spectra recognition, and others. The sizes of the datasets in terms of number of time series range from moderate (
\leq 1000
examples) to large (
\leq 30000
), and includes various lengths between
8
and
18000
. A summary of the dataset characteristics can be found in Table 2 in
[
63
]
. Pre-specified train-test splits are provided for each dataset, which we follow. We evaluate all considered kernels on the moderate datasets (
\leq 1000
time series), but because the non-feature-based become very expensive computationally beyond these sizes, we only evaluate feature-based approaches on medium and large datasets (
\geq 1000
time series). Each featurized approach is trained and evaluated
5
times on each dataset in order to account for the randomness in the hyperparameter selection procedure and evaluation.

Mental Workload Intensity Classification

We evaluate featurized approaches on a large-scale brain-activity recording data set called
fNIRS2MW
.
4
4

4

https://github.com/tufts-ml/fNIRS-mental-workload-classifiers
This dataset contains brain activity recordings collected from overall
68
participants during a
30
-
60
minute experimental session, where they were asked to carry out tasks of varying intensity. The collected time series are sliced into
30
second segments using a sliding window, and each segment is labelled with an intensity level (
0
-
3
), giving rise to overall
\sim 100000
segments, which we split in a ratio of
80-20
for training and testing. We convert the task into a binary classification problem by assigning a label whether the task is low (
0
or
1
) or high (
2
or
3
) intensity.

Satellite Image Classification

As a massive scale task, we use a satellite imagery dataset
5
5

5

https://cloudstor.aarnet.edu.au/plus/index.php/s/pRLVtQyNhxDdCoM
of
N=10^{6}
time series. Each length
\ell=46
time series corresponds to a vegetation index calculated from remote sensing data, and the task is to classify land cover types
[
207
]
by mapping vegetation profiles to various types of crops and forested areas corresponding to
24
classes. We split the dataset in a ratio of
90
-
10
for training and testing.

5.4.5
Results

Table
5.2
compares test accuracies on moderate size multivariate time series classification datasets with
N\leq 1000
from the
UEA
archive.
KSig
provides state-of-the-art performance among all sequence kernels with taking the highest aggregate score in terms of all of average accuracy, average rank, and number of first places. Our proposed random feature variants
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
provide comparable performance on most of the datasets in terms of accuracy, and they are only outperformed by
KSig
and
KSigPDE
with respect to average accuracy and rank. Interestingly,
RFSF-TRP
has more first place rankings, but
RFSF-DP
performs slightly better on average. This shows that on datasets of these sizes, using either of
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
does not sacrifice model performance - even leading to improvements in some cases, potentially due to the implicit regularization effect of restricting to a finite-dimensional feature space - and it can already provide speedups. We visualize the critical difference diagram comparison of all considered approaches in Figure
5.2
.

Table
5.3
demonstrates the performance of scalable approaches, i.e.
RFSF-DP
,
RFSF-TRP
,
RWS
and
RFF
on the remaining
UEA
datasets (
N\geq 1000
), the dataset
fNIRS2MW
(
N=10^{5}
), and the satellite dataset
SITS1M
(
N=10^{6}
). We find it infeasible to perform full cross-validation for quadratic time kernels on these datasets due to expensive kernel computations and downstream cost of dual
SVM
. The results show that both variants
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
perform significantly better on average with respect to accuracy and rank then both
RWS
and
RFF
. Note when
RWS
takes first place, it only improves over our approach marginally, however, when it underperforms, it often does so severely. This is not surprising as both
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
approximate the signature kernel, which is a universal kernel on time series; it is theoretically capable of learning from any kind of time series data as supported by its best overall performance above.

Domain analysis

Next, we provide a domain analysis on the moderate sized
TSC
datasets in Table
5.4
split across 6 data domains
[
63
]
