: audio spectra classification (Audio), electrocardiogram (ECG) recordings, electroencephalography and magnetoencephalography (EEG/MEG), human activitiy recognition (HAR) based on accelerometer or gyroscope data, motion recognition (Motion) using other data sources, and other data modalities. We make the following observations:

(1)

on Audio,
KSig
and
KSigPDE
come in first and second respectively while
GAK
is in third place;

(2)

on ECG,
KSig
is first and
RWS
is second while
RFSF-DP
and
KSigPDE
are tied for third place,
RFSF-TRP
underperforms on this modality;

(3)

on EEG/MEG,
KSig
and
KSigPDE
are first and second again, while
RFSF-TRP
is third, and
RFSF-DP
somewhat underperforms;

(4)

for HAR datasets,
RWS
is first,
KSig
is second, and
KSigPDE
is third, while
RFSF-DP
and
RFSF-TRP
are tied for fourth place;

(5)

on Motion datasets
KSig
is first,
RFSF-DP
is second, and
RFSF-TRP
is third;

(6)

on Other datasets,
KSig
and
KSigPDE
are tied for first place, while
GAK
comes in second, and
RBF
in third.

Overall, we observe that
KSig
takes undisputed first place, while
KSigPDE
often comes in close second. The performance of
RFSF-TRP
and
RFSF-DP
vary across domains, one often outperforming the other indicating that the way they approximate the signature kernel captures patterns in the data differently. Note that
RWS
outperforms the
RFSF
variants on some data modalities, however, it also often underperforms severely while at least one
RFSF
variation always performs at least moderately well on each data domain.

5.5
Conclusion

We constructed a random kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
for sequences that benefits from

(i)

lifting the original sequence to an infinite-dimensional
RKHS

{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
,

(ii)

linear complexity in sequence length,

(iii)

being with high probability close to the signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
.

Thereby it combines the strength of the signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}
which is to implicitly use the iterated integrals of a sequence that has an infinite-dimensional
RKHS

{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
as state-space with the strength of (unkernelized) signature features
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}}
that only require linear time complexity.
Our main theoretical result extends the theoretical guarantees for translation-invariant kernels on linear spaces to the signature kernel defined on the nonlinear domain
\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
; however, the proofs differ from the classic case and require to analyse the error propagation in tensor space.
A second step is more straightforward, and combines this approach with random projections in finite-dimensions for tensors to reduce the complexity in memory further.
The advantages and disadvantages of the resulting approach are analogous to the classic
RFF
technique on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, namely a reduction of computational complexity by an order for the price of an approximation that only holds with high probability.
As in the classic
RFF
case, our experiments indicate that this is in general a favourable tradeoff.

In the future, it would be interesting both theoretically and empirically to replace the vanilla Monte Carlo integration in the
RFF
construction by block-orthogonal random matrices as done in
[
300
]
.
Further, our random features can also be used to define an unbiased appoximation to the inner product of expected signatures, which has found usecases, among many, in nonparametric hypothesis testing and market regime detection
[
44
,
129
]
, training of generative models
[
199
,
130
]
, and graph representation learning
[
265
]
.

Appendix 5.A
Concentration of measure

5.A.1
Classic inequalities

The following inequalities are classic, and their proofs are in analysis textbooks, e.g.
[
75
,
221
]
. Firstly, Jensen’s inequality is useful for convex (concave) expectations.

Lemma 5.11

(Jensen’s inequality)
.

Let
X
be an integrable random variable, and
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
a convex function, such that
f\left(X\right)
is also integrable. Then, the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle f\left(\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]\right)\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(X\right)\right].

(5.33)

Hölder’s inequality is a generalization of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to
L^{p}
spaces.

Lemma 5.12

(Hölder’s inequality)
.

Let
p,q\geq 1

such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Let
X
and
Y
respectively be
L^{p}
and
L^{q}
integrable random variables, i.e.
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{p}\right]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{q}\right]<\infty
. Then,
XY
is integrable, and it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert XY\right\rvert\right]\leq\mathbb{E}^{1/p}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{p}\right]\mathbb{E}^{1/q}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{q}\right].

(5.34)

Although not inherently a probabilistic inequality, Young’s inequality can be used to decouple products of random variables.

Lemma 5.13

(Young’s inequality)
.

Let
p,q>0
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Then, for every
a,b\geq 0

\displaystyle ab\leq\frac{a^{p}}{p}+\frac{b^{q}}{q}.

(5.35)

Lemma 5.14

(Reverse Young’s inequality)
.

Let
p,q>0
such that
\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}=1
. Then, for every
a\geq 0
and
b>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle ab\geq\frac{a^{p}}{p}-\frac{b^{-q}}{q}.

(5.36)

Proof.

Apply Young’s inequality with
p^{\prime}=\frac{1}{p}
and
q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{p}
to
a^{\prime}=(ab)^{p}
and
b^{\prime}=b^{-p}
.
∎

5.A.2
Subexponential concentration

Next, we state a variation on the well-known Bernstein inequality, which holds for random variables in the subexponential class. The condition (
5.45
) on a random variable
X
, in this case, is formulated as a moment-growth bound, called a Bernstein moment condition. We show in Lemmas
5.21
and
5.22
(in a more general setting) that (
5.45
) is equivalent to the random variable being subexponential. Further, note that the condition itself is given in terms of non-centered random variables, while the statement of the theorem is about their centered counterparts. For similar results, see
[
272
, Sec. 2.2.2]
.

Theorem 5.15

(Bernstein inequality - one-tailed)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent random variables satisfying the following moment-growth condition for some
S,R>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}^{k}\right]\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0ptk\geq 2.

(5.37)

Then, it holds for
\tilde{X}_{i}=X_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{X}_{i}\geq t\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{-t^{2}}{2(nS^{2}+Rt)}\right).

(5.38)

Proof.

We have for
\lambda>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{X}_{i}\geq t\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\exp(-\lambda t)\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{X}_{i}\right)\right]

(5.39)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\exp\left(-\lambda t-\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(\lambda X_{i})\right],

(5.40)

where (a) holds for any
\lambda>0
by the Chernoff bound applied to
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{X}_{i}
, and in (b) we used the independence of
X_{i}
-s. Bounding the moment-generating function of
X_{i}
, one gets

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(\lambda X_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(d)}}}{{=}}1+\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}^{k}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(e)}}}{{\leq}}1+\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]+\frac{S^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{k}R^{k}

(5.41)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(f)}}}{{=}}1+\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]+\frac{S^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(g)}}}{{\leq}}\exp\left(\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]+\frac{S^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R)}\right),

(5.42)

where (d) is due to the Taylor expansion of the exponential function, (e) implied by (
5.37
), (f) holds by the sum of geometric series for any
\lambda<1/R
, (g) follows from the inequality
1+x\leq\exp(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Using this to bound (
5.40
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{X}_{i}\geq t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\lambda t-\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\left(\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]+\frac{S^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R)}\right)

(5.43)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\lambda t+\frac{nS^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R)}\right).

(5.44)

Finally, choosing
\lambda=t/(nS^{2}+Rt)
gives the statement.
∎

chapter 5.16

(Bernstein inequality - two-tailed)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent random variables satisfying the following moment-growth condition for some
S,R>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X_{i}\right\rvert^{k}\right]\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0ptk\geq 2.

(5.45)

Then, it holds for
\tilde{X}_{i}=X_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{X}_{i}\right\rvert\geq t\right]\leq 2\exp\left(\frac{-t^{2}}{2(nS^{2}+Rt)}\right).

(5.46)

Proof.

Applying Theorem
5.15
to
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{X}_{i}
and
-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{X}_{i}
, and combining the two bounds gives the two-tailed result.
∎

5.A.3
\alpha
-exponential concentration

In this section, we introduce a specific class of Orlicz norms for heavy-tailed random variables, which generalizes subexponential distributions.

Definition 5.17

(
\alpha
-exponential Orlicz norm)
.

Let
\alpha>0
and define the function

\displaystyle{}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\quad{}_{\alpha}(x)=\exp\left(x^{\alpha}\right)-1\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0ptx\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.47)

The
\alpha
-exponential Orlicz (quasi-)norm of a random variable
X
is defined as

\displaystyle\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}=\inf\left\{t>0:\mathbb{E}\left[{}_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\left\lvert X\right\rvert}{t}\right)\right]\leq 1\right\},

(5.48)

adhering to the standard convention that
\inf\emptyset=\infty
.

If a random variable
X
satisfies
\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}<\infty
, it is either called an
\alpha
-(sub)exponential random variable (if
\alpha<1
)
[
229
,
100
,
35
]
, or sub-Weibull of order-
\alpha

[
153
,
302
]
.

An alternative characterization of the
\alpha
-exponential norm is the following tail-bound.

Remark 5.18

(Tail bound)
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
X
be a random variable such that
\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}<\infty
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{\alpha}}{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha}}\right).

(5.49)

Proof.

Due to Definition
5.17
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{\alpha}}{\left\lVert X\right\rVert^{\alpha}_{{}_{\alpha}}}\right)\right]\leq 2
, hence by Markov’s inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{\alpha}}{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha}}\geq\frac{\epsilon^{\alpha}}{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha}}\right]\leq e^{-\frac{\epsilon^{\alpha}}{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{\alpha}}{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha})}\right)\right]\leq 2e^{-\frac{\epsilon^{\alpha}}{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha}}}.

(5.50)

∎

Note that although
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}
is often referred to as a norm, it does not satisfy the triangle inequality for
\alpha<1
, although we can still relate the norm of the sum to the sum of the norms.

Lemma 5.19

(Generalized triangle inequality for Orlicz norm)
.

It holds for any random variables
X,Y
and
\alpha>0
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert X+Y\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}\leq C_{\alpha}\left(\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}+\left\lVert Y\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}\right),

(5.51)

where
C_{\alpha}=2^{1/\alpha}
if
\alpha<1
and
1
otherwise.

Proof.

See Lemma A.3. in
[
100
]
.
∎

A useful property of
\alpha
-exponential norms is that they satisfy a Hölder-type inequality.

Lemma 5.20

(Hölder inequality for Orlicz norm)
.

It holds for any random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{k}
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}>0
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}\leq\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{k}\left\lVert X_{i}\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha_{i}}},

(5.52)

where
\alpha=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}^{-1}\right)^{-1}
.

Proof.

See Lemma A.1. in
[
100
]
.
∎

Next, we show in the following two lemmas that a random variable
X
is
\alpha
-exponential if and only if
\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{\alpha}
satisfies a Bernstein moment-growth condition.

Lemma 5.21

(Bernstein condition implies Orlicz norm bound)
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
X
be a random variable that satisfies for
S,R>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{k\alpha}\right]\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0ptk\geq 2.

(5.53)

Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}\leq 2\left(S\vee R\right)^{1/\alpha}.

(5.54)

Proof.

Firstly, due to Jensen’s inequality (Lemma
5.11
) and (
5.53
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{\alpha}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{2\alpha}\right]\leq S.

(5.55)

We proceed similarly to the proof of Theorem
5.15
. For
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp{\frac{\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{\alpha}}{t^{\alpha}}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{=}}1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{\alpha}\right]}{t^{\alpha}}+\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{m\alpha}\right]}{t^{m\alpha}m!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}1+\frac{S}{t^{\alpha}}+\frac{S^{2}}{2t^{2\alpha}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{R}{t^{\alpha}}\right)^{m}

(5.56)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{=}}\underbrace{1+\frac{S}{t^{\alpha}}+\frac{S^{2}}{2t^{\cancel{2}\alpha}}\frac{\cancel{t^{\alpha}}}{t^{\alpha}-R}}_{f(t)},

(5.57)

where (a) is due to the Taylor expansion of the exponential function, (b) is due to (
5.53
) and (
5.55
), (c) is the sum of a geometric series for
R<t^{\alpha}
. Defining
f(t)=1+\frac{S}{t^{\alpha}}\left(1+\frac{S}{2(t^{\alpha}-R)}\right)
and solving for
f(t)\leq 2
leads to the quadratic inequality

\displaystyle 0\leq t^{2\alpha}-(S+R)t^{\alpha}+SR-\frac{S^{2}}{2},

(5.58)

which has roots
t^{\alpha}_{1,2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(S+R\pm\sqrt{\left(S-R\right)^{2}+2S^{2}}\right)
. We discard the left branch, which violates the condition
R<t^{\alpha}
since

\displaystyle t_{2}^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\left(S+R-\sqrt{(S-R)^{2}+2S^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(S+R-\left\lvert S-R\right\rvert\right)=S\wedge R\leq R.

(5.59)

Now, as the inequality
\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}\leq\left\lvert x\right\rvert+\left\lvert y\right\rvert
holds for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we get

\displaystyle t_{1}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(S+R+\sqrt{(S-R)^{2}+2S^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(S+R+\left\lvert S-R\right\rvert+\sqrt{2}S\right)

(5.60)

\displaystyle=S\vee R+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}S\leq 2(S\vee R)

(5.61)

and hence choosing
t\geq\left(2(S\vee R)\right)^{1/\alpha}\geq t_{1}
implies
f(t)\leq 2
, and we are done.
∎

The other direction is proven in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.22

(Finite Orlicz norm implies Bernstein condition)
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
X
be a random variable such that
\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}<\infty
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{k\alpha}\right]\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all }k\geq 2,

(5.62)

where
S=\sqrt{2}\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha}
and
R=\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{k\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{=}}k!\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{k\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\left\lvert X\right\rvert}{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}}\right)^{k\alpha}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}k!\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{k\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{\alpha}}{\left\lVert X\right\rVert^{\alpha}_{{}_{\alpha}}}\right)-1\right]

(5.63)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{\leq}}k!\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{k\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(d)}}}{{=}}\frac{k!\left(\sqrt{2}\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha}\right)^{2}\left(\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}^{\alpha}\right)^{k-2}}{2},

(5.64)

where (a) is simply multiplying and dividing by the same values, (b) is the inequality
1+x^{k}/k!\leq e^{x}
for
x>0
, (c) follows from Definition
5.17
, (d) is reorganizing terms to the required form.
∎

We adapt the following concentration inequality from
[
153
]
for
\alpha
-exponential summation.

Theorem 5.23

(Concentration inequality for
\alpha
-subexponential summation)
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent, centered random variables with
\left\lVert X_{i}\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}\leq M_{\alpha}
for all
i\in[n]
. Then, it holds for
t>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right\rvert\geq C_{\alpha}\left(2\sqrt{nt}+\sqrt{2}4^{1/\alpha}t^{1/\alpha}\right)\right]\leq 2e^{-t},

(5.65)

where
C_{\alpha}=\sqrt{8}e^{4}(2\pi)^{1/4}e^{1/24}(2e/\alpha)^{1/\alpha}M_{\alpha}
. Alternatively, it holds for
\epsilon>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\min\left\{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{n}C_{\alpha}}\right)^{2},\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{8}C_{\alpha}}\right)^{\alpha}\right\}\right).

(5.66)

Proof.

The inequality (
5.65
) follows directly from
[
153
, Theorem 3.1]
.

To show (
5.66
), let
g_{1}(t)=2C_{\alpha}\sqrt{nt}
and
g_{2}(t)=\sqrt{2}C_{\alpha}4^{1/\alpha}t^{1/\alpha}
. Now, for
t>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right\rvert\geq 2\left(g_{1}(t)\vee g_{2}(t)\right)\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right\rvert\geq g_{1}(t)+g_{2}(t)\right]\leq 2e^{-t},

(5.67)

which is equivalently written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}X\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\left(g^{-1}_{1}(\epsilon/2)\wedge g^{-1}_{2}(\epsilon/2)\right)\right).

(5.68)

∎

5.A.4
Hypercontractivity

Here we provide an alternative approach for the concentration of heavy-tailed random variables, specifically for polynomials of Gaussian random variables. The following lemma states a moment bound for such Gaussian chaoses, considered in e.g.
[
134
,
24
]
.

Lemma 5.24

(Moment bound for Gaussian polynomial)
.

Consider a degree-
p
polynomial
f(X)=f(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of independent centered Gaussian random variables,
X_{1},\dots X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Then, for all
k\geq 2

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/k}\left[\left\lvert f(X)\right\rvert^{k}\right]\leq(k-1)^{p/2}\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\left[\left\lvert f(X)\right\rvert^{2}\right].

(5.69)

Theorem 5.25

(Concentration inequality for Gaussian polynomial)
.

Consider a degree-
p
polynomial
f(X)=f(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of independent centered Gaussian random variables,
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Then, for
p\geq 2
and
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert f(X)-\mathbb{E}\left[f(X)\right]\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{2/p}}{2\sqrt{2}e\mathbb{V}^{1/p}\left[f(X)\right]}\right),

(5.70)

where
\mathbb{V}\left[\cdot\right]
denotes the variance of a random variable.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathbb{E}\left[f(X)\right]=0
and
\mathbb{V}\left[f(X)\right]=1
.
Since Lemma
5.24
holds, we have for
p,k\geq 2

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert f(X)\right\rvert^{2k/p}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}^{2/p}\left[\left\lvert f(X)\right\rvert^{k}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}(k-1)^{k}\leq k^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{\leq}}k!e^{k}\leq\frac{k!(\sqrt{2}e)^{2}e^{k-2}}{2},

(5.71)

where (a) holds due to Jensen inequality since
(\cdot)^{2/p}
is concave, (b) is Lemma
5.24
, and (c) is due to Stirling’s approximation. Hence,
\left\lvert f(X)\right\rvert^{2/p}
satisfies a Bernstein condition with
S=\sqrt{2}e
and
R=e
. Therefore, by Lemma
5.21
, we have
\left\lVert f(X)\right\rVert_{{}_{2/p}}\leq(2\sqrt{2}e)^{p/2}
.

Then, by Remark
5.18
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert f(X)\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{2/p}}{\left\lVert f(X)\right\rVert_{{}_{2/p}}^{2/p}}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{2/p}}{2\sqrt{2}e}\right).

(5.72)

∎

Appendix 5.B
Random Fourier Features

[
209
]
provides a uniform bound for the
RFF
error over a compact and convex domain
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with diameter
\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert
for some absolute constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq C\left(\frac{\sigma_{{\Lambda}}\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert}{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\exp\left(-\frac{-{\tilde{d}}\epsilon^{2}}{4(d+2)}\right),

(5.73)

where
\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim{\Lambda}}\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert^{2}\right]
, and
[
253
]
shows that
C\leq 66
. Equation (
5.73
) implies
[
244
, Sec. 2]

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert=O_{p}\left(\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert\sqrt{\tilde{d}^{-1}\log\tilde{d}}\right),

(5.74)

where the
X_{n}=O_{p}(a_{n})
notation for
a_{n}>0
refers to
\lim_{C\to 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\left\lvert X_{n}\right\rvert}{a_{n}}>C\right]=0
.

The converse result
6
6

6

These error guarantees can also be improved exponentially both for the approximation of kernel values
[
244
]
and kernel derivatives
[
255
,
35
]
in terms of the size of the domain where it holds, i.e.
\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert
.
for
RFF
derivatives was shown in
[
244
, Thm. 5]
. The idea of the proof is to cover the input domain by an
\epsilon
-net, and control the approximation error on the centers, while simultaneously controlling the Lipschitz constant of the error to get the bound to hold uniformly. We provide an adapted version with two main differences:

(1)

using the Bernstein inequality from Corollary
5.16
, where the Bernstein condition is given in terms of non-centered random variables, and

(2)

using the covering numbers of
[
56
]
.

We will use this theorem in proving Theorem
5.3
for controlling the approximation error of the derivatives of
RFF
s.

Given
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we denote
\left\lvert\mathbf{p}\right\rvert=p_{1}+\ldots+p_{d}
, for a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
the
\mathbf{p}
-th partial derivative by
\partial^{\mathbf{p}}f(\mathbf{z})=\frac{\partial^{\left\lvert\mathbf{p}\right\rvert}f(\mathbf{z})}{\partial^{p_{1}}z_{1}\ldots\partial^{p_{d}}z_{d}}
, for a vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the
\mathbf{p}
-th power by
\mathbf{w}^{\mathbf{p}}=w_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdots w_{d}^{p_{d}}
.

Theorem 5.26

(Concentration inequality for
RFF
kernel derivatives)
.

Let
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant kernel such that
\mathbf{z}\mapsto\nabla\left[\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})\right]
is continuous. Let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a compact and convex domain with diameter
\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert
, and denote by
D_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathcal{X}}=\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lVert\nabla\left[\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})\right]\right\rVert_{2}
, where
\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}=\left\{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\,|\,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}\right\}
.
Let
\Lambda
be the spectral measure of
\mathsf{k}
satisfying that
E_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathbf{w}^{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}}\right\rvert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}\right]<\infty
, and the Bernstein moment condition for some
S,R>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathbf{w}^{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}}\right\rvert^{k}\right]\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0ptk\geq 2.

(5.75)

Then, for
C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathcal{X}}=\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert(D_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathcal{X}}+E_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}})
and
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lvert\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 16\left(\frac{C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathcal{X}}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\epsilon^{2}}{4(d+1)(2S^{2}+R\epsilon)}\right).

(5.76)

Proof.

We adapt the proof of
[
209
,
244
]
. Note that as
\mathcal{X}
is compact, so is
\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}
, and it can be covered by an
\epsilon
-net of at most
T=(4\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert/r)^{d}
balls of radius
r>0

[
56
, Prop. 5]
with centers
\mathbf{z}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{T}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}
. Since for all
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}
there exists
i\in\{1,\dots,T\}
such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leq r
, it holds for
f(\mathbf{z})=\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})-\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})
and
L_{f}=\sup_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lVert\nabla f(\mathbf{s})\right\rVert_{2}
that

\displaystyle\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z})-f(\mathbf{z}_{i})\right\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{\leq}}\sup_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lVert\nabla f(\mathbf{s})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}\sup_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lVert\nabla f(\mathbf{s})\right\rVert_{2}r=L_{f}r,

(5.77)

where (a) is due to the mean-value theorem followed by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and (b) is since
\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}_{i}\right\rVert\leq r
. Now, by triangle inequality, it holds for any
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla f(\mathbf{z})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\nabla\left[\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})\right]\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\nabla\left[\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})\right]\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\nabla\left[\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})\right]\right\rVert_{2}+D_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathcal{X}}.

(5.78)

Differentiating
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})
as defined in (
5.12
), we get by the chain rule and triangle inequality that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla\left[\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})\right]\right\rVert_{2}=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\left\lVert\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}\mathbf{w}_{j}\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}}\cos^{(\left\lvert\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}\right\rvert)}(\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{z})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}\left\lvert\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}}\right\rvert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2},

(5.79)

where
\cos^{(n)}
denotes the
n
-th derivative of
\cos
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The main idea of the proof is then

\displaystyle\bigcap_{i=1}^{T}\{\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z}_{i})\right\rvert<\epsilon/2\}\bigcap\{L_{f}<\epsilon/2r\}\subseteq\{\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z})\right\rvert<\epsilon\,|\,\forall\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}\},

(5.80)

since
\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z})\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z})-f(\mathbf{z}_{i})\right\rvert+\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z}_{i})\right\rvert
and (
5.77
) holds. Therefore, taking the complement and bounding the union, we get our governing inequality for the uniform error over
\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z}_{i})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon/2\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[L_{f}\geq\epsilon/2r\right].

(5.81)

Now, we need to bound all probabilities on the RHS. First, we deal with
L_{f}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[L_{f}\geq\epsilon/2r\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\left[L_{f}\right]\frac{2r}{\epsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(d)}}}{{\leq}}\left(D_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathcal{X}}+\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}}\right\rvert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\right]\right)\frac{2r}{\epsilon}

(5.82)

\displaystyle\leq\left(D_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathcal{X}}+E_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\right)\frac{2r}{\epsilon},

(5.83)

where (c) is Markov’s inequality, (d) is (
5.78
) and (
5.79
).
To deal with the centers in (
5.81
), note
\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})
can be written as a sample average of
\tilde{d}

i.i.d.
terms as per (
5.12
) since

\displaystyle\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})=\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\left(\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}}\cos(\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{z})\right)=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}}\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\cos(\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{z})

(5.84)

so that the Bernstein inequality (Cor.
5.16
) is applicable. For
j=1,\dots,\tilde{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}\sim\Lambda}{\left\lvert\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\cos\left(\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{z}\right)\right\rvert^{m}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}\sim\Lambda}{\left\lvert\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}}\cos^{(\left\lvert\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}\right\rvert)}(\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{z})\right\rvert^{m}}\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}\sim\Lambda}{\left\lvert\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}}\right\rvert^{m}}\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2},

(5.85)

and that
f(\mathbf{z})=\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})-\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})=\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})-\mathbb{E}\left[\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})\right]
by the dominated convergence theorem.
Hence, we may call the Bernstein inequality (Cor.
5.16
) to control
f(\mathbf{z}_{i})
so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z}_{i})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon/2\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{z})-\partial^{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon/2\right]\leq 2\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\epsilon^{2}}{4(2S^{2}+R\epsilon)}\right).

(5.86)

Combining the bounds for
\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z}_{i})\right\rvert
(
5.86
), and for
L_{f}
(
5.83
), into (
5.81
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{M}_{\Delta}}\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2\left(\frac{4\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert}{r}\right)^{d}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\epsilon^{2}}{4(2S^{2}+R\epsilon)}\right)+\left(D_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathcal{X}}+E_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\right)\frac{2r}{\epsilon},

(5.87)

which has the form
g(r)=\tau_{1}r^{-d}+\tau_{2}r
, that is minimized by choosing
r^{\star}=(d\tau_{1}/\tau_{2})^{\frac{1}{d+1}}
. This choice sets it to the form
g(r^{\star})=\tau_{1}^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\tau_{2}^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\left(d^{\frac{1}{d+1}}+d^{\frac{-d}{d+1}}\right)
, so that by substituting back in

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{M}_{\Delta}}\left\lvert f(\mathbf{z})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]

\displaystyle\leq F_{d}2^{\frac{3d+1}{d+1}}\left(\frac{\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert(D_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathcal{X}}+E_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}})}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\epsilon^{2}}{4(d+1)(2S^{2}+R\epsilon)}\right).

(5.88)

Finally, we note that for
d\geq 1
,
F_{d}=d^{\frac{1}{d+1}}+d^{\frac{-d}{d+1}}\leq 2
and
2^{\frac{3d+1}{d+1}}\leq 8
.
∎

Appendix 5.C
Bounds on Signature Kernels

We first set the ground for proving our main theorems by introducing the notion of
L
-Lipschitz kernels to help control distance distortions in the feature space. This subclass of kernels will be useful for us in relating the
1
-variation of sequences in feature space to that in the input space.
After this, we will prove various smaller lemmas and supplementary results for signature kernels, which will lead up to Lemma
5.35
, which is our main tool for proving Theorem
5.3
, and it relates the concentration of our
RFSF
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}}
to the second derivatives of the
RFF
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}
. Then, the proof of Theorem
5.3
quantifying the concentration of the
RFSF
kernel,
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}}
, will follow from putting together Lemma
5.35
with Theorem
5.26
, and we will also make use of the Bernstein inequality from Theorem
5.15
. The proof of Theorem
5.6
for the RFSF-DP kernel,
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}}
, will follow by combining the results of this section with
\alpha
-exponential concentration, in particular, Theorem
5.23
. Finally, Theorem
5.9
for the RFSF-TRP kernel,
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}}
, will be proven using lemmas from this section, and the hypercontractivity result from Theorem
5.25
.

5.C.1
Distance bounds in the RKHS

Definition 5.27

(Lipschitz kernel)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be a metric space. We call a kernel
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
with RKHS
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
an
L
-Lipschitz kernel over
\mathcal{X}
for some
L>0
if it holds for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}}-\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}=\sqrt{\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x})+\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y})-2\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}\leq Ld(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),

(5.89)

where
\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}}=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\cdot),\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{y},\cdot)\in{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
.

Example 5.28

(Finite 2
nd
spectral moment implies Lipschitz)
.

Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded and translation-invariant kernel with RKHS
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
and spectral measure
\Lambda
, such that
\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\right]<\infty
. Then, it holds that
\mathsf{k}
is
\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\top}\right]\right\rVert_{2}^{1/2}
-Lipschitz, so for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
one has

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}}-\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}\leq\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\top}\right]\right\rVert_{2}^{1/2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}.

(5.90)

Proof.

We have for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}}-\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{=}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x})+\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y})-2\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{=}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2-2\exp\left(i\mathbf{w}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\right)\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda(\mathbf{w})

(5.91)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{=}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2-2\cos\left(\mathbf{w}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\right)\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda(\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(d)}}}{{\leq}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\mathbf{w}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda(\mathbf{w})

(5.92)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(e)}}}{{=}}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\top}\right](\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(f)}}}{{\leq}}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\top}\right]\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},

(5.93)

where (a) holds due to the reproducing property, (b) is Bochner’s theorem, (c) is because the imaginary part of the integral evaluates to
0
as the kernel is real-valued, (d) is due to the inequality
1-t^{2}/2\leq\cos(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, (e) is because
\left(\mathbf{w}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\right)^{2}=(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})^{\top}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})
, and (f) is Cauchy-Schwarz inequality combined with the definition of the spectral norm.
∎

Example 5.29

(Random Lipschitz bound for
RFF
)
.

Let
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be an
RFF
kernel defined as in (
5.11
) corresponding to some spectral measure
\Lambda
, and let
\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}
denote its feature space corresponding to the
RFF
map
\tilde{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}
defined as in (
5.11
), so that given
\mathbf{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{\tilde{d}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda
, we have for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{k}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}\cos\left(\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\right).

(5.94)

Let
\mathbf{W}=(\mathbf{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{\tilde{d}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\tilde{d}}
be the random matrix with column vectors
\mathbf{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{\tilde{d}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda
. Then,
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}
is
\left(\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}\right\rVert_{2}}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}\right)
-Lipschitz, so that for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have the inequality

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{y})\right\rVert_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}}\leq\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}\right\rVert_{2}}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}.

(5.95)

Proof.

The proof follows analogously to that of Example
5.28
. Let
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{y})\right\rVert_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{=}}2-\frac{2}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}}\cos(\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}}\left(\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\right)^{2}

(5.96)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(d)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert^{2}_{2},

(5.97)

where (a) is due to the cosine identity
\cos(a-b)=\cos(a)\cos(b)+\sin(a)\sin(b)
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, (b) is due to the inequality
1-t^{2}/2\leq\cos(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, (c) is because
\left(\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\right)^{2}=(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})^{\top}\mathbf{w}_{i}\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})
and
\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}}\mathbf{w}_{i}\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\top}
, (d) is due to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality combined with the definition of the spectral norm.
∎

Bounds for the Signature Kernel

A well-known property of signature features that they decay factorially fast with respect to the tensor level
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 5.30

(Norm bound for signature features)
.

Let
L>0
and
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be an
L
-Lipschitz kernel with RKHS
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}
. Then, we have for the level-
m
signature features
\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x})
of the sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{\otimes m}}\leq\frac{\left(L\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{{1\texttt{-var}}}\right)^{m}}{m!}.

(5.98)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\varphi_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{\otimes m}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes m}}

(5.99)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\left\lVert\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\cdots\left\lVert\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

(5.100)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}L^{m}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\left\lVert\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\right\rVert_{2}\cdots\left\lVert\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}\right\rVert_{2}

(5.101)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\left(L\sum_{i=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\left\lVert\delta\mathbf{x}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\right)^{m}}{m!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(d)}}}{{=}}\frac{\left(L\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\right)^{m}}{m!},

(5.102)

where (a) follows from triangle inequality followed by factorizing the tensor norm, (b) is the
L
-Lipschitzness property, (c) from completing the multinomial expansion and normalizing by the number of permutations (note that
\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})
contains a single permutation of all such multi-indices that have nonrepeating entries), and (d) is the definition of sequence
1
-variation.
∎

The following bound for the signature kernel is a direct consequence of the previous lemma.

chapter 5.31

(Upper bound for signature kernel)
.

Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be an
L
-Lipschitz kernel, and
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
the level-
m
signature kernel built from
\mathsf{k}
. Then, we have the following bound for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left(L^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\right)^{m}}{(m!)^{2}}.

(5.103)

Proof.

Note that without a kernel trick,
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}
is written for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as the inner product

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\left\langle\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}},\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})}\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{\otimes m}}\right\rvert

(5.104)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{\leq}}\left\lVert\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{\otimes m}}\left\lVert\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})}\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}^{\otimes m}}

(5.105)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\left(L^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\right)^{m}}{(m!)^{2}},

(5.106)

where (a) follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and (b) is implied by Lemma
5.30
.
∎

A similar upper bound to Lemma
5.30
also holds for the
RFSF
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
, that now depends on the norms of the random matrices
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(m)}
, hence is itself random.

Lemma 5.32

(Random norm bound for
RFSF
)
.

Let
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}_{\mathsf{S}}
be the level-
m

RFSF
map defined as in (
5.13
) built from some spectral measure
\Lambda
. Then, we have for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}}^{\otimes m}}\leq\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}\cdots\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{2}}{m!}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}\right)^{m},

(5.107)

where
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(m)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}
are random matrices sampled from
\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows analogously to Lemma
5.30
. We have for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}}^{\otimes m}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}})\right\rVert_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}^{\otimes m}}

(5.108)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\left\lVert\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}})\right\rVert_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}}\cdots\left\lVert\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}})\right\rVert_{\tilde{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}}

(5.109)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}\cdots\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{2}}{\tilde{d}^{m/2}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\left\lVert\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\right\rVert_{2}\cdots\left\lVert\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}}\right\rVert_{2}

(5.110)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}\cdots\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{2}}{\tilde{d}^{m/2}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{{1\texttt{-var}}}^{m}}{m!},

(5.111)

where (a) is the triangle inequality and factorization of tensor norm, (b) is using the Lipschitzness of
RFF
s from Example
5.29
, (c) is the same as steps (c)-(d) in Lemma
5.30
.
∎

Then, the following is again an application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

chapter 5.33

(Random upper bound for
RFSF
kernel)
.

Let
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
be the level-
m

RFSF
kernel defined as in (
5.15
) built from some spectral measure
\Lambda
. Then, for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdots\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{(m!)^{2}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}}{\tilde{d}}\right)^{m}

(5.112)

where
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(m)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}
are random matrices sampled from
\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}
.

The following lemma does not concern signatures, but will be useful to us later in the proof of Lemma
5.35
by providing a mean-value theorem for the cross-differencing operator
\delta_{i,j}
.

Lemma 5.34

(2
nd
order mean-value theorem)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a twice differentiable function, and
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex and compact set. Then, we have for any
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}
that

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v})-f(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{y})+f(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\leq\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}f(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}\right\rVert_{2},

(5.113)

where
\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}f(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})=\left(\frac{\partial^{2}f(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})}{\partial s_{i}\partial t_{j}}\right)_{i,j=1}^{d}
refers to the submatrix of the Hessian of cross-derivatives.

Proof.

Keeping
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}
as fixed, we may define
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
as
g(\cdot)=f(\cdot,\mathbf{y})-f(\cdot,\mathbf{v})
, so that the expression above can be written as
g(\mathbf{x})-g(\mathbf{u})
, and by the convexity of
\mathcal{X}
, we may apply the mean-value theorem to find that
\exists s\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{x})-g(\mathbf{u})=\left\langle\nabla g(s\mathbf{x}+(1-s)\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u}\right\rangle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\nabla g(\mathbf{s})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u}\right\rVert_{2},

(5.114)

where
\nabla g(\mathbf{s})=\left(\frac{\partial g(\mathbf{s})}{\partial s_{i}}\right)_{i=1}^{d}
denotes the gradient of
g
, while the second inequality follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the compactness of
\mathcal{X}
(so that the
\sup
exists). Also note that
\nabla g(\mathbf{s})=\partial_{\mathbf{s}}f(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{y})-\partial_{\mathbf{s}}f(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{v})
, so defining
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
h(\cdot)=\partial_{\mathbf{s}}f(\mathbf{s},\cdot)
and applying the vector-valued mean-value inequality
[
221
, Thm. 9.19]
to
h
gives that
\exists t\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert h(\mathbf{y})-h(\mathbf{v})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\operatorname{J}_{h}(t\mathbf{y}+(1-t)\mathbf{v})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sup_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\operatorname{J}_{h}(\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}\right\rVert_{2},

(5.115)

where
\operatorname{J}_{h}(\mathbf{t})=\left(\frac{\partial h_{i}(\mathbf{t})}{\partial t_{j}}\right)_{i,j=1}^{d}
refers to the Jacobian of
h
. Putting the inequalities (
5.114
) and (
5.115
) together and substituting back the function
f
gives the desired result.
∎

This is our final lemma in our exposition of supplementary results about the (random) signature kernels, and it will be our main tool for proving Theorem
5.3
.

Lemma 5.35

(Uniform upper bound for deviation of
RFSF
kernel)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex and compact set,
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant
L
-Lipschitz kernel and
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
the corresponding
RFF
kernel.
Then, the level-
m
(
m\in\mathbb{N}
) signature and
RFSF
kernels are uniformly close for
V>0
by

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert

(5.116)

\displaystyle\leq V^{2m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{L^{2(m-k)}}{\tilde{d}^{k-1}((k-1)!)^{2}}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdots\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(k-1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2},

(5.117)

where
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}
are independent
RFF
kernels with weights
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(m)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}
, and
\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}f(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})=\left(\frac{\partial^{2}f(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})}{\partial s_{i}\partial t_{j}}\right)_{i,j=1}^{d}
for a twice-differentiable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

First of all, by Lemma
5.31
and Lemma
5.33
, the supremum exists.
In the following, given a sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, we denote its
0:l
slice for some
l\in[{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}]
by
\mathbf{x}_{:l}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{l})
.
Then, it holds for any
m\geq 1
recursively for the signature kernel that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1})\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l}),

(5.118)

and analogously for the
RFSF
kernel that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1})\delta_{k,l}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l}).

(5.119)

Combining these recursions together, we have for the uniform error that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{m}=

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert

(5.120)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1})\delta_{k,l}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1})\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert

(5.121)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1})(\delta_{k,l}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})-\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l}))\right\rvert

(5.122)

\displaystyle+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}(\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1}))\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert

(5.123)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1})\right\rvert\left\lvert\delta_{k,l}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})-\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert

(5.124)

\displaystyle+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1},\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1})\right\rvert\left\lvert\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert

(5.125)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert}_{\text{(i)}}\underbrace{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lvert\delta_{k,l}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})-\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert}_{\text{(ii)}}

(5.126)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert}_{\text{(iii)}}\underbrace{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lvert\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert}_{\text{(iv)}},

(5.127)

where (a) follows from adding and subtracting the cross-terms and applying triangle inequality, (b) follows from applying triangle inequality over the summations, (c) follows from noting that if
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V
then so is
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}_{0:l-1}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V
for
k\in[{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}]
and
l\in[{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}]
, and thus justifiably pulling out the supremums.

Now, we deal with terms (i)–(iv) individually. For (i), we have Corollary
5.33
, so

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m-1}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdots\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(m-1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{((m-1)!)^{2}}\left(\frac{V^{2}}{\tilde{d}}\right)^{m-1}.

(5.128)

To deal with (ii), we can apply Lemma
5.34
with
f=\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}-\mathsf{k}
to get

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lvert\delta_{k,l}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})-\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert

(5.129)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lVert\delta\mathbf{x}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\delta\mathbf{y}_{l}\right\rVert_{2}

(5.130)

\displaystyle\leq V^{2}\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}.

(5.131)

For (iii), we note that it is simply
\epsilon_{m-1}
.
Finally, we can write (iv) as an inner product and apply Cauchy-Schwarz and
L
-Lipschitzness of
\mathsf{k}
so that

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lvert\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lvert\left\langle\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{k}},\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{l}}\right\rangle\right\rvert

(5.132)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lVert\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{x}_{k}}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}\left\lVert\delta\mathsf{k}_{\mathbf{y}_{l}}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{k}}}}\leq L^{2}V^{2}.

(5.133)

Putting equations (
5.128
), (
5.131
), (
5.133
) together in (
5.127
), we get that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{m}=

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert

(5.134)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{V^{2m}}{\tilde{d}^{m-1}((m-1)!)^{2}}\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{m}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}\prod_{p=1}^{m-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+L^{2}V^{2}\epsilon_{m-1},

(5.135)

which gives us a recursion for estimating
\epsilon_{m}
. The initial step,
m=1
, can be estimated by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert

(5.136)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\delta_{k,l}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})-\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert

(5.137)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}}\sum_{l=0}^{{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}}\left\lvert\delta_{k,l}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})-\delta_{k,l}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{l})\right\rvert

(5.138)

\displaystyle\leq V^{2}\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{1}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2},

(5.139)

which is actually analogous to (
5.135
) since
\epsilon_{0}=0
. Now, we may unroll the recursion (
5.135
) with the initial condition (
5.139
), and we get

\displaystyle\epsilon_{m}\leq V^{2m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{L^{2(m-k)}}{\tilde{d}^{k-1}((k-1)!)^{2}}\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}\prod_{p=1}^{k-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(5.140)

∎

5.C.2
Proofs of main concentration results

Here, we provide proofs of the main concentration results, i.e. Theorems
5.3
,
5.6
,
5.9
, respectively under Theorems
5.36
,
5.37
,
5.38
.

Theorem 5.36

(Concentration inequality for
RFSF
kernel)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a compact and convex set with diameter
\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}=\{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}:\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}\}
. Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant kernel with spectral measure
\Lambda
, which satisfies for some
S,R>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}\right\rvert^{2k}\right]\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0pti\in[d]\hskip 3.0pt\text{and}\hskip 3.0ptk\geq 2.

(5.141)

Then, the following quantities are finite:
\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right]
,
L=\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\top}\right]\right\rVert_{2}^{1/2}
,
E_{i,j}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}\right\rvert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}\right]
and
D_{i,j}=\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lVert\nabla\left[\frac{\partial^{2}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{j}}\right]\right\rVert_{2}
for
i,j\in[d]
. Further, for any maximal sequence
1
-variation
V>0
, and signature level
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, it holds for the level-
m

RFSF
kernel
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
defined as in (
5.15
) and the signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
defined as in (
5.9
) for
\epsilon>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq

(5.142)

\displaystyle\leq m\begin{cases}\left(C_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\beta_{d,m,V}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}+d\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)(S^{2}+R)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{2}\right)\quad&\text{for}\hskip 3.0pt\epsilon<\beta_{d,m,V}\\
\left(C_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\beta_{d,m,V}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{(d+1)m}}+d\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)(S^{2}+R)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m}}\right)\quad&\text{for}\hskip 3.0pt\epsilon\geq\beta_{d,m,V},\end{cases}

(5.143)

where
C_{d,\mathcal{X}}=2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}16\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\left(D_{i,j}+E_{i,j}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}
and
\beta_{d,m,V}=m\left(2V^{2}\left(L^{2}\vee 1\right)\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)\right)^{m}
.

Proof.

Finite quantities.

To start off with, due to (
5.141
) with
m=2
and Jensen’s inequality (Lemma
5.11
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}\right\rvert^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}\right\rvert^{4}\right]<\infty\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0pti\in\left[d\right].

(5.144)

Hence, by linearity of the expectation
\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert^{2}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[w_{i}^{2}\right]<\infty
. Next, due to Hölder’s inequality (Lemma
5.12
), it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[w_{i}w_{j}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}\right\rvert\right]\leq\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}\right\rvert^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\left[\left\lvert w_{j}\right\rvert^{2}\right]<\infty\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0pti,j\in\left[d\right],

(5.145)

therefore
L<\infty
. Further, applying Hölder’s inequality twice followed by Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}w_{k}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{2/3}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}\right\rvert^{3/2}\right]\mathbb{E}^{1/3}\left[\left\lvert w_{k}\right\rvert^{3}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}^{1/3}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}\right\rvert^{3}\right]\mathbb{E}^{1/3}\left[\left\lvert w_{j}\right\rvert^{3}\right]\mathbb{E}^{1/3}\left[\left\lvert w_{k}\right\rvert^{3}\right]

(5.146)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{1/6}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}\right\rvert^{6}\right]\mathbb{E}^{1/6}\left[\left\lvert w_{j}\right\rvert^{6}\right]\mathbb{E}^{1/6}\left[\left\lvert w_{k}\right\rvert^{6}\right]<\infty\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0pti,j,k\in\left[d\right],

(5.147)

which is finite due to (
5.141
) with
m=3
. Now, because of the
\ell^{1}
-
\ell^{2}
norm inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}\right\rvert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}\right\rvert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}\right]=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}w_{k}\right\rvert\right]<\infty,

(5.148)

hence
\bar{E}<\infty
. Next, as per
[
230
, Thm. 1.2.1.(iii)]
, as (
5.147
) holds for all
i,j,k\in\left[d\right]
,
\mathsf{k}
is
3
-times continuously differentiable, which combined with the compactness of
\mathcal{X}
, hence that of
\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}
, gives
\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lvert\frac{\partial^{3}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{j}\partial z_{k}}\right\rvert<\infty
. Finally, from the
\ell^{1}
-
\ell^{2}
inequality again, we get that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lVert\nabla\left[\frac{\partial^{2}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{j}}\right]\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lVert\nabla\left[\frac{\partial^{2}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{j}}\right]\right\rVert_{1}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lvert\frac{\partial^{3}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{j}\partial z_{k}}\right\rvert<\infty,

(5.149)

which shows the finiteness of
\bar{D}
. This finishes showing that the stated quantities are finite.

Splitting the bound.

To start proving our main inequality, first note that as per Example
5.28
,
\mathsf{k}
is
L
-Lipschitz (see Def.
5.27
). Hence, Lemma
5.35
yields that

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert

(5.150)

\displaystyle\leq V^{2m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{L^{2(m-k)}}{\tilde{d}^{k-1}((k-1)!)^{2}}\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial^{2}_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}\prod_{p=1}^{k-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(5.151)

We bound the summand in the previous line in probability for each
k\in\left[m\right]
.
For brevity, denote
\alpha_{m,k}=\frac{V^{2m}L^{2(m-k)}}{((k-1)!)^{2}}
, and first consider the case
k\geq 2
, so that we have

\displaystyle P_{k}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\alpha_{m,k}}{\tilde{d}^{k-1}}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdots\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(k-1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}\geq\epsilon\right]

(5.152)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{P}\left[\alpha_{m,k}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\ldots+\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(k-1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\tilde{d}(k-1)}\right)^{k-1}\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}\geq\epsilon\right]

(5.153)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{=}}\mathbb{P}\left[\underbrace{\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\ldots+\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(k-1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\tilde{d}(k-1)}}_{(A_{k})}\underbrace{\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}^{\frac{1}{k-1}}}_{(B_{k})}\geq\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{k-1}}\right],

(5.154)

where in (a) we used the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality, and in (b) we divided both sides by
\alpha_{m,k}
and took the
(k-1)
th root.
Further, setting
t=\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{k-1}}
, we have for
\gamma>0

\displaystyle P_{k}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[A_{k}\cdot B_{k}\geq t\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{P}\left[\left(A_{k}-\gamma\right)B_{k}\geq\frac{t}{2}\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[B_{k}\geq\frac{t}{2\gamma}\right]

(5.155)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(d)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau>0}\left\{\mathbb{P}\left[A_{k}-\gamma\geq\frac{\tau}{2}\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[B_{k}\geq\frac{t}{\tau}\right]\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left[B_{k}\geq\frac{t}{2\gamma}\right],

(5.156)

where in (c) we added and subtracted
\gamma B_{k}
and applied a union bound, while in (d) we combined a union bound with the relation
\{XY\geq\epsilon\}\subseteq\{X\geq\tau\}\bigcup\{Y\geq\epsilon/\tau\}
which holds for any
\tau>0
.

Our aim is now to obtain good probabilistic bounds on
A_{k}
and
B_{k}
to use in (
5.156
) with the specific choice of
\gamma=\mathbb{E}\left[A_{k}\right]=\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}
.

Bounding
A_{k}
.

By the inequality between the spectral and Frobenius norms, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{k-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{p=1}^{k-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{p=1}^{k-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}\left(w_{i,j}^{(p)}\right)^{2}.

(5.157)

Now note that
w_{i,j}^{(p)}
are
i.i.d.
copies of the
i
th
marginal of
\Lambda
for all
j\in\left[\tilde{d}\right]
and
p\in\left[k-1\right]
, so that via (
5.141
)
\left(w_{i,j}^{(p)}\right)^{2}
satisfies the Bernstein moment condition

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(w_{i,j}^{(p)}\right)^{2k}\right]\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0pti\in[d],j\in[\tilde{d}],p\in[m],k\geq 2.

(5.158)

Hence, we may apply the Bernstein inequality from Theorem
5.15
so that for
i\in\left[d\right]

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{d}(k-1)}\sum_{p=1}^{k-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}\left(w_{i,j}^{(p)}\right)^{2}-\sigma_{i}^{2}\geq\epsilon\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}(k-1)\epsilon^{2}}{2(S^{2}+R\epsilon)}\right),

(5.159)

where
\sigma_{i}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[w_{i}^{2}\right]
.
Combining these bounds for all
i\in\left[d\right]
and denoting
\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sigma_{i}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[A_{k}-\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\geq\epsilon\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{d}(k-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{p=1}^{k-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}\left(w_{i,j}^{(p)}\right)^{2}-\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\geq\epsilon\right]

(5.160)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{d}(k-1)}\sum_{p=1}^{k-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}\left(w_{i,j}^{(p)}\right)^{2}-\sigma_{i}^{2}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{d}\right]

(5.161)

\displaystyle\leq d\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}(k-1)\left(\frac{\epsilon}{d}\right)^{2}}{2\left(S^{2}+R\frac{\epsilon}{d}\right)}\right).

(5.162)

Hence, we have the required probabilistic bound for the term in (
5.156
) containing
A_{k}
.

Bounding
B_{k}
.

One can bound the spectral norm by the max norm so that

\displaystyle B_{k}^{k-1}=\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{\max}

(5.163)

\displaystyle=\max_{i,j=1,\dots,d}\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lvert\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})}{\partial s_{i}\partial t_{j}}-\frac{\partial^{2}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})}{\partial s_{i}\partial t_{j}}\right\rvert.

(5.164)

Let
i,j\in\left[d\right]
and denote
E_{i,j}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}\right\rvert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}\right]
and
D_{i,j}=\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Delta}}\left\lVert\nabla\left[\partial^{\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{z})\right]\right\rVert_{2}
, which are finite as previously shown. Due to Hölder’s inequality (Lemma
5.12
) and (
5.141
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}w_{j}\right\rvert^{m}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\left[w_{i}^{2m}\right]\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\left[w_{j}^{2m}\right]<\infty.

(5.165)

Recall that
\mathsf{k}
is
3
-times continuously differentiable, so that the conditions required by Theorem
5.26
are satisfied, that we now call to our aid in controlling the
RFF
kernel derivatives,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lvert\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})}{\partial s_{i}\partial t_{j}}-\frac{\partial^{2}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})}{\partial s_{i}\partial t_{j}}\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X},i,j}\epsilon^{-\frac{d}{d+1}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\epsilon^{2}}{4(d+1)(2S^{2}+R\epsilon)}\right),
