(5.166)

where we defined
C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X},i,j}=\left(\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert(D_{i,j}+E_{i,j})\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}
.
Hence, noting that the max satisfies the relation
\{\max_{i}\xi_{i}\geq\epsilon\}=\bigcup_{i}\{\xi_{i}\geq\epsilon\}

and union bounding (
5.164
) in probability, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[B_{k}^{k-1}\geq\epsilon\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lvert\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})}{\partial s_{i}\partial t_{j}}-\frac{\partial^{2}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})}{\partial s_{i}\partial t_{j}}\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]

(5.167)

\displaystyle\leq 16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\epsilon^{-\frac{d}{d+1}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\epsilon^{2}}{4(d+1)(2S^{2}+R\epsilon)}\right),

(5.168)

where we denote
C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X},i,j}=\left\lvert\mathcal{X}\right\rvert^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\left(D_{i,j}+E_{i,j}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}
.

Putting it together.
Now that we have our bounds for
A_{k}
and
B_{k}
, we put everything together, that is, plug the bounds (
5.162
) and (
5.168
) into (
5.156
), so that we get

\displaystyle P_{k}(\epsilon)\leq

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau>0}\left\{d\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}(k-1)\left(\frac{\tau}{2d}\right)^{2}}{2\left(S^{2}+R\frac{\tau}{2d}\right)}\right)+16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\tau}{t}\right)^{\frac{d(k-1)}{d+1}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\left(\frac{t}{\tau}\right)^{2(k-1)}}{4(d+1)\left(2S^{2}+R\left(\frac{t}{\tau}\right)^{k-1}\right)}\right)\right\}

(5.169)

\displaystyle+16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{2\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}}{t}\right)^{\frac{d(k-1)}{d+1}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\left(\frac{t}{2\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}}\right)^{2(k-1)}}{4(d+1)\left(2S^{2}+R\left(\frac{t}{2\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}}\right)^{k-1}\right)}\right)

(5.170)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(e)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle d\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}(k-1)\left(\frac{t^{\frac{k-1}{k}}}{2d}\right)^{2}}{2\left(S^{2}+R\frac{t^{\frac{k-1}{k}}}{2d}\right)}\right)+16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)^{\frac{d(k-1)}{(d+1)k}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}t^{\frac{2(k-1)}{k}}}{4(d+1)\left(2S^{2}+Rt^{\frac{k-1}{k}}\right)}\right)

(5.171)

\displaystyle+16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{2\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}}{t}\right)^{\frac{d(k-1)}{d+1}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\left(\frac{t}{2\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}}\right)^{2(k-1)}}{4(d+1)\left(2S^{2}+R\left(\frac{t}{2\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}}\right)^{k-1}\right)}\right)

(5.172)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(f)}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle d\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}(k-1)\left(\frac{\left(\epsilon/\alpha_{m,k}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}}{2d}\right)^{2}}{2\left(S^{2}+R\frac{\left(\epsilon/\alpha_{m,k}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}}{2d}\right)}\right)+16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{m,k}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{(d+1)k}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}}}{4(d+1)\left(2S^{2}+R\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\right)}\right)

(5.173)

\displaystyle+16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{m,k}\left(2\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\right)^{k-1}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha_{m,k}\left(2\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\right)^{k-1}}\right)^{2}}{4(d+1)\left(2S^{2}+R\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha_{m,k}\left(2\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\right)^{k-1}}\right)}\right),

(5.174)

where (a) follows from substituting (
5.168
) and (
5.162
) into (
5.156
) with the choice of
\gamma=\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}
, (b) from choosing
\tau=t^{\frac{k-1}{k}}
, and (c) from putting back
t=(\epsilon/\alpha_{m,k})^{\frac{1}{k-1}}
.

Note that the previous applies for all
k\geq 2
. For
k=1
, we have by (
5.168
) that

\displaystyle P_{1}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{1}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})-\partial_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\right\rVert_{2}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha_{m,1}}\right]

(5.175)

\displaystyle\leq 16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{m,1}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\exp\left(\frac{-\tilde{d}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha_{m,1}}\right)^{2}}{4(d+1)\left(2S^{2}+R\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha_{m,1}}\right)}\right).

(5.176)

Combining and simplifying.
We can now combine the bounds for
P_{1},\dots,P_{m}
into (
5.151
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}P_{k}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{m}\right)

(5.177)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(g)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle 2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}8C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{2^{k}{\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}}^{k-1}m\alpha_{m,k}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\cdot\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2(k-1)}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{2}}{S^{2}+R\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2(k-1)}m\alpha_{m,k}}}\right)

(5.178)

\displaystyle+2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}8C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\sum_{k=2}^{m}\left(\frac{2^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{(d+1)k}}\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\cdot\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}}}{S^{2}+R\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}}\right)

(5.179)

\displaystyle+d\sum_{k=2}^{M}\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}(k-1)}{2}\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}d^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}}}{S^{2}+R\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}d^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}}\right)

(5.180)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(h)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle 2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}8C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{2^{k}\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\cdot\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{2}}{S^{2}+R\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}}\right)

(5.181)

\displaystyle+2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}8C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{2^{k}\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{(d+1)k}}\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\cdot\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}}}{S^{2}+R\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}}\right)

(5.182)

\displaystyle+d\sum_{k=1}^{m}\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}}}{S^{2}+R\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2^{k}\left(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d\right)^{k}m\alpha_{m,k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}}\right)

(5.183)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(f)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle 2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}8C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\beta_{d,m,V}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{2}}{S^{2}+R\frac{\epsilon}{\beta{d,m,V}}}\right)

(5.184)

\displaystyle+2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}8C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\beta_{d,m,V}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{(d+1)k}}\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}}}{S^{2}+R\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}}\right)

(5.185)

\displaystyle+d\sum_{k=1}^{m}\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}}}{S^{2}+R\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}}\right),

(5.186)

where (g) follows from rearranging the expressions from (f), while (h) from unifying the coefficients and that
1\leq d\leq\max(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2},d)
and
f(x)=x^{2}/(a+bx)
is monotonically increasing in
x
on the positive half-line for
a,b>0
, while (f) from
\alpha_{m,k}=V^{2m}L^{2(m-k)}/((k-1)!)^{2}\leq(VL)^{2m}
, using that
f(x)
is increasing, and defining
\beta_{d,m,V}=m\left(2V^{2}(L^{2}\vee 1)(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d)\right)^{m}
.

Conclusion.

Finally, we split the bound into two cases: the first case is if the error is big, i.e.
\epsilon\geq\beta_{d,m,V}=m\left(2V^{2}(L^{2}\vee 1)(\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\vee d)\right)^{m}
, when we decrease all the exponents to
\frac{1}{m}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]

(5.187)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle m\cdot\left(2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\beta_{d,m,V}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{(d+1)m}}+d\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{\frac{2}{m}}}{S^{2}+R\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m}}}\right)

(5.188)

The other when the error is small, i.e.
\epsilon<\beta_{d,m,V}
, when we increase all the exponents to
1

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}},\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\leq V\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]

(5.189)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle m\cdot\left(2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}\left(\frac{\beta_{d,m,V}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}+d\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}}{2(d+1)}\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)^{2}}{S^{2}+R\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta_{d,m,V}}\right)}\right).

(5.190)

The claimed estimate follows by denoting
C_{d,\mathcal{X}}=2^{\frac{1}{d+1}}16C^{\prime}_{d,\mathcal{X}}
and simplifying.
∎

Next, we prove Theorem
5.6
to show an approximation bound for the
RFSF-DP
kernel.

Theorem 5.37

(Concentration inequality for
RFSF-DP
kernel)
.

Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant kernel with spectral measure
\Lambda
, which satisfies for some
S,R>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}\right\rvert^{2k}\right]\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0pti\in[d]\hskip 3.0pt\text{and}\hskip 3.0ptk\geq 2.

(5.191)

Then, for signature level
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, it holds for
\epsilon>0
that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\min\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}\epsilon}{2C_{d,m}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{m}}\right)^{2},\\
\left(\frac{\tilde{d}\epsilon}{\sqrt{8}C_{d,m}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{m}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m}}\end{array}\right\}\right),

where the absolute constant
C_{d,m}>0
satisfies that

\displaystyle C_{d,m}\leq\sqrt{8}e^{4}(2\pi)^{1/4}e^{1/24}(4e^{3}/m)^{m}\left(\left(2d\max(S,R)\right)^{m}+\left(L^{2}/\ln 2\right)^{m}\right).

(5.194)

Proof.

Let
\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{(1)},\dots,\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{(\tilde{d})}
be independent copies of the
RFSF
kernel, each with internal
RFF
sample size
\hat{d}=1
, such that
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}}\sum_{k=1}^{\tilde{d}}\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{(k)}
.
Our goal is to call Theorem
5.23
with
\alpha=\frac{1}{m}
, and therefore, we compute an upper bound on the
1/m
-norm of
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for all
k\in[\tilde{d}]
; for a definition of the
\alpha
-exponential norm, see Definition
5.17
.

By Lemma
5.32
, it holds for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
and
k\in[m]
that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left(\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\right)^{m}}{(m!)^{2}}\left\lVert\mathbf{w}_{k}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdots\left\lVert\mathbf{w}_{k}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},

(5.195)

where
\mathbf{w}_{k}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{k}^{(m)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda
are the random weights that parametrize
\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{(k)}
for all
k\in[\tilde{d}]
.
Now, calling Lemma
5.21
with
\alpha=1
yields that, due to (
5.191
), the following holds:

\displaystyle\left\lVert{w^{(p)}_{k,i}}^{2}\right\rVert_{{}_{1}}\leq 2\max(S,R)\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0pti\in[d],k\in[\tilde{d}],p\in[m].

(5.196)

Note that for
\alpha=1
,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}
satisfies the triangle inequality (see Lemma
5.19
), and hence

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}_{k}^{(p)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\rVert_{{}_{1}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\lVert{w_{k,i}^{(p)}}^{2}\right\rVert_{{}_{1}}\leq 2d\max(S,R)\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0ptk\in[\tilde{d}],p\in[m].

(5.197)

As
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}
is positive homogenous and satisfies a Hölder-type inequality (see Lemma
5.20
):

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rVert_{{}_{1/m}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\left(\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\right)^{m}}{(m!)^{2}}\left\lVert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}_{k}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\rVert_{{}_{1}}\cdots\left\lVert\left\lVert\mathbf{w}_{k}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\rVert_{{}_{1}}

(5.198)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\left(2d\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\max(S,R)\right)^{m}}{(m!)^{2}}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0ptk\in[\tilde{d}],

(5.199)

where in (a) we used Corollary
5.33
, in (b) we used (
5.199
).
We are almost ready to use Theorem
5.23
, but it requires the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{{}_{1/m}}
bound in terms of centered random variables.
Although
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}
does not satisfy the triangle inequality for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, it obeys that (see
[
100
, Lemma A.3.]
)
\left\lVert X+Y\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}\leq 2^{1/\alpha}\left(\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}+\left\lVert Y\right\rVert_{{}_{\alpha}}\right)
for any random variables
X
and
Y
. For a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\left\lVert c\right\rVert_{{}_{1/m}}=\frac{\left\lvert c\right\rvert}{\ln^{m}2}
, and hence by Lemma
5.31
we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rVert_{{}_{1/m}}\leq\frac{\left(L^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}/\ln 2\right)^{m}}{(m!)^{2}},

(5.200)

where
L=\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\top}\right]\right\rVert_{2}
is the Lipschitz constant of the kernel
\mathsf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. This gives

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rVert_{{}_{1/m}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m}\left(\left\lVert\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rVert_{{}_{1/m}}+\left\lVert{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rVert_{{}_{1/m}}\right)

(5.201)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left(2\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\right)^{m}}{(m!)^{2}}\left(\left(2d\max(S,R)\right)^{m}+\left(L^{2}/\ln 2\right)^{m}\right).

(5.202)

Finally, we have the required Orlicz norm bound for invoking Theorem
5.23
, so that we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{DP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]

(5.203)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\min\left\{\left(\frac{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}\epsilon}{2C_{d,m}}\right)^{2},\left(\frac{\tilde{d}\epsilon}{\sqrt{8}C_{d,m}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m}}\right\}\right),

(5.204)

where the constant
C_{d,m}>0
is defined as

\displaystyle C_{d,m}=\sqrt{8}e^{4}(2\pi)^{1/4}e^{1/24}\frac{(4em\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}})^{m}}{(m!)^{2}}\left(\left(2d\max(S,R)\right)^{m}+\left(L^{2}/\ln 2\right)^{m}\right),

(5.205)

and invoking Stirling’s approximation
\frac{1}{m!}\leq\left(\frac{e}{m}\right)^{m}
gives the stated result.
∎

Now, we prove the analogous result for
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
.

Theorem 5.38

(Concentration inequality for
RFSF-TRP
kernel)
.

Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded, translation-invariant kernel with spectral measure
\Lambda
, which satisfies for some
S,R>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\Lambda}\left[\left\lvert w_{i}\right\rvert^{2k}\right]\leq\frac{k!S^{2}R^{k-2}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 3.0pti\in[d]\hskip 3.0pt\text{and}\hskip 3.0ptk\geq 2.

(5.206)

Then, for the level-
m

RFSF-TRP
kernel as defined in (
5.30
), we have for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
and
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq C_{d,\Lambda}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{m^{2}\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}}{2\sqrt{2}e^{3}R\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right),

(5.207)

where
C_{d,\Lambda}=2\left(1+\frac{S}{2R}+\frac{S^{2}}{4R^{2}}\right)^{d}
.

Proof.

First, we consider the conditional probability
\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\middle|\mathbf{W}\right]
by conditioning on the
RFSF
weights
\mathbf{W}=(\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(m)})
, so that the only source of randomness comes from the
TRP
weights
\mathbf{P}=(\mathbf{P}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{P}^{(m)})
. The idea is to call Theorem
5.25
to estimate the conditional probability, and then take expectation over
\mathbf{W}
. Since Theorem
5.25
quantifies the concentration of a Gaussian polynomial around its mean in terms of its variance, we first compute the conditional statistics of
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

Conditional expectation.

Recall the definition of
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{TRP}}(\mathbf{x})
(
5.28
), where
\mathbf{p}^{(1)}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{(m)}_{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{2\tilde{d}})
, and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, so that

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}^{\texttt{TRP}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}^{(p)},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rangle\right)_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}}.

(5.208)

Hence,
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is written for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}}\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}^{(p)},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}^{(p)},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{i_{p}})\right\rangle}_{A_{i}},

(5.209)

which is a sample average of
\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}

i.i.d.
terms, i.e.
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}}A_{i}
. We only have to verify that
A_{i}
is conditionally an unbiased approximator of
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}
given
\mathbf{W}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]

(5.210)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i}\right],\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\otimes\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle

(5.211)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(c)}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\langle I_{\tilde{d}},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\otimes\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle

(5.212)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(d)}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\langle\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}}),\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle,

(5.213)

where (a) follows from linearity of expectation and independence of the
\mathbf{p}_{i}^{(p)}
’s for
p\in[m]
, (b) from bilinearity of inner product, and independence of
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{W}
, (c) from substituting the covariance, (d) is since the outer product is projected onto the diagonal.

Conditional variance.
We compute the conditional variance of
A_{i}
given
\mathbf{W}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[A_{m,i}^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]

(5.214)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(e)}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i},\mathbf{k}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})\\
\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{k_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{l_{p}})\right\rangle\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]

(5.215)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(f)}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i},\mathbf{k}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})\\
\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{k_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{l_{p}})\right\rangle\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]

(5.216)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{k_{p}})\right\rangle\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{l_{p}})\right\rangle\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]

(5.217)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{l_{p}})\right\rangle\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{k_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{p}^{(p)}_{i},\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]\bigg)

(5.218)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(g)}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i},\mathbf{k}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})\\
\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}

\displaystyle\bigg(\left\langle\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}}),\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{k_{p}}),\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{l_{p}})\right\rangle

(5.219)

\displaystyle+\left\langle\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}}),\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{k_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}}),\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{l_{p}})\right\rangle

(5.220)

\displaystyle+\left\langle\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}}),\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{l_{p}})\right\rangle\left\langle\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{k_{p}}),\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rangle\bigg)

(5.221)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(h)}}}{{\leq}}\mathrlap{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i},\mathbf{k}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})\\
\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}})\end{subarray}}3^{m}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rVert\left\lVert\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rVert\left\lVert\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{k_{p}})\right\rVert\left\lVert\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{l_{p}})\right\rVert}

(5.222)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\mathrlap{3^{m}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}})}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}})\right\rVert\right)^{2}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\Delta_{m}({{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}})}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{p}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{p}})\right\rVert\right)^{2}}

(5.223)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(j)}}}{{\leq}}\mathrlap{\frac{1}{(m!)^{4}}{\left(\frac{3\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{2}}{\tilde{d}^{2}}\right)}^{m}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert^{4}_{2},}

(5.224)

where (e) follows from linearity of expectation and independence of the
\mathbf{p}_{i}^{(p)}
’s for
p\in[m]
, (f) from Isserlis’ theorem
[
131
]
, (g) is the same as (a)-(d), (h) is the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, (i) from factorizing the summation, (j) is the same as Lemma
5.32
.

Therefore, we have due to Lemma
5.33
for the variance that

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[A_{m,i}\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[A_{m,i}^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]-\mathbb{E}^{2}\left[A_{m,i}\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]\leq\frac{3^{m}+1}{(m!)^{4}}{\left(\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{2}}{\tilde{d}^{2}}\right)}^{m}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert^{4}_{2}.

(5.225)

Let
\beta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{3^{m}+1}{(m!)^{4}}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{2m}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{2m}
. Then, as
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\,|\,\mathbf{W}
is a sample average,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]\leq\frac{\beta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}\tilde{d}^{2m}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert^{4}_{2}.

(5.226)

Conditional bound.
Since
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\,|\,\mathbf{W}
is a Gaussian polynomial of degree-
2m
, with expectation
\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, and variance (
5.226
), we have by Theorem
5.25
for
\epsilon>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}}{2\sqrt{2}e\mathbb{V}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\left[\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]}\right)

(5.227)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\tilde{d}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}}{2\sqrt{2}e\beta_{m}^{\frac{1}{2m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert^{\frac{2}{m}}_{2}}\right).

(5.228)

Undoing the conditioning.
We take the expectation in (
5.228
) so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\,|\,\mathbf{W}\right]\right]

(5.229)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\tilde{d}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}}{2\sqrt{2}e\beta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})^{\frac{1}{2m}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert^{\frac{2}{m}}_{2}}\right)\right]

(5.230)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(k)}}}{{\leq}}2\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}}{2\sqrt{2}e\beta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})^{\frac{1}{2m}}}\frac{\tilde{d}}{\lambda\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert^{\frac{2}{m}}_{2}}\right)\right]

(5.231)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(l)}}}{{\leq}}2\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-2\left(\frac{\lambda\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}}{2\sqrt{2}e\beta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})^{\frac{1}{2m}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{\lambda\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert^{\frac{2}{m}}_{2}}{\tilde{d}}\right)\right]

(5.232)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(m)}}}{{\leq}}2\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\lambda\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}}{e\beta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})^{\frac{1}{2m}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{\lambda\sum_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}{m\tilde{d}}\right)\right],

(5.233)

where (k) follows form multiplying and dividing with a
\lambda>0
, (l) from applying Lemma
5.14
with
p=\frac{1}{2}
and
q=1
, and (m) from the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality.

Bounding the MGF of
{w_{i,j}^{(p)}}^{2}
for
p\in[m],i\in[d],j\in[\tilde{d}]
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda{w^{(p)}_{i,j}}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(n)}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\left[{w_{i,j}^{(p)}}^{k}\right]\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(o)}}}{{\leq}}1+\lambda S+\frac{\lambda^{2}S^{2}}{2}\sum_{k\geq 0}(\lambda R)^{k}

(5.234)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(p)}}}{{=}}1+\lambda S+\frac{\lambda^{2}S^{2}}{2}\frac{1}{1-\lambda R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(q)}}}{{=}}1+\frac{S}{2R}+\frac{S^{2}}{4R^{2}},

(5.235)

where (n) is the Taylor expansion, (o) is the condition (
5.206
) and applying Jensen inequality to the degree-
1
term, (p) is the geometric series for
\lambda<\frac{1}{R}
, and (q) is choosing
\lambda=\frac{1}{2R}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda\sum_{p=1}^{m}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{(p)}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}{m\tilde{d}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda\sum_{p=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}{w_{i,j}^{(p)}}^{2}}{m\tilde{d}}\right)\right]

(5.236)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(r)}}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}^{1/(m\tilde{d})}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\sum_{p=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}}{w_{i,j}^{(p)}}^{2}\right)\right]

(5.237)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(s)}}}{{\leq}}\left(1+\frac{S}{2R}+\frac{S^{2}}{4R^{2}}\right)^{d},

(5.238)

where (r) is due to the Jensen inequality (Lemma
5.11
), and (s) follows from the independence of the
w_{i,j}^{(p)}
’s for
p\in[m],i\in[d],j\in[\tilde{d}]
.
Finally, plugging this into (
5.233
), we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\tilde{\mathsf{k}}^{\texttt{TRP}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2\left(1+\frac{S}{2R}+\frac{S^{2}}{4R^{2}}\right)^{d}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{\tilde{d}_{\texttt{TRP}}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{m}}}{\sqrt{2}eR\beta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})^{\frac{1}{2m}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

(5.239)

Finally, note that
\beta_{m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})^{\frac{1}{2m}}=\left(\frac{3^{m}+1}{(m!)^{4}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2m}}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{{1\texttt{-var}}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{{1\texttt{-var}}}\leq\frac{2e^{2}}{m^{2}}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{{1\texttt{-var}}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{{1\texttt{-var}}}
, since
3^{m}+1\leq 4^{m}
for
m\geq 1
, and
\frac{1}{m!}\leq\left(\frac{e}{m}\right)^{m}
due to Stirling’s approximation.
∎

Appendix 5.D
Algorithms

Algorithm 7

Computing the
RFSF
map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}
.

1:

Input:
Sequences
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, measure
\Lambda
, truncation
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
RFF
sample size
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

2:

Optional: Add time-parameterization
\mathbf{x}_{i}\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{i,t},t/\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}})_{t=1}^{\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}}
for all
i\in[N]

3:

Tabulate to uniform length
\ell=\max_{j\in[N]}\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}
by
\mathbf{x}_{i}\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}})
for all
i\in[N]

4:

Sample independent
RFF
weights
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}

5:

Initialize an array
U
with shape
[M,N,\ell-1,2\tilde{d}]

6:

Compute increments
U[m,i,t,:]\leftarrow\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i,t+1})-\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i,t})
for
m\in[M]
,
i\in[N]
,
t\in[\ell-1]

7:

Initialize array
V\leftarrow U[1,:,:,:]

8:

Collapse into level-
1
features
P_{1}\leftarrow V[:,\Sigma,:]

9:

for

m=2

to

M

do

10:

Update with next increment
V\leftarrow V[:,\boxplus+1,:]\boxtimes_{3}U[m,:,:,:]

11:

Collapse into level-
m
features
P_{m}\leftarrow V[:,\Sigma,:]

12:

end

for

13:

Output:
Arrays of
RFSF
features per signature level
P_{1},\dots,P_{M}
.

for all

Tabulate to uniform length
\ell=\max_{j\in[N]}\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}
by
\mathbf{x}_{i}\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}})
for all
i\in[N]

Sample independent

,

Algorithm 9

Computing the
RFSF-TRP
map
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{S}_{\leq M}}^{\texttt{TRP}}
.

1:

Input:
Sequences
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X})
, measure
\Lambda
, truncation
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
RFSF
and
TRP
sample size
\tilde{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

2:

Optional: Add time-parameterization
\mathbf{x}_{i}\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{i,t},t/\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}})_{t=1}^{\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}}
for all
i\in[N]

3:

Tabulate to uniform length
\ell=\max_{j\in[N]}\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}
by
\mathbf{x}_{i}\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i,\ell_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}})
for all
i\in[N]

4:

Sample independent
RFF
weights
\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{W}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda^{\tilde{d}}

5:

Sample standard normal matrices
\mathbf{P}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{P}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}^{\tilde{d}}(0,\mathbf{I}_{2\tilde{d}})

6:

Initialize an array
U
with shape
[M,N,\ell-1,\tilde{d}]

7:

Compute projected increments
U[m,i,t,:]\leftarrow{\mathbf{P}^{(m)}}^{\top}\left(\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i,t+1})-\tilde{\varphi}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{i,t})\right)
for
m\in[M]
,
i\in[N]
,
t\in[\ell-1]

Initialize array
V\leftarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}}}U[1,:,:,:]

9:

Collapse into level-
1
features
P_{1}\leftarrow V[:,\Sigma,:]

10:

for

m=2

to

M

do

11:

Update with next increment
V\leftarrow V[:,\boxplus+1,:]\odot U[m,:,:,:]

12:

Collapse into level-
m
features
P_{m}\leftarrow V[:,\Sigma,:]

13:

end

for

14:

Output:
Arrays of
RFSF-TRP
features per signature level
P_{1},\dots,P_{M}
.

Chapter 6
Recurrent Sparse Spectrum Signature Gaussian processes

6.1
Introduction

Time series forecasting plays a central role in various domains, including finance
[
238
]
, renewable energy
[
283
,
2
]
, and healthcare
[
28
]
. Ongoing research faces several challenges, such as non-linear data domains, ordered structure, time-warping invariance, discrete and irregular sampling, and scalability.
Among the numerous existing approaches, we focus on signature approach, which address the first four challenges and have demonstrated state-of-the-art performance as a kernel for sequential data
[
267
]
. However, the trade-off between accuracy and scalability remains unresolved. While the exact computation of the signature kernel requires quadratic complexity for sequence length
L

[
146
]
, existing approximate methods reduce complexity to linear, at the cost of degraded performance on large-scale datasets. Moreover, these methods treat the signature as a global feature, which limits their ability to capture recent local information effectively. This is particularly crucial for time series forecasting, where capturing near-term correlations are often as important as identifying long-term trends.

Contributions

We propose Random Fourier Decayed Signature Features with Gaussian Processes, which dynamically adjust its context length based on the data, prioritizing more recent information, and call the resulting model Recurrent Sparse Spectrum Signature Gaussian Process (RS
3
GP). By incorporating variational inference, our model learns the decay parameters in a data-driven manner, allowing it to transform time series data into a joint predictive distribution at scale while maintaining accuracy. Our approach outperforms other
GP
baselines for time series on both small and large datasets and demonstrates comparable performance to state-of-the-art deep learning diffusion models while offering significantly faster training times.

6.1.1
Related work

Gaussian process approach

There are two prominent approaches: (1) specialized kernels for sequences
[
176
,
58
,
60
,
5
]
, and (2) state-space models
[
86
,
84
,
187
,
78
,
71
,
127
]
. These approaches can complement each other; modeling the latent system as a higher-order Markov process allows sequence kernels to capture the influence of past states. The random projection approach, also known as sparse spectrum approximation in the Gaussian process community
[
158
,
288
,
89
,
62
,
167
]
.

Deep learning approach

From classic LSTMs
[
121
]
to transformers
[
275
]
, deep learning methods have been widely applied to sequential data. In the context of probabilistic forecasting, diffusion models
[
242
,
119
,
149
]
are considered among the state-of-the-art techniques. Although deep learning models can approximate any continuous function, they often require a large number of parameters, suffer from high variance, and offer limited interpretability. This creates opportunities for alternative approaches, which can serve not only as competitors but also as complementary components within larger models.

6.2
Background

6.2.1
Signature Features and Kernels

The path signature
\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})
provides a graded description of a path
\mathbf{x}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by mapping it to a hierarchy of tensors
\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})=\left(1,\mathsf{S}_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\mathsf{S}_{2}(\mathbf{x}),\ldots\right)\in\prod_{m\geq 0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes m}
of increasing degrees.
Among its attractive properties are that: it maps from a nonlinear domain (there is not natural addition of paths of different length) to a linear space
\prod(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes m}
;
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})
is injective up to time-parameterization (thus naturally factoring out time-warping) and it can be made injective by adding time as a path coordinate (i.e. setting
x^{0}_{t}=t)
; it linearizes path functionals, that is we can approximate non-linear path functionals
f(\mathbf{x})
as linear functionals of signatures
f(\mathbf{x})\approx\langle\ell,\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})\rangle
, see Section
1.2.2
for details. Additionally, if the path is random, the expected path signature characterizes the distribution,
\mu\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mu}[\mathsf{S}(\mathbf{x})]
is injective, see
[
44
]
.
All this makes it a viable transformation for extracting information from time series and paths in machine learning.

Discrete-time signature features

In practice, we do not have access to continuous paths but are only given a sequence
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Here, we focus on the higher-order discretized signature variation from Section
1.2.6
, where the order parameter is set to the truncation level
p=M
. Hence, in this case we can simply identify
\mathbf{x}
with a continuous time path by its linear interpolation for any choice of time parameterization.
To compute signatures, denote with
\bar{\Delta}_{m}(n)
the collection of all ordered
m
-tuples such that

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}_{m}(n)=\left\{1\leq i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{m}\leq n\,:\,i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in[n]\right\}.

(6.1)

Then, it can be shown using Chen’s relation (Proposition
1.14
) that its signature can be computed by the recursion for
1\leq m
, and
1\leq l\leq L
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l})=\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l-1})+\sum_{p=1}^{m}\mathsf{S}_{m-p}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l-1})\otimes\frac{(\delta\mathbf{x}_{l})^{\otimes p}}{p!}

(6.2)

with the identification
S_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:0})\equiv 0
, and the first-order difference operator is defined as
\delta\mathbf{x}_{k}=\mathbf{x}_{k}-\mathbf{x}_{k-1}
. Unrolling this along the length of the time series with respect to the first term, we get

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l})=\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sum_{p=1}^{m}\mathsf{S}_{m-p}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k-1})\otimes\frac{(\delta\mathbf{x}_{k})^{\otimes p}}{p!}.

(6.3)

Recall from Section
1.2.6
that this expression can be evaluated in closed form:

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:k})=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!}\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},

(6.4)

where
\mathbf{i}!=k_{1}!\cdots k_{q}!
such that there are
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
unique indices in the multi-index
\mathbf{i}
and
k_{1},\cdots,k_{q}
are the number of times they are repeated.
It will be important later on that (
6.2
) allows to compute the signature up to all time steps using a paralellizable scan operation.

Discrete-time signature kernels

As
\mathsf{S}_{m}(\mathbf{x})
is tensor-valued, it has
d^{m}
coordinates, which makes its computation infeasible for high-dimensional state-spaces or high
m
.
Kernelization allows to alleviate this by first lifting sequences and paths from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into a RKHS and subsequently computing inner products of signature features in this RKHS. This alleviates the computational bottleneck and further adds more expressivity by first lifting the paths into the RKHS; see Section
1.2.4
.
Concretely, the signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\mathsf{k}_{S_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),

(6.5)

where
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
is the truncation level. Although truncating the series (
6.5
) may seem like a limitation, it often happens that a careful selection of finite truncation level outperforms the untruncated case, since it can mitigate overfitting.

If we identify two sequences
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with paths given by their linear interpolations for some choice of time parameterization, then we get the analogous expression to (
6.4
):

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\bar{\Delta}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!\mathbf{j}!}\delta_{i_{1},j_{1}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}})\cdots\delta_{i_{m},j_{m}}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{m}},\mathbf{y}_{j_{m}}),

(6.6)

where
\mathbf{i}!
is as previously, and
\delta_{i,j}
is a second-order differencing operator, such that
\delta_{i,j}\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})=\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\mathbf{y}_{j+1})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\mathbf{y}_{j})-\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j+1})+\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j}).
A similar recursion to (
6.2
) exists to compute (
6.6
), see Algorithm
2
in Section
1.3
. For further details, see Sections
1.2.4
and
1.2.6
.

6.2.2
Random Fourier Features

Kernelization circumvents the computational burden of a high- or infinite-dimensional feature space, but the cost is the associated quadratic complexity in the number of samples which is due to the evaluation of the Gram matrix.
Random Fourier Features
[
209
]
address this by constructing for a given kernel
\mathsf{k}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
a random feature map for which the inner product is a good random approximation to the original kernel.
Many variations proposed, see
[
173
,
35
]
.

Existing variations are based on Bochner’s theorem, which allows for a spectral representation of stationary kernels. Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded, stationary kernel. Then, Bochner’s theorem states that there exists a non-negative finite measure
\Lambda
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that
\mathsf{k}
is its Fourier transform. In other words, for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathsf{k}
can be be represented as

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp(i\bm{\omega}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}))\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda(\bm{\omega})

(6.7)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\cos(\bm{\omega}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda(\bm{\omega}),

(6.8)

since the kernel is real-valued. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\Lambda(\mathbb{R}^{d})=1
so
\Lambda
is a probability measure, since it amounts to rescaling the kernel.

Now, we may draw Monte Carlo samples to approximate (
6.8
),
\bm{\omega}_{1},\dots,\bm{\omega}_{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda
for
D\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\approx\frac{1}{D}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\cos(\bm{\omega}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})).

(6.9)

There are two ways to proceed from here: one is to use the cosine identity
\cos(x-y)=\cos(x)\cos(y)+\sin(x)\sin(y)
, or to further approximate the sum in (
6.9
) using the identity

\displaystyle\cos(x-y)=\mathbb{E}_{b\sim\mathcal{U}(0,2\pi)}\left[\sqrt{2}\cos(x+b)\sqrt{2}\cos(y+b)\right]

(6.10)

see
[
89
, App. A]
. We approximate the expectation (
6.9
) by drawing
1
sample for each term:

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\approx\frac{2}{D}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\cos(\bm{\omega}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+b_{i})\cos(\bm{\omega}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{y}+b_{i}),

(6.11)

where
b_{1},\dots,b_{D}\sim\mathcal{U}(0,2\pi)
. This represents each sample using a
1
-dimensional feature, which will be important for us later on in Section
6.3.1
when constructing our variation of the Random Fourier Signature Feature map. Now, let
\Omega=(\bm{\omega}_{1},\dots,\bm{\omega}_{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times D}
and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
. Then, the
RFF
feature map can be written concisely as

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{x})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{p}}\cos\left({}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}\right).

(6.12)

This offers to approximate a stationary kernel using a random, finite-dimensional feature map, and consequently to reformulate downstream algorithms in weight-space, which avoids the usual cubic costs in the number of data points. Probabilistic convergence of the
RFF
is studied in the series of works:
[
209
]
, optimal rates were derived in
[
244
]
, extended to the kernel derivatives in
[
255
]
, conditions on the spectral measure relaxed in
[
35
]
.

6.3
Recurrent Sparse Spectrum Signature Gaussian processes

6.3.1
Revisiting Random Fourier Signature Features

Now, we revisit how
RFF
s can be combined with signatures to extract random features from a time series. To do so, we adapt the construction of Random Fourier Signature Features (
RFSF
) from Chapter
5
. We provide an adapted version of the diagonally projected
RFSF
variant (
RFSF-DP
), with a twist, which allows us to devise even more computationally efficient features.

We will focus on the discrete-time setting, but the construction applies to continuous-time in an analogous manner. Firstly, we will build a
1
-dimensional random estimator for the signature kernel. Let
\mathsf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, bounded, stationary kernel with spectral measure
\Lambda
,
\bm{\omega}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{\omega}^{(m)}\sim\Lambda
and
b^{(1)},\dots,b^{(m)}\sim\mathcal{U}(0,2\pi)
, and
\phi^{(p)}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\cos({\bm{\omega}^{(p)}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+b^{(p)})
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, for sequences
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds for the signature kernel
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}
as defined in (
6.6
) that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!\mathbf{j}!}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\sqrt{2}\delta\phi^{(p)}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}})\sqrt{2}\delta\phi^{(p)}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}})\right].

(6.13)

The unbiasedness of the expectation is straightforward to check, since due to linearity and independence of
\phi^{(p)}
’s, it reduces to verifying the relations (
6.10
) and (
6.8
), which in turn recovers the formulation of the signature kernel from (
6.6
). Next, we draw
D\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
Monte Carlo samples to approximate (
6.13
), hence, let
\bm{\omega}^{(1)}_{1},\dots,\bm{\omega}^{(m)}_{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda
and
b^{(1)}_{1},\dots,b^{(m)}_{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{U}(0,2\pi)
, and
\phi^{(p)}_{k}(\mathbf{x})=\cos({\bm{\omega}^{(p)}_{k}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+b^{(p)}_{k})
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{S}_{m}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\approx\frac{1}{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}})\\
\mathbf{j}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}({\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!\mathbf{j}!}\prod_{p=1}^{m}\sqrt{2}\delta\phi^{(p)}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}})\sqrt{2}\delta\phi^{(p)}_{k}(\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}}).

(6.14)

We call this approximation Random Fourier Signature Features (
RFSF
), and the features corresponding to the kernel on the
RHS
of (
6.14
) can be represented in a finite-dimensional feature space. Let us collect the frequencies
{}^{(p)}=(\bm{\omega}^{(p)}_{1},\dots,\bm{\omega}^{(p)}_{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times D}
and phases
\mathbf{b}^{(p)}=(b^{(p)}_{1},\dots,b^{(p)}_{D})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, and define the
RFF
maps as in (
6.12
) so we have
\varphi^{(p)}(\mathbf{x})=\cos({{}^{(p)}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}^{(p)})
. Then, the
RFSF
map
\Phi_{m}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{D}
is defined for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x})=\sqrt{\frac{2^{m}}{D}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}(K)}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!}\bigodot_{p=1}^{m}\delta\varphi^{(p)}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}}),

(6.15)

where
\odot
refers to the Hadamard product. Although (
6.15
) looks difficult to compute, we can establish a recursion across time and signature levels. The update rule analogous to (
6.2
) is

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l})=\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l-1})+\sum_{p=1}^{m}\frac{1}{p!}\Phi_{m-p}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l-1})\bigodot_{q=m-p+1}^{m}\delta\varphi^{(q)}(\mathbf{x}_{l}),

(6.16)

which can be computed efficiently using parallelizable scan operation. Additionally, we overload the definition of the differencing operator
\delta
to mean fractional differencing for some learnable differencing order
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Thus, in (
6.15
), we learn a separate differencing order parameter for each feature channel, see Appendix
6.A.1
.

In practive, we use the first
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
levels in conjunction, and normalize each
RFSF
level to unit norm, so that the full
RFSF
map
\Phi:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{MD+1}
is

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\left(1,\frac{\Phi_{1}(\mathbf{x})}{\left\lVert\Phi_{1}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{2}},\dots,\frac{\Phi_{M}(\mathbf{x})}{\left\lVert\Phi_{M}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{2}}\right).

(6.17)

We note that
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
can be computed with complexity
O((M+W)LD+MLDd)
, where
W\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is the window size for fractional differencing, see Appendix
6.A.2
. This is more efficient than the scalable variants of Chapter
5
, i.e. it avoids the
2^{M}
factor as in
RFSF-DP
, and it also avoids the
D^{2}
factor as in
RFSF-TRP
.

6.3.2
A forgetting mechanism for signatures

The recursive step in
RFSF
(
6.16
) allows to compute the feature map up to all time steps of a time series, similarly to a recurrent neural network (
RNN
), in one forward pass. This suggests to perform sequence-to-sequence regression (including time series forecasting, when the target sequence is future values of the time series) by considering the
RFSF
map over an expanding window. However, signatures have no built-in forgetting mechanism, and it is well-known that the level-
m
signature feature has magnitude
O\left(\nicefrac{{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1\texttt{-var}}^{m}}}{{m!}}\right)
, and the same is true for (
6.15
), which shows that
RFSF
only accumulates information without a way of forgetting. This is in contrast to modern
RNN
s, which have built-in gating mechanisms that allow or disallow the flow of historical information, allowing them to focus on more recent information.

In this section, we propose a novel forgetting mechanism tailored for
RFSF
, but which can be applied to signature features in general. We tackle this by introducing time step dependent decay factors, which multiply each increment in the formulation (
6.15
). We assume exponential decay in time, so let
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
be channel-wise decay factors. Then, we define Random Fourier Decayed Signature Features (
RFDSF
) as

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l})=\sqrt{\frac{2^{m}}{D}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\bar{\Delta}_{m}(K)}\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}!}\bigodot_{p=1}^{m}\bm{\lambda}^{\odot(l-i_{p})}\odot\delta\varphi^{(p)}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{p}}).

(6.18)

A recursion analogous to (
6.16
) allows to compute (
6.18
):

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l})=\bm{\lambda}^{\odot m}\odot\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l-1})+\sum_{p=1}^{m}\frac{1}{p!}\bm{\lambda}^{\odot(m-p)}\odot\Phi_{m-p}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l-1})\bigodot_{q=m-p+1}^{m}\delta\varphi^{(q)}(\mathbf{x}_{l}),

(6.19)

which can be unrolled over the length of a sequence so

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l})=\sum_{k=1}^{l}\bm{\lambda}^{\odot m(l-k)}\sum_{p=1}^{m}\frac{1}{p!}\bm{\lambda}^{\odot(m-p)}\odot\Phi_{m-p}(\mathbf{x}_{0:l-1})\bigodot_{q=m-p+1}^{m}\delta\varphi^{(q)}(\mathbf{x}_{k}),

(6.20)

which is an exponential decay over time steps of an appropriately chosen sequence depending on signature levels lower than
m
. Hence, (
6.20
) is still computable by a parallelizable scan operation, allowing for sublinear time computations. This makes the feature map well-suited for long time series, due to the adjustment of context length by the decay factors, and the possibility for parallelization across time. The theoretical complexity is the same as previously, i.e.
O((M+W)MLD+MLDd)
, see Appendix
6.A.2
, but we emphasize that a work-efficient scan algorithm allows to compute the recursion on a
GPU
log-linearly.

6.3.3
Recurrent Sparse Spectrum Signature Gaussian processes

Next, we construct our (Variational) Recurrent Sparse Spectrum Signature Gaussian Process (
(V)RS
3
GP
) model. We define a Gaussian process, which treats the hyperparameters of the random covariance function in a probabilistic fashion
[
89
]
by incorporating them into inference. We formulate our variational
GP
model in the feature space, as opposed to in the function space.

Bayesian formulation

We focus on supervised learning, and assume our dataset consists of multiple input time series
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
each with a corresponding univariate output time series
\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R})
, such that
{\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{x}}}={\mathsf{L}_{\mathbf{y}}}
. We note that the multivariate case can also be handled by stacking multiple
GP
priors, one for each output coordinate.

As the signature is a universal feature map, we expect that a linear layer on top of the computed
RFDSF
features will work well for approximating functions of time series. We assume a probabilistic model, which models the prediction process as a linear layer on top of
RFDSF
, corrupted by Gaussian noise, that is,

\displaystyle y_{l}=\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Phi(\mathbf{x}_{0:l})+\epsilon_{l}\quad\text{for }l=0,\dots,L,

(6.21)

where
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{MD+1}
, such that
M
is the signature truncation level and
D
is the
RFF
dimension. We place an
i.i.d.
standard Gaussian prior on
\mathbf{w}
,
p(\mathbf{w})=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{MD+1})
. The model noise is
i.i.d.
Gaussian with learnable variance
\sigma_{y}^{2}>0
, so that
p(y\,|\,\mathbf{w},\bm{\Omega},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Phi(\mathbf{x}),\sigma_{y}^{2})
, where we explicitly denoted the dependence on the hyperparameters of the
RFSF
map
\Phi
, frequencies
\bm{\Omega}=({}^{(1)},\dots,{}^{(M)})\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times d\times D}
, and phases
B=(\mathbf{b}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{b}^{(M)})\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times D}
. Next, we specialize to the case of the ARD Gaussian kernel, which has
\Lambda=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},D^{-1})
, where
D\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{D\times D}
is a diagonal matrix of lengthscales. We assign independent lengthscales to each random feature map in (
6.18
).
Then, following previous work
[
89
,
57
]
, we place the priors on the
RFF
parameters

\displaystyle p(\bm{\Omega})=\prod_{m=1}^{M}p({}^{(m)})=\prod_{m=1}^{m}\mathcal{N}^{D}(\mathbf{0},D_{m}^{-1}),

(6.22)

\displaystyle p(B)=\prod_{m=1}^{M}p(\mathbf{b}^{(m)})=\prod_{m=1}^{M}\mathcal{U}^{D}(0,2\pi),

(6.23)

where
D_{m}=\operatorname{diag}(\ell_{m,1},\dots,\ell_{m,d})
refers to the diagonal lengthscale matrix of the
m^{th}

RFF
map.

As noted in
[
57
]
, this model corresponds to a 2-layer Bayesian neural network, where the first layer is given by the
RFDSF
activations with priors on it as given by (
6.22
), while the second layer is a linear readout layer with a Gaussian prior on it. Inference in this case is analytically intractable. For a Gaussian likelihood, although
\mathbf{w}
can be marginalized out in the log-likelihood, it is not clear how to handle the integrals with respect to
\bm{\Omega}
and
B
. Further, disregarding the latter issue of additional hyperparameters, it only admits full-batch training, and this limits scalability to datasets with large numbers of input-output pairs. To this end, we introduce a variational approximation, which allows for both scalability and flexibility.

Variational treatment

We variationally approximate the posterior to define an evidence lower bound (
ELBO
).
We define factorized variational distributions over the parameters
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{MD+1}
, frequencies
\bm{\Omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times d\times D}
, and phases
B\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times D}
. The variational over
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{MD+1}
is as usual given by a Gaussian
q(\mathbf{w})=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{w}},\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}})
, where
\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{MD+1}
is the variational mean and
\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(MD+1)\times(MD+1)}
is the variational covariance matrix, which is symmetric and positive definite, represented in terms of its Cholesky factor,
L_{\mathbf{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(MD+1)\times(MD+1)}
, such that
\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}=L_{\mathbf{w}}L_{\mathbf{w}}^{\top}
. The other parameters get the factorized variationals

\displaystyle q(\bm{\Omega})=\prod_{m=1}^{M}q({}^{(m)})=\prod_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{D}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{mij},\sigma^{2}_{mij}),

(6.24)

\displaystyle q(B)=\prod_{m=1}^{M}q(\mathbf{b}^{(m)})=\prod_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{D}\mathcal{B}_{[0,2\pi]}(\alpha_{mi},\beta_{mi}),

(6.25)

where
\alpha_{mi},\beta_{mi}>0
, so that the posteriors over the component frequencies of
\omega
are independent Gaussians, while the posteriors over the phases
b
are independent beta distributions on
[0,2\pi]
.

The
KL
-divergence to the posterior is then minimized:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(\mathbf{w},\bm{\Omega},B)\,\|\,p(\mathbf{w},\bm{\Omega},B\,|\,\mathbf{y})\right]=\int q(\mathbf{w},\bm{\Omega},B)\log\frac{q(\mathbf{w},\bm{\Omega},B)}{p(\mathbf{w},\bm{\Omega},B\,|\,\mathbf{y})}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\Omega}\,\mathrm{d}B.

(6.26)

Then, by the usual calculations, we get the
ELBO
,
\mathcal{L}_{\texttt{ELBO}}\leq\log p(\mathbf{y})
, such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\texttt{ELBO}}=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{q}\left[\log p(y_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{w},\bm{\Omega},B)\right]}_{\text{data-fit term}}-\underbrace{\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(\mathbf{w})\,\|\,p(\mathbf{w})\right]-\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(\bm{\Omega})\,\|\,p(\bm{\Omega})\right]-\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(B)\,\|\,p(B)\right]}_{\text{{KL} regularizers}}

(6.27)

The predictive distribution for a set of (possibly overlapping) sequences
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{N})\subset\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is then, denoting
\Phi(\mathbf{X})=\left[\Phi(\mathbf{x}_{1}),\ldots,\Phi(\mathbf{x}_{N})\right]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times(MD+1)}
and
\mathbf{f}=\Phi(\mathbf{X})\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

\displaystyle q(\mathbf{f})=\mathcal{N}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{X})\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{w}}^{\top},\,\,\Phi(\mathbf{X})L_{\mathbf{w}}L_{\mathbf{w}}^{\top}\Phi(\mathbf{X})^{\top}\right).

(6.28)

In order to evaluate the data-fit term in (
6.27
), and make inference about unseen points in (
6.28
), we require a way to handle the randomness in
\bm{\Omega}
and
B
. Two solutions proposed in
[
57
]
are to perform Monte Carlo sampling or to sample them once at the start of training and keep them fixed while learning their distributional hyperparameters using the reparameterization trick
[
144
]
. Our observations align with theirs, and we found that resampling leads to high variance and non-convergence. The reason for this is likely that the factorized variational distributions do not model correlations between
\mathbf{w}
and
\bm{\Omega},B
. Hence, we use a fixed random outcome throughout with the reparameterization trick, see Appendix
6.A.3
for details.

Training objective

Although the
ELBO
(
6.27
) lower bounds the log-likelihood, it suffers from well-known pathologies. One of these is that it greatly underestimates the latent function uncertainty, and mainly relies on the observation noise for uncertainty calibration, which leads to overestimation. This is clearly unideal for time series forecasting, where heteroscedasticity is often present, and since useful information carried by the kernel about the geometry of the data space is lost. There are several solutions proposed by
[
133
]
, and we adopt the approach called Parametric Predictive
GP
Regression (
PPGPR
), which restores symmetry between the training objective and the test time predictive distribution by treating both latent function uncertainty and observation noise on equal footing. The key idea is to modify the data-fit term in (
6.27
) taking the
\log
outside the
q
-expectation so we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\texttt{PPGPR}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\mathbb{E}_{q}\left[p(y_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{w},\bm{\Omega},B\right]-\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(\mathbf{w})\,\|\,p(\mathbf{w})\right]-\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(\bm{\Omega})\,\|\,p(\bm{\Omega})\right]-\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(B)\,\|\,p(B)\right]

(6.29)

which although is not a lower-bound anymore to the log-likelihood, leads to better calibrated uncertainties. However, as noted by
[
133
]
, this objective can lead to underfitting in data regions, where a good fit is harder to achieve, and instead relying on uncertainty overestimation. We account for this by modifying the objective function by introducing a penalty term for the latent function variance. Let
q(f_{i})=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})
. Then, we modify the objective as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\mathbb{E}_{q}\left[p(y_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{w},\bm{\Omega},B\right]-\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(\mathbf{w})\,\|\,p(\mathbf{w})\right]-\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(\bm{\Omega})\,\|\,p(\bm{\Omega})\right]-\mathsf{D}_{\texttt{KL}}\left[q(B)\,\|\,p(B)\right]-\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{2},

(6.30)

where
\alpha>0
is a regularization hyperparameter. This promotes the optimizer to escape local optima, and rely on a better fit of the predictive mean, rather than on uncertainty overestimation to fit the data. We give further details in Appendix
6.A.4
.

6.4
Experiments

Implementation

We implemented our models and
GP
baselines using
PyTorch

[
203
]
and
GPyTorch

[
91
]
.
The computing cluster used has 4 NVIDIA RTX 3080 TI
GPU
s.

6.4.1
Synthetic Dataset

Dataset

We handcrafted a dataset to test the ability of our
(V)RS
3
GP
model of adapting its context length to the data on a task which requires reasoning on multiple time horizons. The dataset consists of a multi-sinusoidal wave with multiple frequencies, which includes both large and low frequencies, requiring reasoning over both short and long time periods.

Methods

We compare three models: Variational Recurrent Sparse Spectrum Gaussian Process (
VRS
3
GP
), our model constructed in the previous section;
RS
3
GP
, which ablates the previous model as it does not learn variationals over the covariance parameters and sets them equal to the prior; Sparse Variational Gaussian Process (
SVGP
) using the
RBF
kernel and a fixed hand-tuned context length.
(V)RS
3
GP
uses
D=200
and
M=5
, while
SVGP
uses
100
inducing points.

Qualitative analysis

Figure
6.1
qualitatively illustrates the predictive mean and uncertainty of the different approaches.
SVGP
achieves a perfect fit of the training data, but in the testing regime fails to properly capture the underlying dynamics.
RS
3
GP
properly captures both short and long horizon dynamics, but in certain data regions underfits the dataset.
VRS
3
GP
on the other hand fits the data perfectly, and also perfectly captures the temporal dynamics, being more flexible than
RS
3
GP
due to variational parameter learning.

Figure 6.1
:
Predictive mean and uncertainty on a toy dataset composed of multi-sinusoidal waves with four distinct components including low and high frequencies, comparing various
GP
approaches. The true function is depicted in blue, while the predictive mean
\pm 3
standard deviations are shown in red. The dataset consists of 700 training points, with a context window of 100 for
SVGP
, and a prediction horizon of 100 for all.

In this section, we present empirical results on several real-world datasets.

Datasets

We conducted experiments on eight univariate time series datasets from different domains, available in
GluonTS

[
6
]
—Solar
[
154
]
, Electricity
[
9
]
, Traffic
[
9
]
, Exchange
[
154
]
, M4
[
183
]
, UberTLC
[
92
]
, KDDCup
[
98
]
, and Wikipedia
[
92
]
. We use GluonTS
[
6
]
to load the datasets, which has pre-specified train-test splits for each. See
[
262
, App. B.1]
for details.

Metric

We employed the continuous ranked probability score (
CRPS
)
[
97
]
for evaluating probabilistic forecasts. We approximate the
CRPS

by the normalized mean quantile loss, and report means and standard deviations over three independent runs, see
[
262
, App. B.3]
.

Baselines

We extend the list of baselines from
[
149
]
. As such, we have included comprehensive baselines from three groups: classical statistics, deep learning, and other
GP
methods. For statistics, we included
SeasonalNaïve
,
ARIMA
,
ETS
, and a
RidgeRegression
model from the statistical literature
[
126
]
. Additionally, we compared against deep learning models that represent various architectural paradigms such as the
RNN
-based
DeepAR

[
227
]
, the
CNN
-based
MQ-CNN

[
286
]
, the state space model-based
DeepState

[
214
]
, the self-attention-based
Transformer

[
275
]
, and diffusion-model-based
TSDiff

[
149
]
. For
GP
models, we add
SVGP

[
118
]
, uses 500 inducing points with the
RBF
kernel; and
DKLGP

[
289
]
which augments
SVGP
with a deep kernel using a
2
-layer
NN
with
64
units. See
[
262
, App. B.4]
for further details.

Results

Table
6.1
shows the results of our models
VRS
3
GP
and
RS
3
GP
compared to baselines. We omit results for
SVGP
and
DKLGP
on Traffic and Wikipedia since inference time takes longer than 12 hours. Overall, our models achieve top scores on 2 datasets, outperforming the state-of-the-art diffusion baseline
TSDiff
, and provide comparable performance to deep learning baselines on the remaining 4/6 datasets. Importantly, they outperform linear baselines. They also outperform the
GP
baselines on 4/6 datasets. One of the datasets our model achieves the top score on, KDDCup, is the one with longest time series length,
L\sim 10000
, which suggests that this is due to its ability to adapt its context memory to long ranges. Moreover, Table
6.2
illustrates that our models
VRS
3
GP
and
RS
3
GP
are significantly quicker to train
TSDiff
, and both
SVGP
and
DKLGP
. This speed up is especially pronounced for datasets with long time series such as Solar, Electricity, Exchange, UberTLC and KDDCup, since our model is able to process a long time series in sublinear time due to parallelizability as detailed in Section
6.3
. We further investigate the scalability in Appendix
6.A.5
.

6.5
Conclusion and Limitations

In this work, we introduced the Random Fourier Decayed Signature Features for time series forecasting, incorporating a novel forgetting mechanism into signature features, which addresses the need to adaptively prioritize local, recent information in long time series. Our proposed model, the Recurrent Sparse Spectrum Signature Gaussian Process (
RS
3
GP
), leverages variational inference and recurrent structure to efficiently transform time series data into a joint predictive distribution. We demonstrated that our approach outperforms traditional
GP
models and achieves comparable performance to state-of-the-art deep learning methods, while offering significantly faster training times. Limitations include reliance on the Gaussian likelihood, which may be unsuitable for non-Gaussian or heavy-tailed distributions, where asymmetric noise models are warranted, or in cases where explicit target constraints can be used to refine predictions. Additionally, while we introduced a decay mechanism for handling the trade-off between global and local information, more sophisticated forgetting mechanisms might be warranted for capturing complex dependencies in non-stationary time series. Future work could explore these contexts, and extend to the multivariate forecasting regime.

Appendix 6.A
Model details

6.A.1
Fractional Differencing

Fractional differencing
[
102
]
is a technique used in time series analysis to transform non-stationary data into stationary data while preserving long-term dependencies in the series. Unlike traditional differencing methods, which involve subtracting the previous value (integer differencing), fractional differencing provides a more flexible way of modelling persistence in time series by introducing a fractional order parameter. Hence, fractional differencing generalizes the concept of differencing by allowing the differencing parameter

q
to take non-integer values.

The general form of fractional differencing is expressed through a binomial expansion:

\displaystyle\delta^{q}X_{t}=(1-B)^{q}X_{t}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{q\choose k}(-1)^{k}X_{t-k},

(6.31)

where
B
is the backshift operator,
q>0
is the fractional differencing order parameter, and
q\choose k
is the generalized binomial coefficient, i.e.

\displaystyle{q\choose k}=\frac{\Gamma(q+1)}{\Gamma(k+1)\Gamma(q-k+1)},

(6.32)

where
\Gamma
is the Gamma function. This formula creates a weighted sum of past values, where the weights decay gradually depending on

q
. Note that for integer values of
q
, the formula collapses to standard differencing.

In our case in equation (
6.15
) and (
6.18
), we apply fractional differencing to each channel in the lifted time series it is applied to, such that the
RFF
maps
\varphi^{(p)}
each have
D
channels, and each channel is convolved with an individual filter as in (
6.31
) depending on the channel-wise fractional differencing parameter. In practice, we limit the summation in (
6.31
) to a finite window
W\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, since the sequence decays to zero fast this does not lose much.

6.A.2
Algorithms

Additionally to the notation defined in Section
1.3.1
, we extend the possible array operations. Recall that for arrays
1
-based indexing is used. Elements outside the bounds of an array are treated as zeros as opposed to circular wrapping.
Let
A
and
B
be
k
-fold arrays with shape
(n_{1}\times\dots\times n_{k})
, and let
i_{j}\in[n_{j}]
for
j\in[k]
. We define the following channelwise array operations:

(i)

Let
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}
. The channelwise geometric scan along axis
j
:

\displaystyle A[\dots,:,\boxplus^{\bm{\lambda}},:,\dots][\dots,i_{j-1},i_{j},,i_{j+1}\dots]:=\sum_{\kappa=1}^{i_{j}}\lambda_{i_{k}}^{i_{j}-\kappa}A[\dots,i_{j-1},\kappa,i_{j+1},\dots].

(6.33)

(ii)

Let
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}
and
W\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. The channelwise fractional difference along axis
j
:

\displaystyle A[\dots,:,\boxminus^{\mathbf{q}}_{W},:,\dots][\dots,i_{j-1},i_{j},,i_{j+1}\dots]:=\sum_{\kappa=0}^{W-1}{q_{i_{k}}\choose\kappa}A[\dots,i_{j-1},i_{j}-\kappa,i_{j+1},\dots],

(6.34)

where
{q_{i_{k}}\choose\kappa}
is as in (
6.32
).

Algorithm 10

Computing the
RFSF
map.

1:

Input:
Time series
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, spectral measure
\Lambda
, truncation
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
RFF
dimension
D\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, frac. diff. orders
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, frac.diff. window
W\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

2:

Sample independent
RFF
frequencies
{}^{(1)},\dots,{}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Lambda^{D}
and phases
\mathbf{b}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{b}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{U}(0,2\pi)^{D}

3:

Initialize an array
U
with shape
[M,L,D]

4:

Compute
RFF
s per time step
U[m,l,:]\leftarrow\cos({{}^{(m)}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{l}+\mathbf{b}^{(m)})
for
m\in[M]
and
l\in[L]

5:

Compute fractional differences
U\leftarrow U[:,\boxminus_{W}^{\mathbf{q}},:]

6:

Accumulate into level-
1
features
P_{1}\leftarrow U[1,\boxplus,:]

7:

Initialize list
R\leftarrow[U[1,:,:]]

8:

for

m=2

to

M

do

9:

Update with next step
P^{\prime}\leftarrow P_{m-1}[+1,:]\odot U[m,:,:]

10:

Initialize new list
R^{\prime}\leftarrow[P^{\prime}]

11:

for

p=2
to
m

do

12:

Update with current step
Q\leftarrow\frac{1}{p}R[p-1]\odot U[m,:,:]

13:

Append to
R^{\prime}+=[Q]

14:

end

for

15:

Aggregate into level-
m
features
P_{m}\leftarrow R^{\prime}[\Sigma][\boxplus,:]

16:

Roll list
R\leftarrow R^{\prime}

17:

end

for

18:

Output:
Arrays of
RFSF
features per signature level and time step
P_{1},\dots,P_{M}
.

Algorithm 11

Computing the
RFDSF
map.

1:

Input:
Time series
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathsf{Seq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, spectral measure
\Lambda
, truncation
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
